The identity laws say that tacking the do-nothing loop onto either end of an arrow gives back the arrow. If the loop changed things, it wouldn't be the loop. Those six terms, objects, arrows, composition, identities, associativity and identity laws, are all of Eilenberg and Mac Lane's definition.

Composing relations is older than the definition, too. Who put a name on that move first? I went looking. Augustus De Morgan, writing on the logic of relations around 1860, took the ``is'' out of the old syllogism, put a general relation in its place, and composed two relations to get the conclusion. The example that gets quoted from that tradition, Peirce's a decade later, is ``$x$ is the lover of a servant of $y$,'' which is relational composition with better gossip. The paper, his fourth ``On the Syllogism,'' has more dates than most. He signed it at University College London on 12 November 1859, added an appendix dated 15 March 1860, and read it to the Cambridge Philosophical Society on 23 April 1860; it was printed in the Society's Transactions in a volume dated 1864, on pages 331 to 358, and Peter Heath's 1966 collection of his logical writings has it on pages 208 to 246. With that many dates to pick from, it's no surprise the sources I read give different years, which is a small reminder that history is where you start digging. Then in 1870 Charles Sanders Peirce, an American scientist and philosopher you met in chapter nine, carried George Boole's algebra of logic (the arithmetic of true and false, and Boole was a largely self-taught schoolmaster) over to relations, with composition as one of its basic operations, and he's usually counted as the real founder of the theory. So following one arrow after another is older than computers, older than categories, older than Turing's machine: it is a nineteenth-century logician's product of relations.

And where did they get the word? They didn't get it from mathematics. They borrowed category from the philosophers, from Aristotle and Kant, and functor (a map from one whole category to another) from the philosopher Rudolf Carnap, and then gave both words exact meanings none of those philosophers had in mind. So Aristotle walks back into this chapter without being asked: the man who put the form inside the bronze lent his word to the field that keeps track of forms by their arrows. That doesn't change the laws. It's a reminder that a word can start in philosophy and arrive in mathematics with a precise meaning bolted on, which is the thing I've been trying to do with established since the start of the book.

What a thing is is how it maps. For now the residue is the emphasis I needed: structure lives in the maps. Nothing later in this book needs more category theory than that sentence, and the chapter on a box over any machine, where a record is pinned down by the maps between a machine and the base it extends, is where the sentence does its work.

\subsection{Structure is the part that compresses}

Play this one with me. I write ``th'' and you guess the next letter. You said ``e,'' and you were right. Now ``the q'': you said ``u.'' Congratulations, you just measured the structure of English. In 1948 Claude Shannon measured it properly, in a paper that founded information theory, and found that about half of written English is ``determined by the structure of the language.'' It's the reason a zip file of a long letter is so much smaller than the letter. Be careful what that says. It does not say that every other letter is fixed by the ones before it; you just guessed ``e'' after ``th'' and you could have been wrong (think ``than''). It says that, on average and over long stretches, about half of what's written could be rebuilt from the rest, so that the text is roughly twice as long as the choices in it. And he said why that matters in one line that I'd frame if I framed things: the structure ``allows one to make a saving in time'' (he added ``or channel capacity''). The more you can predict, the less you have to read.

Thirty years later Jorma Rissanen, a Finnish information theorist at IBM's research lab in San Jose, turned that saving into a price. His idea is called the minimum description length principle, and it says that the best model of some data is the one that makes the total description shortest, where the total is the length of the model plus the length of the data once the model has explained what it can. A model is paid for in bits, and it's worth stating only if it saves more than it costs. Peter Grünwald, a statistician who leads a machine learning group at CWI, the Dutch national institute for mathematics and computer science in Amsterdam, in a tutorial you can find online, adds the footnote that keeps this honest: the price of a model depends on the code you chose to write it in, so the price itself needs a principle behind it, and that principle is something you have to argue for, not something you're handed.

That's a lot of ``length of'' to swallow in one paragraph, so do one with me, with numbers small enough to check on the back of an envelope. Every number in it comes from a code I'm about to make up on the spot, and I'll say every rule of the code out loud, because Grünwald's footnote is the whole point: change the rules and every number moves.

The data is a row of thirty-two digits, each a 0 or a 1, and you and I agree in advance that the row is thirty-two long, so nobody has to spend anything saying so:

\begin{center}\texttt{0101010101010101 0101010101010101}\end{center}

(The space is only there to rest your eyes.) Every description in the code starts with one digit that says which kind of description follows: 0 means ``here's the row, written out,'' 1 means ``here's a pattern, then the exceptions to it.'' Each digit is one yes-or-no answer, one bit, which is what Shannon's unit is. Written out, the row costs that first digit plus the thirty-two digits themselves, thirty- three bits, and there's no model in it at all. A pattern description goes like this. First come five digits giving the block length minus one: 00000 means one digit, and 11111 means thirty-two. That covers every allowed length. Then comes the block itself. The rule is ``repeat the block, then stop at exactly thirty-two digits, cutting off the last repetition if necessary.'' Then come five digits for how many places the row disagrees with the pattern (up to thirty-one, which is plenty here), and five more digits for each of those places, numbered zero through thirty-one and listed in increasing order without repeats. At every listed place, flip the predicted digit.

Do it for the row above. The block is 01, two digits long. So the model costs 1 (which kind) plus 5 (the block's length) plus 2 (the block), which makes eight bits. Then comes what's left to say about the data once the model has said its piece: the row disagrees with ``repeat 01'' nowhere, so that's five digits saying ``zero exceptions'' and nothing else, five bits. The total is thirteen, against thirty-three for writing it out. The pattern pays for itself, with twenty bits to spare, and Rissanen's sentence has just happened on paper: a model is worth stating when it saves more than it costs.

Now spoil the row. Flip two of its digits anywhere you like. The model doesn't change, eight bits, but now the leftover says ``two exceptions, here and here'': five plus five plus five, fifteen, for a total of twenty-three. It's still cheaper than thirty-three; the pattern still pays, it just pays less, because the data has more to say that the model can't. Flip four, and the total is $8 + 5 + 4 \times 5 = 33$, a dead tie, and a pattern that saves nothing isn't worth stating. Flip five and it's thirty-eight, and now writing the row out is cheaper than explaining it, and this particular pattern has lost to writing the row out. Another pattern might still win. Flip places 5, 11, 17, 23 and 29 of the original row: now the block 010100, repeated and cut off at thirty-two digits, fits exactly. Its description costs $1+5+6+5=17$ bits. Five changes defeated ``repeat 01'' and produced a better description of their own. The comparison is between descriptions in the stated code, not a count of spoiled digits alone.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
Message & Original row & Two flips & Five specified flips\\ \hline
Write all 32 digits & 33 & 33 & 33\\
Repeat 01, list exceptions & 13 & 23 & 38\\
Repeat 010100, no exceptions & does not fit & depends & 17\\ \hline
\end{tabular}

\footnotesize For the last column, flip positions 5, 11, 17, 23 and 29. That defeats one
short description and supplies another. Pattern descriptions include the type bit,
length field and exception-count field. The raw row uses one type bit followed by
its thirty-two digits.
\end{center}

So ``is there structure here?'' has turned into a question with a number on each side, and a break-even point you can find yourself: four spoiled digits, in this code.

And now change the code, because I promised you'd see the numbers move. Say I'd used seven digits for each exception's place (wasteful, but nobody stopped me). Then three spoiled digits cost $8 + 5 + 3 \times 7 = 34$, one more than writing the row out, where the five-digit code charged twenty-eight and the pattern paid. The pattern stopped paying one spoiled digit sooner. The row is the same, the pattern is the same, the verdict is different, and the only thing that changed was a convention I chose. That's Grünwald's footnote, and it's why I won't let you take any of those numbers anywhere else. They're the price of a description in one made-up code, a length of a written-down message, and nothing more. They are not the charge I opened this book with. That charge, as page one states it, is on one step a machine takes. How long anybody's description is doesn't enter it, and if the two ever turn out to be related, that has to be shown. Both of them being counted in whole numbers doesn't get to stand in for the showing.

Now push, because this is where ``structure means information'' gets born, and it's wrong, and it's an easy thing to believe. Flip a fair coin two hundred times and write down the string. Usually there is no substantially shorter way to say that string than to say the whole thing. You can watch it happen in the little code above, cut to thirty-two digits: a coin-flip row disagrees with ``repeat 01'' in about sixteen places, which would cost eighty-odd bits to list, and even the one block guaranteed to fit with no exceptions, the whole row used as its own block, costs $1 + 5 + 32 + 5 = 43$. The written-out row at thirty-three beats those two descriptions. Another block could do better; a lucky run of coins can make a pattern, so check it. The general reason long random rows usually resist substantial compression is a count: put each short description beside the one row it prints, and there are too few short descriptions to stand beside more than a small fraction of all the long rows. Nikolai Vereshchagin, a mathematician at Moscow State University, and Paul Vitányi, a computer scientist at CWI in Amsterdam, in a 2004 paper on exactly this question, put it without mercy: every bit of a string like that is ``meaningless information, random noise.'' Such a string carries maximum information and zero structure. So in this sense structure is the part that compresses, the part where the ``th'' tells you about the ``e,'' and most information and most structure are different things. Hold that one tightly, because if you built something that charged for information, you'd be charging for the wrong thing, and for a while I wasn't sure which one I'd built.

And now notice that the word just changed meaning on you, and on me. I'm pointing at it so it doesn't slide past. Shannon and Rissanen and Vereshchagin mean regularity: a pattern that lets you say something shorter. Tarski, a few pages back, and Paul Halmos, a Hungarian-American mathematician whose short textbook on sets is coming up, mean a set with named relations on it, and those two are not the same thing. You can run the test yourself: number a thousand objects, flip a coin for each, heads goes in group one, tails in group two. The result is a perfectly good sorting in the set theorist's sense, every object in exactly one group. And the list of who went where is a thousand coin flips, which is to say it compresses about as well as a thousand coin flips: typically very little under a code fixed before the tosses. Feed it to a code built like the little one, with the fields widened to fit a thousand, and you get the coin-flip row again, only longer: typically most of the assignment still has to be written down. A lucky pattern can save some of it; the coin tosses alone do not guarantee there is none. By the set theorist's rule, the sorting is the structure, and it's every bit as much a sorting as ``evens in one group, odds in the other,'' which compresses to almost nothing. The two are the same kind of object with wildly different description lengths. So the two meanings don't even rank things the same way: one asks ``is there a sorting?'' and the answer is yes for both; the other asks ``does a short rule say who went where?'' and the answer is yes for evens and odds and typically no for a substantial saving on the coins. The coin sorting has structure in the set theorist's sense without necessarily offering a shorter description. I'll keep both meanings, and I'll try to say which one I'm using, and when I slip you'll know what to look for.

\subsection{No place for a grouping}

You built Turing's machine on a table in chapter one: a tape, a head, a state, and a card of rules. Chapter one also kept three claims apart: what's proved about it, the Church-Turing thesis, and the claim about physics. Every laptop and phone and server computes nothing a Turing machine can't. What differs between them is speed, space, energy and cost. Hold on to that last word. Cost is where this book lives.

Now do the thing I did, and do it honestly. Look through those four parts for a place that says ``these squares belong together.'' There isn't one, and that's not me failing to look. Everything the machine is at any moment is which state it's in, where the head is, and what's on the tape, and the table says what happens next, and that's the full list. So any grouping has to live in the states and the table, as a rule, or on the tape, as symbols, which is to say as bytes like any other bytes. The machine can hold a grouping. It just has no place where holding one is different from holding anything else.

In 1972 David Parnas, a software engineer, wrote a short paper on how to cut a program into modules. He took one small program and cut it two ways. Both worked. But one was easy to change later and the other wasn't, and the easy one could be ``much less efficient'' if talking between modules cost anything. His point was that a module is ``a responsibility assignment,'' a decision about who answers for what, and how you cut decides what every later change costs. If you've ever maintained someone else's code, you've paid for their cut the way Parnas said you would, probably at two in the morning. What survives the push is that a division is a commitment: it changes what the work costs, and it is no caption, no label stuck on afterwards. I'd like to say the thing I built understood that from the beginning. It didn't. I did, eventually.

\subsection{Laying them all down}

Now lay every one of those on top of the others and see what shows through. I see seven things. They're my overlay, the places where I see the sources agreeing. The sources never proved it together; some of them never met, and some of them disagree (Eilenberg and Mac Lane put maps and their composition at the centre; Shannon's structure and Tarski's are two different words, as I just showed you). You've pushed on each source yourself by now, so you're in a position to tell me if I'm seeing things.

A list of seven slogans would be easy to nod at and impossible to check, so I'm going to carry one thing through all seven and let you watch each one add to the one before it. The thing is the square you drew for the Pythagoreans, side, diagonal and all. Draw a few more on the same sheet: one small, one big, the small one again but tilted, and one stretched out into a long rectangle. You'll want a pile.

Structure is about relations among things, and the things themselves aren't it. You see that in the diagonal, the bronze, Leibniz's room, Bourbaki's unspecified elements, Tarski's named relations and the arrows. On your sheet, what makes a square a square was never the ink. It's that the four sides match and the corners are square, and that the diagonal stands to the side the way it does whether or not any number names it. Every one of those is a relation among parts. But on its own this first thing is too loose to use, because a sheet of paper is drowning in relations: this square is left of that one, this one is nearer the coffee ring. Which relations count?

Structure is what stays the same under a chosen set of changes. You see that in Euclid's triangles laid together, Klein's allowed moves, Noether's symmetry, and Frege's direction that every parallel line shares. This second thing gives the answer the first thing was missing: the relations that count are the ones that survive the moves you've decided to allow. Slide the small square across the sheet, turn it, and the diagonal still stands to the side exactly as before; ``left of the coffee ring'' doesn't make it across. The second thing is a filter on the first.

The same structure can be carried by different stuff: the statue in marble, or two sets that answer the same vocabulary the same way. The third thing is the second again with one more change allowed, which is to swap the material. Cut the square out of cardboard, or lay it out in string on the table, and if the relations survive, it's the same square in every way you agreed to care about. Aristotle's bronze is one more thing you agreed not to look at, and the wooden sandwich is what happens when the material was never on that list.

The first act of structure is a sameness that sorts a pile you were already given. It's there in Euclid's first common notion, Frege's direction, Tarski's one question, and Russell's letter, with Zermelo's repair that you must start from a pile. At this point the overlay stopped being a list for me and turned into a shape, so slow down here with me. Look at your pile of drawings and say: two of them count as the same when an allowed move, for now a slide or a turn, carries one exactly onto the other. Watch what happens to the pile. It falls into groups. The small square and its tilted twin go together; the big one sits on its own; the rectangle sits on its own. And it falls cleanly, nothing half in one group and half in another, for a reason you can check. Klein's allowed moves always come with three guarantees: doing nothing counts as a move, every move can be undone, and two moves in a row is again a move. Those three are, in different clothes, ``everything is the same as itself,'' ``if this matches that, then that matches this,'' and ``if this matches that and that matches a third, then this matches the third.'' Put the last two together and you get Euclid's first common notion, ``things which are equal to the same thing are also equal to one another.'' So the second thing, sameness under chosen moves, is a sorting of the pile the moment you ask which drawings it calls the same. Frege did it with lines and parallels. Tarski's one question does it in the crudest way there is, by cutting the pile in two. And the pile has to be there first, because a sameness has to be a sameness among something; that's Russell's letter, and Zermelo's repair.

Adding structure rules possibilities out. Every one of Euclid's postulates rules one out. Half of English is already decided. A conserved quantity limits where a system can go. On your sheet: once the small square and its tilted twin are in one group, you've given up the right to answer any question the moves respect differently for the two of them. Tell me the side of one and the diagonal of the other isn't an open question any more. A sorting rules out a whole bundle of possibilities at once.

Held structure gets used without re-deriving it. You see that in Shannon's saving in time, Rissanen's compression, Parnas's one-module-at-a-time, and Leibniz's analysis of situation (reasoning about where things are straight from their relations, with no coordinates laid down first). The fifth thing pays off here: the possibilities you ruled out are ones you never have to check again. Know which group a drawing is in and you know everything the group shares without getting the ruler out, the same way the row of 01 never needed writing out. There's one catch, and I'll need a whole section for it: it only pays if you kept the sorting somewhere you can read it back.

And structure is always relative to a chosen frame, whether that frame is Klein's group, the vocabulary, the code you compress against, or Parnas's criteria. Nobody gets to say ``the'' structure of a thing without saying from where, and I do it constantly, so watch me for it. On your sheet: allow stretching as one of the moves and the rectangle walks over and joins the squares; allow only slides and turns and it doesn't. Same pile, same ink, different groups, because I chose different moves. The groups were never in the paper. They were in the paper and the choice, together.

I think the seven hang together this way. Relations are what structure is made of. The chosen moves pick which relations count, and a change of material is just one more move. Asking which things the moves call the same sorts the pile. The sorting rules possibilities out, and what's ruled out is what you get to skip later. And because the moves were chosen, all of it is relative. Read that way, the fourth thing sits in the middle: it's where the first three turn into something you can write down, and where the last three get their start.

That's my reason for starting the next subsection with a sorting and a door across it, and I want to be exact about what kind of reason it is, because it's mine. None of these people said ``start with a partition.'' Klein would tell you to start with the moves, Eilenberg and Mac Lane with the arrows, Shannon with the probabilities. What I can actually point at is narrower. In the framings about when two things are the same shape or the same kind (Euclid laying one triangle on another, Klein's moves, Frege's directions, Tarski's question) there's a moment where somebody decides what counts as the same, and that decision, written down, is a sorting of a pile. In Shannon, Rissanen and Noether I can't find that moment as cleanly, and I'm not going to bend them until I do. So the overlay doesn't prove that a sorting comes first. It shows me where the framings I trust most keep putting their first foot down, and I'm putting mine there too, with one thing added that none of them needed and the toll does: a direction across the sorting.

I looked for a framing that denies any of the seven and didn't find one on my own. There are two, and they're worth pushing on, because each one denies a different piece.

The seventh, that structure is always relative to a chosen frame, is denied outright by David Lewis and Theodore Sider. Hilary Putnam had argued that if every way of carving things up is as good as every other, a consistent theory can always be made true just by picking what its words refer to. Lewis answered him in 1983, in a paper called ``New Work for a Theory of Universals,'' with an answer he credits to G. H. Merrill: some properties are natural and some aren't, some cuts carve nature at its joints and some are lines somebody drew, and the natural ones are the ones words can pick out. Sider built a whole book on it in 2011, Writing the Book of the World, and he uses this chapter's word: ``The world has a distinguished structure, a privileged description,'' and ``structure is a worldly, not conceptual or linguistic, matter.'' On their view there's a frame nobody chose.

Push on it from this book's side. Grant them their joints. A machine still doesn't get handed them. Chapter fourteen shows that the event a box turns up on is a choice the rules don't make for you, and the toll from chapters two and three charges the same whether that event is a joint of nature or a line I drew. So whether the world has a structure of its own is a question this book can't settle, and what a machine keeps and pays for stays relative to the frame it was built with. That's a narrower seventh than the one I wrote above, and it's the one I can stand behind.

The fourth, that the first act is a sameness that sorts a pile you were already given, is denied by Michael Makkai and by the people behind univalent foundations. Makkai, in a 1998 paper, ``Towards a Categorical Foundation of Mathematics,'' doesn't give up sets with members. What he gives up is one sameness that holds across everything at once: ``there is no global equality present for all things simultaneously.'' Any property worth stating about the objects of a kind has to come out the same for two objects that match exactly in structure. The univalent foundations book, written in 2013 during a year at the Institute for Advanced Study organised by Steve Awodey, Thierry Coquand and Vladimir Voevodsky, makes that an axiom: ``identity is equivalent to equivalence.'' Things that match exactly in structure count as the same, for everything the language is allowed to say. And there can be more than one way for two things to match, the way a square matches itself four ways by turning, so sameness there carries which match you meant. That's Benacerraf's puzzle from earlier in this chapter, about which sets the number three really is, shut down at the door: nobody gets to ask a question the structure doesn't answer.

Push on that one and it gives less than the first, because a machine that steps still has a pile: the states it can reach. At any tick its box is down or up, so those states are already cut in two, whatever the foundations underneath say about where the pile came from. And the cut is legal on their own terms. What Makkai gives up is a sameness across every kind of thing at once; inside one kind he keeps it, and a set, in his words, is ``a relatively orderly part of the world in which an equality predicate reigns.'' A machine's states are one kind, and a yes-or-no question put to each of them never leaves it. Makkai and the univalent people change what you're allowed to say about the pile. They don't take away the cut.

\subsection{The smallest piece}

Now the sentence I wanted to say at the top and had no right to until here, and even here I have to split it in two, because half of it is a textbook fact and the other half is me. The piece of structure I'm going to start from is a cut: a pile sorted in two, with a door between the halves that swings one way. A partition is the cut with the door taken off, and the partition comes with a textbook fact, so I'll do that half first.

The textbook half: a partition and an equivalence relation are the same object. That's Paul Halmos, Naive Set Theory, 1960, section seven, and you could read that section before dinner. I'll say it in words first. Take any pile of things. An equivalence relation on it is a way of saying ``these two count as the same'' that behaves the way sameness should: everything is the same as itself; if a is the same as b then b is the same as a; and if a is the same as b and b the same as c, then a is the same as c. Every such relation sorts the pile into groups, the things that count as the same go together, and every sorting into groups hands you back a relation, ``same group.'' Do one and then the other and you land where you started, in both directions. So a partition and an equivalence relation are one object seen from two sides, and that's the fourth thing on the overlay, the sorting of a given pile, now with a name and a page number.

You can check that with the buttons from chapter four, or anything else in a pile. Say two things count as the same when they're in the same group. Is everything the same as itself? Yes, it's in its own group. If a is with b, is b with a? Yes. If a is with b and b is with c, is a with c? They're all in b's group, and b is only in one group, so yes. Now go the other way: start with any ``counts as the same'' that follows those three rules, and gather everything the same as a into one group. Could two of those groups overlap without being the same group? If something is the same as a and the same as b, then by the rules a is the same as b, so their groups are one group. So the groups never half-overlap: you get a clean sorting. And doing one and then the other lands you back where you started. That's the whole proof, and it's textbook mathematics. I'm claiming nothing new in it.

And the smallest structure that sorts, by my ruler, the one question, is the same object a third time. Put a yes-or-no question to every thing in the pile. The ones that say yes are one group and the ones that say no are the other. Going the other way, a sorting into two groups becomes a question the moment you point at one group and call it ``yes.'' The only thing a question has that the sorting doesn't is which group got the label. So the set theorist's first step and the logician's first step are the same step, and nobody has to pick a side, which is a relief, because it's an argument you can lose with yourself for a long time.

Now put the door back. In chapter five you met ``at or below,'' with its two rules: every place is at or below itself, and at or below carries across a third place. It doesn't have to go both ways. Take the places that are each at or below the other, and put them in one group. That ``each at or below the other'' follows all three of sameness's rules, so it's a clean sorting, the buckets. And between buckets, ``at or below'' only goes one way: if two buckets were each at or below the other, they'd be one bucket. So an order is exactly a sorting with doors between the groups, and every door swings one way. Take the doors off and a sorting is what's left.

A yes-or-no question is the smallest case of that: two buckets, no and yes, and one door from no to yes. The thing settling on one side of a line, from the first page of this book, is a move through that one door. What chapters one through three found keeping takes is what it costs to go through it and not come back.

So much for the textbook half. The other half is mine, so hold it looser, because Halmos says nothing about ``smallest'' or ``first,'' and I'd be lying if I let his page number carry my sentence. In what I build, the order goes: a pile, then a split of the pile with a door across it, then more splits at once, which is an order. The groups come first because a question needs something to be about, and a door needs two sides to stand between. That's an order for my construction. It isn't a law that every account of structure has to start by sorting. You could start with arrows between the things themselves and no groups at all, and people do, and nothing I've shown you rules that out. So try that road, measured with the same kind of ruler: fewest dots first, then fewest arrows between different dots. One dot can't tell anything apart. Two dots with no arrow between them is a plain split, with no door. So the smallest picture with a direction in it has two dots, call them NO and YES, a do-nothing loop on each so a thing is allowed to stay put, and one arrow from NO to YES with nothing coming back. Category theorists call it the walking arrow. Now lay any machine onto it: put every state on one of the two dots, so that every move of the machine follows an arrow in the picture. The dots sort the states into two buckets. The loops let a move stay where it is. The one arrow lets a move cross from NO to YES. And there's no arrow back, so no move goes from YES to NO. That's a split with a one-way door, exactly, and every split with a one-way door lays onto the picture that way.

So the arrow road doesn't come out smaller. It comes out at the same place. The honest sentence is: a split with a door across it is where I chose to start, and it's where both rulers hit bottom. It's the smallest by the ruler I stated. If you have a smaller start, by a ruler you can state, I want to see it. It would change this book.

\subsection{Buckets}

So I'll do the symmetric half once, for real, because it's the half you can do with your hands. Take everything in front of you and sort it into buckets, with nothing in two buckets and nothing left on the floor, and that's the whole of the definition. It's the thing you do with a kindergartner and a pile of blocks, and I am aware that I've just marched you past Pythagoras, Plato, Euclid, Frege and Noether to arrive at a pile of blocks. Keep the blocks anyway. They're about to cost you something.

Take ten blocks, and put the red ones in one bucket, the square ones in another and everything else in a third. Now a red square turns up, as they do, and you've got your first crisis: it belongs in two buckets and the rule says one. So this isn't a partition yet, and you have to decide which bucket wins. That decision is the whole thing, because a partition is a pile of small rulings like that one, and once you've made them all, the pile is holding a fact it wasn't holding a minute ago.

The next part isn't kindergarten. Why would that fact cost anything? Watch what it does to one question. Before the buckets, ``are these two in the same group?'' means working it out: go find the first one, go find the second one, apply the rule to each, and compare. After the buckets, it's a glance: read the first one's bucket, read the second one's bucket, and you're done. With each block's address already in hand, and a table that retrieves a label in one operation, that is two lookups whether the pile has ten blocks or a billion. Those are access and cost assumptions. A pile with no addresses, or an index that must be searched, has not been given that same two-lookup rule. What you're looking at is Shannon's saving in time and Parnas's one-module-at-a-time, the sixth thing on the overlay, showing up in a kindergarten.

Look at what makes that glance possible: a table, with a written-down line for each block saying which bucket it went in. The partition is the fact; the table is one way of holding the fact so it can be read back fast, and you had to build the table, and you have to store it, and it has to be the kind of thing you can read in one look (a bucket number, say; a list of every block in the bucket would have you searching through it again). Anybody who's used a database has met this table; it's what an index is, and an index is a thing you build, store, and keep up to date, and every part of that takes something. Take the table away and keep the partition and you're back to working it out. And sometimes there was never a search to save: if the rule is ``evens here, odds there,'' you can answer ``same group?'' by looking at the last digit of each, no buckets needed. What survives is that holding a sorting in a form you can read back is worth something, and building that form cost something, and those are already two different events. Building the table, storing it, checking that it's right, and deciding to rely on it are four different things. The first cost question I want to put to that structure lives in the gap between the third and the fourth. Nothing in the kindergarten has shown a charge in that gap. It has separated the acts well enough to ask.

So who, in everything I read, says what has to be paid? I found three answers that come close, and I'll be exact about how close, because this is the edge of what I know. Rolf Landauer, in 1961, and Charles Bennett after him, say that any step which takes two possible situations and makes them one, erasing a bit or merging two paths a computation could have taken, has a heat floor when it removes uncertainty under their thermodynamic assumptions. That is a physical claim about how the device and its surroundings are prepared, Landauer's principle, and in this book it is always a premise, never something I prove. It's the place, of all I've found, where a fact about information turns into a fact about heat, though at room temperature the amount for one bit is far too small to show up on anybody's electricity bill. In 2012 a lab in Lyon erased one bit, held in a single tiny bead, and measured the average heat it gave off coming down toward Landauer's floor as the erasing was slowed, and other labs have found the same since. Not everyone accepts the principle in general, and I'll come back to the people who don't. Is sending blocks to buckets a step of that kind? Push on it, because the answer depends on what you keep. If you keep each block and write its bucket next to it on a spot you knew was blank, you've merged nothing; you can read the block right back off the pair. You have spent something, though: the blank spot the label now sits on, which the machine hadn't used before, and any heat from the writing depends on its implementation. Bennett's whole point in his 1973 paper was that a computation which keeps its inputs, and keeps enough of its working to retrace its steps, can be run without any merging step at all, and it pays for that by taking fresh states. If instead you throw the block away and keep only the bucket label, then two different blocks can leave the same label behind, and that is a many-to-one step, the kind Landauer charges for. Both are things people call ``sorting into buckets.'' Only the second loses a distinction. So the bucket by itself doesn't hand you Landauer's heat. The forgetting does, when there is forgetting, and keeping instead is paid for with fresh states. Whether a given machine forgets is a question about that machine. It has to be answered on the page, and I won't wave at it here. Rissanen says a model is paid for in the bits it takes to state, and Grünwald says the price depends on the code. Parnas says a bad cut is paid for later, at two in the morning. Peirce, in 1879, priced research itself: in his words, ``the relations between the utility and the cost of diminishing the probable error of our knowledge.'' Abraham Wald's sequential tests, from 1945, are built to need fewer samples, on average, than a test of fixed size with the same chance of being wrong. Those price the looking and the risk in the answer. Neither puts a price on the step that locks the answer in. And that's where it stops. Rovelli, back in chapter six, prices a choice that sticks, and his choice is one nobody could predict, which that section showed is a different act. I couldn't find anyone who says that the moment you vouch for a grouping, the moment you stand behind ``these belong together,'' has a price of its own. Just arranging them that way is a different act. That could mean nobody says it. It could mean I didn't find the person who does. I honestly can't tell you which.

So that's where I am at the end of one word. A grouping, the act of making it, the information kept about it, and a commitment to rely on it are different things. The blocks let you separate them with your hands. Now take them down to the smallest case there is, a bucket with two sides, one block, and a ruling that sticks, which is a thing settling on one side of a line and staying there, the case the first chapters started from and showed what keeping it takes. The construction I will study keeps selected distinctions in a state. Some are there from the start. Some are newly recorded. Those are different cases: a box that already says YES can sit there forever under a do-nothing rule, with neither a merge nor fresh states. The count in chapters one through three starts with NO and follows a new YES that stays under a fixed total rule. That is the case to which its merge-or-fresh conclusion applies. A grouping by itself does not supply that run or its price.

%===========================================================================
\section{The counter}
\label{sec:counter}
%===========================================================================

Back in chapter four you kept a tally beside the two boxes, the way you'd keep score beside a board game. The tally lived in your hand. The machine never saw it. That's fine for a game on a table. For anything that's going to run on its own, with nobody keeping score, the total has to live somewhere the machine can reach, which means the machine can, in principle, write it.

So this chapter is about what a running total inside a machine has to be before you're allowed to call it a receipt. Here's the answer first, because it's short, and then the chapter is about why each word is there: a receipt is a total that lives in the state, that only climbs, that equals the sum of what was charged, and that nothing else could have been.

\subsection{A stick, split down the middle}

The oldest receipt I could find that you can still hold is a piece of wood. From about 1100, under Henry the First, until 1826, the English treasury recorded what it was owed on tally sticks: a strip of hazel, notched across with cuts of set widths, then split lengthwise so each half carried the same notches. The treasury's own handbook from around 1180, the Dialogue of the Exchequer, gives the widths: a thumb's width for a hundred pounds, a swollen barleycorn for a pound, a bare cut for a penny. The payer kept one half, called the stock, which is one story for where the word stockholder comes from. The treasury kept the other. When the time came to settle, you laid the two halves together, and only the two original halves fit, because wood splits along a grain nobody can copy. Add a notch to your half and the other half shows a gap where the partner should be. Try to take a notch away and you find that wood can't be uncut.

When they finally stopped using them, the clerks burned centuries of old tallies in the furnaces under the House of Lords, overheated the flue, and burned down the Palace of Westminster. I mention that because it's the best fact I picked up for this book, and because it tells you how many of them there were.

Do it yourself. Draw a border around a strip of card, notch it once for every coin you hand a friend, then split it lengthwise through the notches and keep half each. Don't trim the edge or swap in a fresh strip. Now add a notch to one half and put them back together. You'll see the mismatch straight away. And you can't put a cut back: a patch is just one more thing to look at. The total is the notches, one per coin, and the count only climbs. Both halves started with the same count, so if they disagree now, somebody changed one. The mismatch won't tell you who. What the stick gives you is a comparison you can make, not an object nobody can forge.

Economists read the same stick another way, and they're not wrong either. Alfred Mitchell-Innes made the treasury tally his star witness in 1913 that money is credit: the crown paid the people it owed by ``raising a tally'' on some branch of its income, a wooden promise to pay. A receipt to me, money to him, and it's the same piece of wood. Narayana Kocherlakota proved a version of his point in 1998: in a wide range of economies where nobody can be held to a promise, anything money lets people do, a memory of everyone's past dealings lets them do too. His title says it in three words: money is memory.

\subsection{Why it has to climb}

Page one already showed why there are no refunds: if a later move could pay you back, the move that settles charges you one, the next move hands it back, and the whole run comes out free. A total that can come back down is an accountant you can lean on to revise the books. ``This run cost at least one'' would be true of a run that ended having cost nothing. So the count only climbing isn't decoration. It's what makes ``at least'' mean something.

The same rule turns up in two places that have nothing to do with each other, pointing in opposite directions, and that's a big part of why I trust it.

In 1978 Leslie Lamport asked how a set of computers passing each other messages, with no shared clock, could ever agree on what happened before what. His answer is a counter on each machine with two rules: add one before you do anything, and when a message arrives carrying the sender's count, jump your own counter above it, never below your own. Those counters, he showed, respect cause and effect: if this caused that, this has the smaller number. And they do it, in his words, ``with no actual timing mechanism.'' They're clocks only in name.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[thiele]
\node[pipe,text width=3.3cm,minimum width=0pt] (a) at (0,0) {Alice sends\\stamp 2};
\node[pipe,text width=3.3cm,minimum width=0pt] (b) at (5.8,0) {Bob receives\\stamp 3};
\node[pipe,text width=3.3cm,minimum width=0pt] (c) at (5.8,-2.8) {Bob replies\\stamp 4};
\node[pipe,text width=3.3cm,minimum width=0pt] (d) at (0,-2.8) {Alice receives\\stamp 5};
\draw[pipearrow] (a) -- node[above=6pt]{message} (b);
\draw[pipearrow] (b) -- node[left=8pt]{next local act} (c);
\draw[pipearrow] (c) -- node[below=6pt]{reply} (d);
\node[pipe,text width=9.1cm,minimum width=0pt] at (2.9,-5.1)
  {Elsewhere, Carol numbers an unrelated act 1.\\A smaller stamp alone does not prove a causal connection.};
\end{tikzpicture}

\small Start Bob at zero and Alice just after her local act 1. On receipt,
each raises the local count above both its old value and the incoming stamp.
\end{center}

Try it with three people passing notes and you'll see it work in two minutes. Then let one of them reset their counter to zero after reading a note, and watch a reply come out numbered before the note it replies to. A counter that goes down can't be leaned on for order. The other direction doesn't hold, though: two people can number two unrelated acts 1 and 2, and the numbers alone don't say the first caused the second.

Where did Lamport get it? In the notes he keeps on his own papers, he says the timestamps came from an earlier note by Paul Johnson and Bob Thomas on keeping copies of a database in step, whose method wasn't quite right: it let things happen in an order that broke cause and effect. What let him see the fix, he says, was special relativity, which teaches that the one order of events everybody agrees on is the order of what could have caused what. And he passes on a line from Jim Gray, the database man whose wedding you met in chapter eight: Gray had heard two opinions of the paper, that it was trivial and that it was brilliant. Lamport says he can't argue with the first and isn't inclined to argue with the second.

Now the other direction. In 1949 Alan Turing, and in 1967 Robert Floyd, each asked how you prove a program stops. Both times the answer was to find a number that goes down on every step and can't go down forever, like a whole number that can't go below zero. If every step lowers it, the steps can't go on forever. Up-only gives you order, and down-only gives you stopping. They're the same shape, seen from the two ends: a number that only goes one way is something you can build on.

And there's a real case where a counter was allowed to go down and got gamed exactly there. Ethereum, one of the largest blockchains, charges ``gas'' for every step of a program, and for years it gave gas back when a program freed up storage. Someone noticed you could fill storage when gas was cheap, hold it, and cash the refunds when gas was dear, and a token was built to do exactly that. The real work in a block could reach nearly twice the block's stated limit. In 2021 a change called EIP-3529 cut the refunds sharply and capped them at one fifth of the gas used. The written reasons are the token and those overfull blocks. I read both as the same lesson: a counter that goes down was being leaned on at the place where it went down. Gas comes back in a later chapter, as the one real system of five that charges at the moment of the write.

\subsection{The total is the charges and nothing else}

The next property is the plainest. The counter after a step is the counter before, plus what that step was charged. Add up a whole run, and all the middle values cancel out, the way a sum of differences collapses to last minus first. The total is the sum of the charges.

Why say it? Because it's the property that fails quietly. Say a machine charges one for raising its record, and somewhere in its error handling forgets to add the charge when the step fails halfway through. Or it charges two on one path and one on another for the same move. Or it has one step that writes the counter directly. Then the counter is mostly the sum of the charges, and ``mostly'' is the whole gap between a receipt and a note that says ``trust me, about this much.'' The rule is that there's no ``mostly'': every step, every outcome, every error, adds exactly its charge, and a step that fails is charged the same as one that succeeds, because the charge is on the attempt. For a machine built that way, the rule can't fail. What can fail is the claim that a real machine is built that way, and checking that means going through every move it has, the failure branches included, one at a time. It's dull, and it's the whole job.

One thing it doesn't say is that every unit bought something. The total is exact about what was spent. What the spending was worth is a later chapter's question.

\subsection{Only one honest count}

The next property surprised me by being provable at all. Fix the price list: this move costs one, that one costs zero. Now say somebody else keeps their own books: their own total for every state, starting at zero, and going up on every step by exactly what the price list says. Could their books disagree with the machine's counter, on some state you can actually get to?

No, and the proof is the tally stick. Both totals start at zero. On every step, both go up by the same amount. So on every state you can reach from the start, they're equal. Agreement at the start survives the first move, then the second, then every move after it. Given the price list, the count is forced. It's the arithmetic, not a second opinion.

It only says that about states you can reach. On a state nothing reaches, it says nothing, which is the right thing for a receipt to say.

And one thing follows that's easy to miss. If the counter is part of the state, then a run that comes back to a state it's already been in has the same counter both times, so the moves on that loop cost nothing in total. A machine that keeps its counter inside its state can only go round a loop that costs nothing. Every paid move lands it on a state it's never been in, because the counter is higher there than anywhere before. So a counter that only climbs is itself paid for in fresh paper. On a finite machine, that means no move can be priced above zero at all: play any move over and over, the states come back around, and a loop costs nothing. A machine that keeps its own receipt, with even one paid move, needs endlessly many states. That's chapter two again, coming up from underneath.

Here's a puzzle that shows where the ``only one'' stops. Take the one-switch machine whose set costs one and whose reset costs zero. Set it once and it's up. Reset it and set it again, and it's up again, the same state. Now picture a second machine that keeps the whole list of moves. To it, ``set'' and ``reset, set'' are different. So you can always walk a list of moves into the second machine, one way and one way only. But you can't always walk the first machine's states into it, because two lists that land the first machine on the same state can land the second on different ones. You can do it exactly when the second machine never tells apart two lists the first one treats the same. Joseph Goguen put that as a general rule in 1972, in a paper whose title says it outright, ``Realization is universal,'' and Thomas Colcombet and Daniela Petrişan redid it in 2020. None of that uses the toll at all.

The nearest relative in physics is in thermodynamics. Elliott Lieb and Jakob Yngvason showed in 1999 that once you fix which states can be reached from which, and that follows a short list of rules, there's a quantity that goes up exactly along it, and it's the only one once you pick where zero is and what one unit is. Their order goes in and their count comes out forced. Here the price list goes in and the count comes out forced.

\subsection{The toll is an old trick with a new name}

It would be nice to have a new kind of cost argument here. This isn't one. The toll is a case of a technique computer scientists have used since 1985, and that a mathematician studying whether things settle down had, in another form, in 1892.

It's called the potential method. Give every state a number, the potential, that measures how much is stored up in it. Then look at each step and ask: does the step's cost, plus the change in potential, ever come out negative? If it never does, then any run costs at least as much as the potential dropped from start to finish, because the drop had to be paid for along the way. Robert Tarjan set it out in 1985, crediting the potential view to Daniel Sleator. Tarjan used it the other way round, to show a run costs at most so much.

Do his coin version. A stack of coins. PUSH adds one and costs one. POP-ALL removes them all and costs as many as it removed. Let the potential be the number of coins. PUSH costs one and adds one to the potential. POP-ALL of k coins costs k and drops the potential by k. So any run from an empty stack costs at most twice the number of pushes, however many expensive pops are in it.

The decreasing number underneath it is Aleksandr Lyapunov's, from his 1892 doctoral thesis on whether a moving system settles down. It was in Russian. A French translation came out in 1907, made by a French naval engineer, Édouard Davaux, and corrected by Lyapunov himself. The English one came out in 1992, a hundred years after the original, translated from the French. A translation of a translation. Did the computer scientists of 1985 get it from him? I can't tell you, and nothing here needs them to have. What I have is a picture: the same idea, a number that only goes one way along a run, in a thesis about whether a moving system settles down and in a paper about data structures, close to a century apart.

Now point it at the record. Let the potential be one when the record says not done and zero when it says done. A step that takes not done to done drops the potential by one, so for the step's cost plus the change in potential not to come out negative, that step has to cost at least one. That's the toll, exactly. And ``any run from not done to done costs at least one'' is just the method's usual conclusion, the potential dropping from one to zero.

Ask for the other direction too, that a step's cost is never more than the potential it drops, and the two together pin every price exactly: one on a step that settles something, zero on every other step, and nothing ever going back to not done. One catch: that only works if the price can depend on where the step starts. With a single price per button, like the stamp, a button that settles once still costs one when it's pressed again on done, so no price list per button can meet both directions.

Two more tools sit next to this. Run a list of moves inside a wrapper that adds up a number as it goes, and you get back the final state and the run's total; Norman Danner and Daniel Licata built cost reasoning for programs on exactly that, in 2015 with Ramyaa Ramyaa and in 2022. And give each move its price ahead of time, as a tag that adds up as the moves do; that's Shin-ya Katsumata's grades from 2014, which Dominic Orchard, Vilem Liepelt and Harley Eades put into a working language in 2019. The potential method itself went into the checking of programs before any of those: Martin Hofmann and Steffen Jost in 2003, and Jan Hoffmann, Klaus Aehlig and Hofmann in 2011. The difference with this book is when the number gets written. A cost checked ahead of time gets checked before the program runs, by whoever checks it. A cost kept in the state gets written while it runs, by the step itself. The number is the same. The moment of truth is different. For a program with loops whose cost depends on its input, a fixed tag can only bound the cost, while the counter holds the exact number; cost-aware type theories like calf, from Yue Niu, Jonathan Sterling, Harrison Grodin and Robert Harper in 2022, reason about such costs with proofs.

And there's one more neighbour, closer than any of those. A number the step keeps up to date and nothing else ever reads is what people who prove programs correct call ghost state. Susan Owicki and David Gries added it to programs in 1976, because some true facts about a program can't be proved without it, under the rule that it never steers the program. Jean-Christophe Filliâtre, Léon Gondelman and Andrei Paskevich worked out in 2014 when ghost code can be removed without changing what the program computes. By that test the counter is ghost whenever no step reads it. So what does putting it in the step buy? Two things. The number is there while the machine runs, where somebody looking through a window can see it, not only inside a proof written afterwards. And the rule that keeps it covers every move the machine has, so every run.

Two last relatives. Chapter four's floor, ``any run from not done to done costs at least one,'' is the cheapest-path bound in a system with weights on its moves: every path from the not-done states to the done ones crosses the line between them, and every step across that line weighs at least one. Weighted machines like that go back to Marcel-Paul Schützenberger in 1961. And the physicists who study what you can turn into what, when some operations are free, have the same shape: Bob Coecke, Tobias Fritz and Robert Spekkens gave the general setup in 2016, Fritz worked out its algebra in 2017, and Tomáš Gonda and Spekkens catalogued the numbers that never go up under free operations in 2023. Read with ``done'' as the thing you're after and one unit of cost as the currency, the not-done potential is the simplest of those. It's a neighbour to know about, not a result of mine.

So what's new, if the method isn't? Only where it's pointed. Tarjan and Sleator's method proves things about costs. What this book adds is which potential, the one that drops when something settles, and what happens to that when you can only see part of the state through a window.

\subsection{What the counter is, said once}

A receipt is a total that lives in the state, only climbs, equals the sum of the charges on every path including the failures, and is the only such total, on every state a run from the start reaches, for its price list. Each of those had a home before this book: the stick for a total that lives in the state, Lamport's clocks for climbing, Turing's and Floyd's numbers that only go down for the same rule run backwards, the cancelling sum for adding up, the step-by-step argument for the one honest count. None of them is physics, and none of them says what the charges should be. Once you've chosen the charges, the counter is the arithmetic.

There's one thing a counter can't do just by existing. The stick works because you can lay the two halves together. What if all you're ever shown is one half, through a window that doesn't show the notches? Then you're back in chapter seven. Take two states that differ only in their count, one at 1 and one at 2, and let nothing that comes through the window depend on the count. The window gives the same view for both, any reader gives the same answer for both, and one of the answers is wrong. So a window that leaves out the count can't give it back. To read the count, the count has to come through.

%===========================================================================
\section{What a ticked box lets you lean on}
\label{sec:lean-on}
%===========================================================================

I keep writing ``you can lean on it'' and then backing off. Let me stop doing that and say what it would take. A box went from not ticked to ticked. The toll charged the move that ticked it. It stays ticked. Chapter eight made it established: it could only be ticked right after a check of that very claim passed. And the right window lets you see it.

Now: what is a later step allowed to do because of that? That's a different question from ``is the claim true,'' and the gap between those two is what this chapter is about.

\subsection{The coin that moved}

Run this on a friend. The feeling of it is the point.

Put a coin under one of three cups while they watch; say cup one. Send them out of the room. Move the coin to cup three. Then, and this is the trick, put a second coin under cup one. Bring them back and ask: is there a coin under cup one?

Say they answer yes, because they trust the coin they saw has stayed put. There is a coin there, so they're right. And their reason was a good one: they watched it go in, and nobody usually moves coins around. But look at what happened. Their reason is about the first coin. The answer came out true because of the second coin. They were right by luck.

That's Gettier's trap again, from chapter eight, done with cups. Edmund Gettier's three pages in 1963 broke a definition of knowing: that you know something when it's true, you believe it, and you've got good reasons. Gettier took that definition from two philosophers of the 1950s, Roderick Chisholm and A. J. Ayer, and the only place he mentions Plato is a footnote saying Plato ``seems to be considering'' something like it. The Stanford Encyclopedia's entry on the analysis of knowledge, by Jonathan Ichikawa and Matthias Steup, says that definition gets called ``traditional,'' and that the reason part of it is ``often traced to Plato's Theaetetus.'' The same entry calls the idea that it was ever widely held ``a bit of a convenient fiction.'' And Julien Dutant, a philosopher at King's College London, wrote a 2015 paper called ``The Legend of the Justified True Belief Analysis,'' arguing that the older tradition asked for something stronger: a mark on the belief that can't be wrong. I've read only summaries of Dutant's paper, so take that last part as his, secondhand.

Bertrand Russell had a related case in 1948: somebody looks at a stopped clock at the one moment it happens to be right. They're right about the time, and they know nothing. And 1948 isn't the bottom either. The Stanford Encyclopedia gives a case from the eighth-century Buddhist philosopher Dharmottara: you're looking for water on a hot day, you see water ahead, it's a mirage, and when you get there you happen to find water under a rock. And one from Peter of Mantua, a logician who died in 1400: Plato is running right next to you, you take him for Socrates, so you believe Socrates is running, and Socrates is in fact running, in Rome, where you can't see him. People in different centuries, languages and traditions kept building the same trap by hand: a reason that points at one thing, and a truth that happens to be sitting on another.

What would fix your friend's reason? Lift cup one again. Now there's a new look at the coin that's actually there. That gives them evidence about this coin, now, instead of a memory about the coin that left.

\subsection{Plato nearly had it}

The dialogue where Plato goes after knowing is the Theaetetus, and it's named for a real person, so I wanted to know who. He was a mathematician. In the dialogue he's a teenager, and he tells Socrates that his teacher had been showing them, one case at a time, that the side of a square of area three, or five, and so on up to seventeen, can't be written as any fraction. That's the Pythagoreans' diagonal from chapter ten, done case by case. The young man's idea was to stop going one case at a time and find one word for all of them. Euclid's tenth book, on lengths that share no common measure, is thought to be largely his work. The dialogue opens with him being carried home to Athens from the army at Corinth, badly wounded and sick, barely alive; the usual date is 369 BC. And the conversation itself survives as a record: Euclid of Megara, not the geometer, says he took notes as soon as he got home, filled them out later, and had Socrates correct them whenever he came to Athens. So the book about what turns a true belief into knowing survives as a record Socrates himself corrected.

In it, Socrates tries three different meanings for the ``account'' that should turn a true belief into knowledge: a statement you can say out loud; a list of the thing's parts; and, third, the mark that sets this thing apart from everything it could be confused with. Each one fails, and the dialogue ends with nobody knowing what knowledge is, which is one of the more honest endings in philosophy. But the third one is the one I'd underline: the account as the thing that tells the real case apart from its rivals. He couldn't make it work because he had no way to say what that account had to be attached to.

Socrates gives his own reason for dropping it: if the account means knowing the mark, then knowledge has been defined as true belief plus knowledge of something, and the word you were defining is back inside its own definition. A circle. Does a check get out of it? It gets the word out: ``a check that ran and found the mark'' isn't defined in terms of knowing. Whether the check was any good is still a real question, and the rest of this chapter puts a price on it. So the circle moves rather than vanishing. It moves somewhere you can look at it.

\subsection{Three things on hand}

Here's what I mean by being entitled to lean on a claim. Three things have to be there.

An object, kept in the machine's state: a grouping, a list, a count, something you could print out. A belief doesn't count.

A checked relation between that object and the claim: a check that actually ran, with its result on the record. ``A check exists somewhere'' doesn't count.

A paid event: a step of the run where the object was put in place, and which the price list charged at least one. In a Thiele machine, the step that settles something is a paid step, because the toll charges it.

Keep the second and third apart, because they come apart. A note saying ``coin under cup one, checked'' can be written without anyone lifting a cup: an object, but no checked relation. Now actually lift the cup, find the coin, and write it down, but let that step be free, so the box never gets ticked and nothing gets charged. The object and the check are both there. The paid event is missing. And the other way round: ticking the box costs one, but the toll alone says nothing about what the step looked at. Payment by itself supplies no check.

And here's the sentence most likely to get lost: nothing in the bare machine makes a later step read the box. A move is handed the state and goes where its rule says, and its rule is allowed to ignore the box completely. So when I say a run is entitled to lean on a claim, I mean what somebody checking the run afterwards may conclude. The machine doesn't have to act on it, and unless somebody builds it to, it won't.

Line up the three things this book has called by related names, so they don't blur:

The bare toll: a paid move from not done to done. Nothing says a check happened at all.

Entitlement: an object, a check that ran with its result on the record, and a paid event. Nothing yet says the check was about this very claim.

An established record: from a clean start, it goes up only right after a check of the same property, on the same thing, with nothing changed in between, and the raise pays.

Even that last one is missing two things. Whether the check is honest is a fact about the checker, not something the record holds. And nothing asks whether the check could have failed: a check that says yes to everything is perfectly legal, true, and useless.

Why does the honest checker carry so much? The philosopher Linda Zagzebski argued in 1994 that any account of knowing that leaves even a small gap between having good reasons and being right lets a Gettier case through: take a case where bad luck makes the reasons point wrong, then add good luck that makes the belief true anyway. The three conditions plus an honest check close that gap, and only half of honesty is needed: the half that says no when the claim is false. A check that can be wrong, a set of tests that sometimes passes broken code, puts you right back in her gap, and then the record establishes something smaller and still true: this procedure said yes.

And who vouches that the check is honest? The record doesn't. Honesty is about what the check does on every possible input, and a finished state only holds this run. A proof about the checker can be written down and checked, but the checking is a check too, with its own honesty to vouch for, and somewhere the chain stops at a proof somebody accepts. In this book that proof is read once, outside every run, and nothing on the record pays for it. So an established claim is two things stuck together: something about the run, which the record carries, and something about the checker, which a proof carries.

The engineering version of this was built in 1996 and 1997. George Necula called it proof-carrying code: when you send a program to a computer that doesn't trust you, you send a proof with it that the program follows that computer's safety rules, and the computer checks the proof that arrived before it runs anything. Not your reputation, not ``a proof exists somewhere,'' but the one in the box. Stephen Cook's certificates from 1971, in chapter four, have the same bones: a claim comes with something that's quick to check, and the checker checks that.

And David Hume, in 1748, arguing about miracles, laid out what a receipt replaces. He weighed testimony against evidence and wrote that ``a wise man, therefore, proportions his belief to the evidence.'' The way I read him, his whole argument is a method for how much to trust a witness when you have no receipt. The move in this book is to stop weighing. A claim on the record is either backed by its object and its paid event, or it's testimony from outside, and a run that leans on testimony hasn't earned it itself.

Robert Brandom, a philosopher at the University of Pittsburgh, built a whole account of language on two words this book leans on, commitment and entitlement, in Making It Explicit in 1994. In his picture, as I read it and as his reviewers describe it, people talking keep score on each other: what each speaker is committed to, and what each is entitled to claim. Many claims count as entitled by default until somebody challenges them, and entitlement can be borrowed: I can claim a thing on your say-so and point to you if I'm challenged. Lay mine beside his and the differences are the content. His score is kept by the other people in the room; here it's in the machine's state. His entitlement often comes by default; here it's an event that has to happen and gets charged. His borrowing is my testimony, let back in on a leash: borrowed entitlement is only as good as the copy is genuine, which is chapter seven's problem.

\subsection{Changing your mind about the cups}

There's a whole field about how a reasonable mind should change what it believes when something new comes in, and I couldn't keep its people apart until I put them back on the table with the cups.

Your friend believes the coin is under cup one. They can learn something that doesn't clash with that, say cup two is empty, and just add it. They can lift cup one, find nothing, and have to change their mind about a table nobody touched. Or you can tell them you moved the coin. Those last two felt alike to me at first. They aren't. In one the friend was wrong. In the other the friend was right, and then the table changed.

Krister Segerberg, a Swedish logician, built a logic in 1998 for accepting something so it can never be given up. Isaac Levi split taking on a belief into a deliberate kind and a routine kind: deliberate is reasoning your way there, and routine is accepting whatever a procedure you trusted in advance hands you, the way you'd accept a reading off a thermometer you've already decided to trust, or what's under a cup when you lift it. The trust goes in once, before the answer comes. A passing check behind an established record is routine in exactly that sense.

Neil Tennant and Gösta Grahne I first took as two separate complaints. They're one move made from opposite sides. The target is the best-known impossibility result in that field, Peter Gärdenfors's theorem from 1986, that a natural rule for ``if'' statements can't live with reasonable mind-changing. Tennant argued in 2008 that it only works by treating some additions as changes of mind: the friend who learned cup two was empty gets counted as having changed their mind. Grahne argued in 1998 that it only bites real changes of mind, so the friend whose coin was moved is out of its reach. Side by side, they squeeze the theorem down to one case: being wrong about a table nobody touched.

The line this book leans on hardest is between those two kinds of change. Revising is changing what you believe about a world that stayed put. Updating is changing it because the world moved. Hirofumi Katsuno and Alberto Mendelzon drew that line for databases in 1991; Arthur Keller and Marianne Winslett had been working on updates since the mid-1980s. And look at what the friend sees: an empty cup one is the same empty cup whether they misremembered or you moved the coin. What they see can't tell revising from updating. That's a window with two states behind one view. What tells them apart is a count of how often the cups were touched. A machine can keep that count, a version number for each thing, and treat every write as an update: a write moves the version number, so every fact about the old version stops counting for the new one.

Alexandru Baltag, Bryan Renne and Sonja Smets gave a logic in 2014 that keeps apart three things Gettier's cases blur: good belief, knowing with the reason written out, and evidence that settles it. The friend who watched has good belief. The friend who lifted the cup has evidence that settles it.

Put that together and it says something exact about this book's record. A record that's never wrong and never corrected would be a stubborn fool's dream if its sentences were about the present. They aren't. Each one says a check passed on one version of one thing, and that sentence stays true after the version number moves on. The record can be never wrong and never corrected only because its sentences have the version stamped on them. Strip the stamps, and the first write would turn it into a liar that can't take anything back.

Push on that and it gives twice. The record is Segerberg's never-give-it-up acceptance every time, with no changing its mind in it. If a check turns out dishonest, the only fix that fits a record like this is a second record on top saying ``withdrawn,'' the way a list of cancelled certificates works. And the record is never wrong partly because it only talks about past versions. Whoever reads it wants now, and getting from ``held at that version'' to ``holds now'' crosses every write since. The record doesn't make that step for you.

Chapter eight met the logicians who taught knowing to count, Ho Ngoc Duc and Artemov and Kuznets. One more thing about them, now that a record is on the table. Duc's knowing points forward, at what an agent could come to know with more steps of reasoning. A record points back, at what was checked. They're one arrow, read from opposite ends. A check is one of Duc's trains of thought, and what I've added is a direction and a charge on stopping. Where the charge sits follows from one choice: I want it to bind every program the machine runs, and a charge that does that has to sit inside the step, since a program can skip any charge kept somewhere it can write.

Three things entitlement isn't. It isn't permission: a system that says who may write to what grants that from outside and says nothing about truth. It isn't ``it's true, so I can use it'': that's the cups. And it isn't ``right by luck'': a shortcut that gives the right answer because the inputs happened to cooperate is the stopped clock.

\subsection{Count the rivals}

Now the number, because a book about what keeping costs has to say how much, and the idea fits in a game with three playing cards.

Shuffle cards marked 1, 2 and 3 face down. Sort them using only questions of the form ``is this one bigger than that one?'' and count the questions. There are six orders the cards could be in. Each question has two answers, so after one question you've split the six into at most two piles, after two at most four, and you need to get down to one. Four is less than six, so two questions can't always do it, and three can. Draw the tree of questions and you'll see it: six leaves, three deep. The number of questions you need is at least the number of halvings it takes to get from all the possibilities down to one, rounded up. Donald Knuth gives this, in the sorting volume of The Art of Computer Programming, as the floor for all sorting by comparing: a tree so many questions deep has at most two to that many leaves.

Is the floor always the price? Try five cards. There are 120 orders, and seven halvings get you under that, so you need at least seven questions, and seven is enough, though not by the obvious method. Finding the way is a good evening with a deck of cards; Knuth has it, under the name merge insertion, from Lester Ford and Selmer Johnson in 1959. Now take twelve cards. They can be in 479,001,600 orders, so the count says at least 29. Ford and Johnson's method takes 30, and in 1965 Mark Wells showed by a computer search that nothing does better: twelve cards need 30. The floor was 29 and the price is 30. So the count is a floor and only a floor. It never promises the floor can be reached.

Ralph Hartley measured information exactly this way in 1928, before Shannon: by the number of possibilities, with no chances in it, just the count. That's the measure I use here, and it's Hartley's.

The same count works with something you can hold. Put three coins under three cups. Each coin is heads or tails, so there are eight possible rows. Somebody tells you heads under the first cup and tails under the second. Lift the first cup and compare: if it matches, four possible rows are left. Lift the second and compare: two left. The last coin could still be either. Eight, four, two.

This is a parlour game, and an old one. In A Christmas Carol, in 1843, Scrooge's nephew Fred plays a game called Yes and No at his Christmas party: he thinks of something, and everybody else asks questions he may only answer yes or no. He's thinking of an animal, a disagreeable, savage one that growls and grunts and lives in London, and the answer is Uncle Scrooge. Most people know it as twenty questions, and twenty is a good number for it: twenty questions that each split the field in half can single out one of about a million things. But the halving only happens because Fred answers truthfully about what's actually in his head. Lie once and the count still shrinks, around the wrong animal.
