The cups showed the same thing: the four and the two are real because you lifted the cup and compared. Being told what was under it wouldn't have made them real.

Now put it on a run. Say a run comes with a list of possibilities still on the table, and a shorter list with some crossed off. Picture the crossing off as a tree of questions, like the card tree. If the tree is no deeper than the number of paid steps the run took, and it has enough leaves to cover the narrowing, then the run paid at least the number of halvings it takes to point at one thing on the long list, minus the same for the short list.

Chapter six is why the payment has to go in as an assumption. Looking can narrow a list without costing anything: the demon's watcher narrowed two candidates to one for free. So the narrowing alone doesn't force anybody to pay. Wherever this book puts a price on narrowing, the payment goes in out in the open: either a tree no deeper than the paid steps is handed in, or the run does the narrowing itself, with every test step charged at least one and every test keeping at least a set share of the list.

That last condition does real work, and you can see it fail. Take a list of eight things and one test: ``is it this one?'' If it passes, one thing is left. That's three halvings for one paid step. If every test has to keep at least half the list, that test isn't allowed, and with it allowed, the bound breaks. So the bound is: if every test keeps at least a share of one over two-to-the-k, the halvings dropped are at most k times what the run paid.

\subsection{Narrower means a stronger test}

One link here is pure logic, and Plato's third account is the whole of it. Take a list of candidates and something you can observe about each one. The list's test passes an observation when some candidate on the list would show it. Shrink the list, and the test can only get pickier: anything the short list's test passes, the long list's test passes too.

Pickier isn't the same as strictly pickier, though, and the cards show the gap. Take all six orders as the long list, and as the short list the three where card 1 sits left of card 2: 1-2-3, 1-3-2 and 3-1-2. Observe ``is 1 left of 2?'' Every survivor says yes, and the dropped 2-1-3 says no. So the long list's test passes yes and no, and the short list's test passes only yes: strictly pickier. Now, with the same two lists, observe ``is the first card a 3?'' The survivor 3-1-2 says yes and the other two say no. Among the dropped ones, 3-2-1 says yes and 2-1-3 says no. Both tests pass yes and no. Same strength, even though three orders are gone.

What made the difference is a dropped candidate whose observation no survivor shares. That candidate is Plato's mark, the thing that sets this case apart from everything it could be confused with, turned into something you can check. Narrow the list and keep one candidate like that, and the test is strictly stronger. Narrow it without one, and the test may not have moved at all.

\subsection{What you've got}

Put the pieces on one list. Narrow a list of candidates with a test that tells some dropped candidate apart from every survivor. Find the paid step. Count the charges against a tree the run paid for. Then three things follow, on any Thiele machine: the short list's test is strictly stronger than the long one's; some step of the run took the record from not done to done, and that step was charged at least one; and the run's charges add up to at least the halvings the list lost.

A statement like that is true by packaging. It takes the conditions in and hands the conclusions back. The right word for it is a checklist, a checklist for leaning on a claim. It doesn't discover anything about leaning.

And a checklist isn't nothing. On 30 October 1935, at Wright Field outside Dayton, Ohio, the Army was evaluating Boeing's new four-engine bomber, the Model 299. Shortly after take-off it plunged back onto the field and caught fire; that's how the wire report of that day has it, and later histories add that it pitched up and stalled first, which is what controls locked in place would do. Two men died of their injuries: the Army's pilot, Major Ployer Hill, and Boeing's chief test pilot, Leslie Tower. The airplane was fine. The crew had left the gust locks on, a mechanism worked from the cockpit that holds the control surfaces still while the plane sits on the ground. There's a famous line that a newspaper called it too much airplane for one man to fly. I couldn't find that line in any paper from 1935, and a search of the scanned newspapers of 1935 and 1936 didn't turn it up either; what the papers that November report, like the New Castle News on the 12th, is the locked controls. Atul Gawande, a surgeon who writes about how people get complicated work right, gave the line in a New Yorker article in December 2007, ``as a newspaper put it,'' without naming the paper. The words are older than his article, though. Edward Jablonski's 1965 history of the Flying Fortress says the talk after the crash was that it was ``too much airplane for one pilot to handle,'' Martin Caidin's 1968 book has ``rumors that the 299 was too much airplane for any one pilot,'' and the exact line is in DeWitt Copp's A Few Great Captains in 1980, as word reported from the War Department, which Copp says wasn't true. So it's an old saying about that airplane. Whether a newspaper printed it in 1935, I still can't show you. What I can find is what came after. The answer was a card: a list of things to check, in order, every time. The most careful history of it I found is by Walter Boyne, a retired Air Force colonel. By his account it wasn't the first written routine for a pilot; those go back before the First World War. And it's this crash, he says, that made the checklist the way a crew flew. The airplane went on to be the B-17. Nothing on that card was a discovery. Every item on it was something the pilots already knew. What the card did was make sure none of it got left out when it mattered.

\subsection{What standing behind a grouping gets you}

Chapter ten ended on buckets. The toll never needed them: it prices any yes-or-no question. Here a grouping gets a job, and an exact one.

Put two red buttons and two blue buttons on the table, numbered 1 to 4, with 1 and 2 red. The question is colour. Draw circles round 1 and 2, and round 3 and 4. Call a grouping sound when every two buttons inside one circle are the same colour. This one is. Split the red circle into two circles of one button each, and it stays sound; splitting can't put two different colours together. Join 1 and 3 into a circle instead, and it fails. Rename the circles North and South and nothing changes, because soundness only looks at which buttons share a circle.

Now the record. Whatever keeps the fact that a grouping was found sound, a mark on a card or a box in a machine, is a question that goes from not done to done and stays, so everything the first chapters said about that applies to it: the raise pays the toll, and keeping it takes two arrows meeting or fresh paper. None of that depends on what the mark is about. What the mark lets you lean on does depend on it.

Here's exactly what it lets you lean on. If your circles are sound, then any way of cutting them smaller is sound too, whatever the question is: every pair the smaller circles keep together was already together, and already matched. And for any grouping that isn't a cutting-smaller of yours, there's a question your circles pass and that one fails. To see it, find two buttons it puts together that your circles keep apart. Give everything in the first button's circle one colour and everything else another. Your circles are all one colour each, so they pass. The other grouping has both colours in one circle, so it fails.

So standing behind a sound grouping entitles you to exactly the groupings you get by cutting it smaller. A grouping that isn't a cutting-smaller can still happen to be sound for this question; paint all four buttons red and every grouping passes. But nothing in standing behind yours promises it. Change a button's colour after the mark was made, and the mark is still about the old colours. A record says what held when it was made, nothing more.

That's the three things from the start of this chapter in their smallest case: the object is the grouping, the checked relation is soundness for the question, and the paid event is the raise of the mark that keeps it. Like everything in this chapter, it says what somebody checking the run may conclude.

Which leaves the word on the cover. I named the toll ``No Free Insight,'' and I've been using ``insight'' as if it meant one thing. The next chapter says what it means.

%===========================================================================
\section{What insight is}
\label{sec:insight}
%===========================================================================

I named the toll ``No Free Insight'' before I had any careful account of what insight is. The book has been narrowing it down ever since. Chapter six took looking, copying and finding out off the list: those can be free. What's left is the part that stays. So it's time to say what that is, in a way that someone who studies real insight, in real heads, wouldn't laugh at. Who are those people, and what did they find? Here's what I ran into, pushed on as I went.

\subsection{Sultan and the sticks}

In 1913 the psychologist Wolfgang Köhler went to Tenerife to run a research station with a colony of chimpanzees, and the First World War kept him there until 1920, five years longer than planned, with the chimpanzees to watch. The famous one is Sultan. Food is outside the cage, out of reach. Two sticks are inside, each too short, but one fits into the end of the other. Sultan tries each stick and fails. He sulks. He plays with the sticks. And then, by Köhler's account, at a moment you can see, he joins them and reaches the food. Köhler's word was Einsicht, which came into English as ``insight,'' and the whole school of psychology he belonged to, the Gestalt school, said that's what thinking is: a rearranging, the moment two sticks stop being two sticks and become one tool. Piling up tries until one works was, for them, something else.

Karl Duncker gave the human version, in German in 1935 and in English in 1945, five years after he died at thirty-seven; he'd spent his last years working with Köhler at Swarthmore. You can set it up yourself: a candle, a box of tacks, matches, and the job of fixing the candle to the wall. The hard part is seeing the box as a shelf while it's sitting there being the thing that holds the tacks. And he found that if you hand people the box empty, with the tacks poured out beside it, they solve it far more readily. Same objects. What changed was which relation between them was on the table.

I like both of these for the same reason: the insight didn't add an object. Sultan had both sticks the whole time. The box was there the whole time. What arrived was a relation, a piece of structure in chapter ten's sense, and it arrived all at once. Hold on to that, because the thing the toll charges for also arrives in one step, and at its best it's also a relation.

\subsection{It isn't a feeling}

A feeling of getting closer is a different thing from solving the problem. In the 1980s the psychologist Janet Metcalfe asked people to solve problems while rating, every fifteen seconds, how close they felt to the answer, how ``warm.'' On ordinary problems, like algebra, the warmth climbs steadily and peaks at the answer, which is what you'd expect. On insight problems, riddles of the candle-box kind, the warmth stays flat, flat, flat, and then the answer arrives. And the people who felt warmest beforehand were, if anything, more likely to be wrong. She put that in the title of a 1986 paper: premonitions of insight predict impending error. That's about how well warmth predicts in those tasks. It doesn't show that solving something comes with no feeling, or that everybody follows the same curve.

Try it yourself. Write a number from one to seven every fifteen seconds while you do a long sum, then while you do a riddle, and compare the two rows of numbers.

And the most famous insight story there is fits Metcalfe uncomfortably well. Archimedes, asked by Hiero, the king of Syracuse, whether a goldsmith had cheated him on a crown, gets into a bath, sees the water rise, and runs home naked shouting ``eureka,'' I've found it. That comes from Vitruvius, a Roman writer on architecture, writing about two centuries later. Is it true? Galileo Galilei, at twenty-two, in 1586, took it as known that Archimedes caught the goldsmith. What he didn't buy was the method. Water spilling over the side, as Vitruvius tells it, was to him ``a crude thing, far from scientific precision,'' and he guessed some writer had written down a rumour. So he wrote a little paper, La Bilancetta, proposing a balance weighed in air and in water instead, which he thought was probably what Archimedes actually did, since it rests on Archimedes's own results. Whether there was ever a bath, or a shout, nobody can check now.

Look at what the legend kept: the bath, the shout, running home naked. The feeling. What it left shaky is the part that would count as the check, the test that could have caught the goldsmith. The bit everybody remembers is the bit that tells you least.

So an account of insight that keys on the ``aha'' is keyed to something the evidence says is unreliable. Whatever insight is, it isn't the feeling of having it. That's a relief, because a machine doesn't have feelings, and a word that only meant a feeling would be cheating if I hung it on one. The version this book can price has to be keyed to something that happens in a step. The question is which step.

\subsection{What happens when a computation learns}

Keep the board from chapter four in front of you: the NO box, the YES box, the tally line, and the stamp. Add one more move that does nothing to the boxes; call it WORK. It stands in for every ordinary step a machine takes.

The first thing that can happen is plain looking. Something comes in and gets written down. A count ticks. Nothing is claimed about what it means, and the box doesn't move. The demon's notepad in chapter six was this. The statistician Dennis Lindley measured it in 1956, in a paper about how much information an experiment gives you, as how much your uncertainty is expected to drop. That's a real quantity, and it's about what was seen. On the price list it can cost nothing: the toll says nothing at all about a move that doesn't take NO to YES, so a price list may charge it zero. ``May'' is the word that matters. A price list is allowed to charge for looking anyway. And in a real device a look takes something too: chapter six showed it uses up a blank page, borrowed until the page is wiped.

Lindley's measure has a back story, and it runs through Bletchley Park. I. J. Good, who was Alan Turing's main statistical assistant there in 1941, wrote in 1979 that Turing had worked out how much evidence an experiment should be expected to give, to judge one of the codebreaking methods before running it, partly getting to Lindley's idea fifteen years early, in work that stayed secret. Turing measured evidence in ``bans,'' with a tenth of one called a deciban, and the name came from the town of Banbury, where tens of thousands of the sheets used in that method were printed. And Good adds that Turing didn't know the measure he was using had already appeared in 1878, in a paper by Charles Sanders Peirce, the same Peirce from chapter nine, who'd called it the weight of evidence. It changes nothing here, but it shows how well trodden this kind of thing is. For a century and a half careful people have measured what an observation tells you, and every one of those measures is about what was seen. The toll leaves that side of the line alone.

The second thing that can happen is the raise. Some move takes the box from NO to YES, and that's where the toll applies: that move costs at least one, and every run from NO to YES pays at least one somewhere. What gets charged, at its best, is the Gestalt thing: a relation committed to, one tool claimed out of two sticks. I say ``at its best'' because chapter four was careful about what YES means: the coin is in YES, and nothing more. A raise that writes down which relation it's committing to carries Sultan's tool with it. A bare stamp commits to nothing at all, and it pays exactly the same.

The third thing isn't a kind of step. It's something about the price: it's the same, whatever got settled. Say the YES state also shows something striking, a deep relation, a result nobody could have guessed, Sultan's two sticks become one tool. The toll charges the raise at least one. It doesn't charge the striking raise more, because the reason it charges anything only looks at the box before and after. On the board, the stamp from NO makes one mark whether you were thinking of Duncker's box or of lunch. A different price list could charge them differently. The toll doesn't make it.

The candle box has a footnote that says the same thing from the other side. In 1962 the psychologist Sam Glucksberg ran Duncker's problem with money on it: some people were told they could win five or twenty dollars depending on how fast they solved it. With the tacks in the box, the people chasing the money did worse than the people who weren't. With the box empty the problem was easier for everybody, and I'll leave what the money did there alone. Paying more didn't buy the insight, and on the hard version it got in the way. That's a fact about heads, not records, and I won't push it further. But it makes a nice parallel to the board. On the board a deeper raise isn't charged more, and in Glucksberg's room a bigger prize didn't bring the insight sooner. In neither place does the price measure the depth.

So: looking can stay off the bill, the raise can't, and the toll doesn't charge more for a deeper claim. Who decided that split? Not the prices alone. The toll prices exactly one thing, the move that settles. A price list is allowed to put a floor under more moves than that, and allowed to leave others at zero. The sorting into ``moves that settle'' and ``moves that don't'' is by whether the box moves, and that sorting is mine. The part that's forced is the floor under the move that settles, once you grant finite chairs, done staying done, and a price on two arrows meeting. That's chapter three. Anything a price list charges beyond that is a choice, and any real machine owes you its whole price list so you can see which choices it made.

\subsection{Test, then commit}

The philosopher Karl Popper, who you met in chapter nine, said a theory earns its standing by being exposed: you make a guess, you try to kill it, and what survives a real attempt counts for something, where ``real'' means the test could have failed. His word for that earned credit is corroboration.

Add a move to the board: CHECK. It looks at something, a sum, a coin under a cup, and it can come out pass or fail, and it doesn't move the coin. Charge it whatever you like. Charging it on the attempt, pass or fail, fits Popper, because what made his test count was that it really ran and could have failed. CHECK is the test, and it never takes NO to YES. The stamp is the commit. So the honest way to go is two moves: test, then commit, with two separate costs.

But nothing on the bare board makes the stamp wait for the check. The stamp takes NO to YES from anywhere. Press the stamp with no CHECK before it, and you land in YES and make one mark, the same box and the same mark as the player who checked first. Their tally lines differ by whatever CHECK was charged, and if you charged it nothing, they don't differ at all. The receipt says a commitment was paid for. It doesn't say the commitment was earned. Popper's credit needs the test. The toll doesn't.

You can build a board that makes the stamp wait: let CHECK leave a mark when it passes, and let the stamp only take NO to YES when that mark is there. That's chapter eight's established record, and it's a good design. It's a design. Nothing in the definition asks for it. The gap between the word and the bare machine is real, and I'm leaving it where you can see it: the toll prices the commit, and whether a test came first is a fact about the run, or about a design somebody chose.

\subsection{What the word means}

So here's insight, in the sense this book prices: a distinction made to stay. A move that takes something from not settled to settled, for good, and that move costs at least one, on any machine with the toll, whatever it's about and however deep it is. At its best, what's settled is a relation committed to, a claim checked and closed. A bare stamp that commits to nothing pays the same, and the toll can't tell the two apart. An established record is one way to make sure it's the first kind.

Three limits, each from a different place. From Metcalfe: it isn't a feeling, so a machine's version has to be a step. From the board: the floor is the same for a shallow commit and a deep one, so price isn't depth. A machine can pay a lot to check something trivial, and Duncker's box, a real insight, is one small commit. And from test-then-commit: a paid stamp proves nothing on its own. Price and correctness are separate books.

Which leaves a suspicion I'd have in your place. Everything so far has been one box on one sheet of paper, with a rule I chose and a count I added. Is the shape of that box something I picked, or is it what any box the machine itself turns on has to look like, on any machine at all? That's the next chapter.

%===========================================================================
\section{A box over any machine}
\label{sec:any-machine}
%===========================================================================

For the whole book so far I've been building up a box and a tally, and you'd be right to suspect that the whole thing is special pleading for one little board. So this chapter answers that suspicion with no particular machine in it at all. Take any machine that steps, anything with states and one next state for each. Put a box over it that watches it, under rules I'll spell out. Then ask: what can the box be?

The answer is one shape, and it's a shape you can build from a cup and a die.

\subsection{A cup and a die}

Put a cup upside down on the table. The rule is: the first time a six comes up, turn the cup right side up. There's no rule for turning it back over. Now roll a die twenty times. If a six comes up, the cup turns over on the first one and stays turned over. Write down which roll did it. That number is where the record happened, and the cup afterward is the record. If no six comes up, the cup stays down.

To make the die part of a machine with one next state, write the twenty rolls down first and read them off one at a time. The machine underneath is the list and a finger pointing at the next entry; each step moves the finger one place, whatever the cup is doing. The question the cup asks at each step is: is this entry a six? Nothing about the cup changes which entry comes next.

The cup is a latch, the same thing you built with two boxes in chapter four. Electrical engineers have had the circuit since 1918, when two British physicists teaching engineering in London, William Eccles and Frank Jordan, applied for a patent on a two-valve arrangement that holds whichever state you last put it in. Their 1919 paper calls it a trigger relay. It became the flip-flop, and the memory inside the device you're reading this on comes from it. Cut its reset wire and what's left has one input, ``set,'' and one rule: the new state is the old state, or set. Once set, it stays set. The cup is that circuit with its reset wire cut.

Did anybody ever build a whole memory out of cups with no reset wire, on purpose? Somebody did, and for a reason close to this book's. In the mid-1950s Wen Tsing Chow, an engineer at the Arma division of the American Bosch Arma Corporation in Garden City, New York, was asked by the United States Air Force, according to the Wikipedia history of the programmable read-only memory, for a safer way to hold the fixed numbers, the targeting constants, inside the guidance computer of the Atlas missile. What he and his colleague William Henrich patented, filed in 1957 and granted in 1962 under the plain title ``Storage matrix,'' was a grid with a connection at every crossing, where you set the numbers by taking out the connections you didn't want, or burning them out with too much current. The word burn is right there in the patent, and people still say they burn a chip. A fuse blown in one stays blown. That proves nothing here, because nothing below cares what a cup is made of. But it's good to know the shape wasn't invented for a sheet of paper. Somebody needed it, for higher stakes than a die.

Here's the claim, loosely first: every box that answers only to what the machine is doing, and never comes back down, is a cup with no reset wire, turned by some event the machine passes through.

\subsection{What "answers only to the machine" has to mean}

The content is in the rules, so here they are.

Call the machine underneath the base: the part the box watches. Now build a bigger machine that has the base inside it, plus whatever else you like, plus the box and a tally. The bigger machine has to run the base faithfully: start where the base starts, step when the base steps, and stop when the base stops. It can carry as much extra hidden stuff as it wants. The base doesn't get to see any of that, and the extra stuff doesn't get to change what the base does.

Now the five rules for the box and the tally.

The box's next position depends only on what the base is doing right now and where the box is right now. ``What the base is doing'' means the state it's in and, when it has more than one move from there, the move it's making, like the stamp and the WORK move on the board from chapter thirteen. Nothing else. No clock on the wall, no hidden dial, no outside hand. I'll call that answering to the machine.

The tally never goes down.

The step that turns the box up costs at least one. That's the toll.

Once up, the box stays up.

And some run, from a place the machine can start, actually turns it up. Otherwise the box is decoration.

Here's what follows. Ask the bigger machine what it would do with the box down, above each state of the base, and each move when it has a choice: does the box go up on the next step, or not? Call that answer the event. Since the box answers only to the base, that's one answer per base state, or per state and move; no hidden stuff can change it. Now take any actual moment. If the box is down, the event is exactly what happens to it. If the box is up, it stays up, whatever the event says. Put those together: the box's next position is ``already up, or the event holds right now.'' That's the cup's rule. The cup turns over the first time the event holds and never turns back.

Look at which rules that used: only two of the five. Answering to the machine, and staying up. The tally, the toll, and the reachable turn played no part. A cup is what you get from ``answers only to the base'' and ``never comes back down,'' all by themselves.

The other three rules matter for the other direction. Pick any base and any event the base can actually reach. Build the cup on that event, price the turn at one, everything else at zero. Check the five rules: the cup only looks at the base and itself; the tally only goes up; the turn costs one; the cup stays up; and the run that reaches the event turns it. So every cup is a box like that too. Put the two directions together: boxes that answer only to the machine and never come down are exactly cups, nothing more and nothing less, over any machine at all.

The closest relatives are in proving programs correct, and they're old. Martín Abadi and Leslie Lamport, in 1991, added ``history variables'' to descriptions of systems: extra state that records what has happened and never steers the system. A history variable that only ever turns on is the cup. And there's a logic for talking about the past, set out by Orna Lichtenstein, Amir Pnueli and Lenore Zuck in 1985, with an operator ``once'': ``once h'' is true now exactly when h has been true at some point so far. A program watching a running system keeps ``once h'' in a single bit with exactly the cup's rule, and Klaus Havelund and Grigore Roşu built watchers like that in 2002. What this chapter adds is the other direction: a box that answers to the machine and never comes down can't be anything else.

One thing about that argument cuts both ways: it never asks what the base is. You decide that, by deciding what goes in the base and what goes in the extra stuff. Draw the line low, with the base being only the machine's own working state, and ``answers only to the base'' is a real demand. Draw it high, put every table and every rule into the base, and it's easy to meet. The cup comes out the same either way. So the argument holds wherever you draw the line, and that's exactly why it can't tell you where a record's meaning lives. The meaning lives in what the event looks at, and that's in the base, by how you drew it. When you meet a real machine, ask it first what it puts below the line.

\subsection{A base that never merges}

What about a base where no two arrows ever meet? Can it carry a cup?

It can, and you can see exactly what it pays. Keep every earlier position of the cup on a strip that grows by one entry each step, newest first. Then the whole machine still never has two arrows meeting: if two whole states land in the same place, the strips are the same, so the old cup positions were the same, and the base parts were the same because the base never merges, so the states were the same. But the strip gets longer every step, so the machine never comes back to a state it's been in. That's the fresh-paper column from chapter two. And on a machine with finitely many states, there's no fresh paper to be had, so a box that stays up can't be turned on by a move where no two arrows ever meet. That's the chairs, again.

People who study processes that talk to each other ran into the same thing from the other side. Vincent Danos and Jean Krivine, two computer scientists in Paris, wanted to pin down what a transaction is. In 2004 they built a version of Robin Milner's calculus of processes where every step can be rolled back, and in 2005 they modelled a transaction in it as a process that wanders freely through everything that can be undone, until it does something declared impossible to undo: the commit. As I read it, they start from steps that all come undone and ask what has to be added before anything sticks. This chapter starts from a box and asks what the step under it has to be. Theirs is declared. I thought mine was forced by counting, since on a finite machine the first turn of the cup makes two arrows meet whatever anyone calls it. Push on that and it gives: I choose the event, and on a base that grows its history forever, nothing merges at all, so there my staying up is declared too.

\subsection{The clean start}

There's an arrow of time in this, and I should say where it came from, because I put it there by hand. The cup starts down. That's the only reason a turned cup means the event happened: it was down at the start, and only the event turns it. Turn the cup up before the first roll and it tells you nothing about the die. So the clean start is assumed. It's a small version of what the philosopher David Albert, in 2000, named the past hypothesis: an assumed low-entropy beginning of the universe, an idea that goes back to Boltzmann. Several accounts of why records point to the past lean on it. Whether every trusted record leans on it is their argument, and I'm not settling it. Every result in this book that reads a turned cup as news that the event happened assumes a clean start and starts from there. The cup argument above doesn't need it, and that's exactly why, on its own, it can't say the event happened.

When I went looking for who else had found the arrow hiding in the start, I got five papers that read like five unrelated answers. They lined up once I asked each the same question: what does the cup being down at the start rest on? There are two places to put it. At the beginning, as a fact about how things started. Or in the structure of what causes what.

David Wolpert, a physicist, sorted memory systems in 1992, and one of his kinds works only because it begins in a prepared state, set up by a process that runs one way. That's the cup put down by somebody before the game. Wolpert and Jens Kipper, a philosopher, put that sorting on firmer footing in 2024, and they say plainly that they have no formal proof this kind can only remember the past. I trust them more for that sentence.

Aram Ebtekar and Marcus Hutter built a toy world in 2024 where every step can be reversed, imposed a clean start on it, and got records of the past and none of the future. As I read their route, the clean start goes through cause and effect first, and the arrow comes out of that. Thomas Blanchard, a philosopher, got the same lopsidedness of knowing out of a condition on cause and effect, also in 2024. So he starts in the middle of Ebtekar and Hutter's road. One is a toy world and the other is philosophy, and the fit between them is my reading.

And Carlo Rovelli, two years before his paper on the cost of choosing from chapter six, studied traces in a 2022 paper called ``Memory and Entropy,'' and concluded that a memory turns a low-entropy start into information you can use. He's at the first place, and he says what the beginning gets spent on. Read next to the cup: that's the blank the record uses up. The cup can say yes once because it started at no.

So does the cup explain why records point backwards? It doesn't, and I'll say that plainly. The step runs forward and the runs start clean, and anything built on those two can only hand the arrow back. Even the strip that grows forever starts empty, so it leans on the same blank. On the map, this book sits at the first place, with Wolpert and Rovelli, and it sits there by assumption.

Constructor theory, David Deutsch and Chiara Marletto's way of writing physics from chapter two, has a 2025 paper on time saying that laws stated as which transformations are possible can't mention time at all, so duration has to come from a second system you count, a clock. This book is in that position by construction: the only time in it is the machine's own steps. The hidden clock in the next section gets thrown out by the first rule, which read next to them is a rule about which system is allowed to be the clock: the one being recorded. But their laws have no step in them, and I put a step into the base by definition. So I'm in their position only at the far end of their argument, where a clock already exists. The base is my clock, and I assumed it.

Physicists have built this cup on paper in quantum mechanics too, and it behaves the way the reversible base above says. The question that sorted them for me is where the cup puts what it forgets. Klaus Hepp, in 1972, wanted a measuring device inside quantum mechanics itself, and studied a particle that flips spins in a long chain as it passes, now called the Coleman-Hepp model. I haven't read Hepp's paper itself; I know it from John Bell's, which took it apart in 1975: the record is exactly definite only if the chain is endlessly long. That's the fresh-paper column, a history that never ends. Jonathan Halliwell, in 1999, used a two-state detector that flips once when a particle passes and is kept from flipping back by a large surrounding environment, and his summary calls it ``effectively irreversible.'' I like that he puts the word ``effectively'' right there where you can see it. And Murray Gell-Mann, who named the quark, and James Hartle gave the property a name in 1995: the permanence of the past, past alternatives that stay settled as the story goes on. Hepp's chain had to be endless. Halliwell's had to live in an environment. Mine stays up, and the toll writes a unit on a tally. That's fine in a model of computation, and it's exactly where this stops being physics.

\subsection{Both rules do work}

A result with two conditions should have two counterexamples, one for each condition dropped. It does, and they're the two ways a record goes wrong in the world.

Drop ``stays up.'' Take a base that just counts, and a box that flips at every step: up, down, up, down. It answers to the machine. It doesn't stay up. And it's not a cup on any event. That's the coin from chapter three, the toggle: working state dressed up as a record. If a take-back has to stick, it's a second cup, ``withdrawn,'' which is how lists of revoked web certificates actually work: you add an entry; you never delete the certificate. Alin Tomescu and his coauthors built add-only lists like that for public logs in 2019, and a revocation fits exactly: it goes in once and stays. Two cups can also look at each other: try it with two cups where the second only turns on an even roll if the first is already up, and you'll see the second can never turn before the first. Each is still a cup; its event just gets to look at the other one.

Drop ``answers to the machine.'' Take a base that counts, and a box that turns up at the fifth tick of a hidden clock, whatever the base is doing. It stays up, and it doesn't answer to the machine. That's a timestamp. A timestamp says when, not what happened. A record switched by an outside party or a clock records that party or that clock, and leaves the computation out. Try it with the written-down die rolls: keep a second, hidden tally beside the list, and turn the cup when that tally reads five, then move both the tally and the finger. Start two runs at the same place on the list with the cup down, but one with the hidden tally at five and one at zero. The next cup positions disagree, although everything about the base agrees.

One more thing about the timestamp, because it's sneakier than it looks. Start it in step with the base, hidden tally and finger together. Then, on every state its runs actually reach, the hidden tally equals the position in the list, and the timestamp is exactly the cup on ``this is position five.'' The first rule only throws it out because the rule looks at every state, including the out-of-step ones no run from a clean start ever reaches. So the honest version is: a timestamp with its clock free to start anywhere fails even on the states its runs reach. Started in step, it's a cup on the runs it has.

Permanent but not answering to the machine is a timestamp. Answering to the machine but not permanent is a toggle. A record is the thing that's both. That's the most useful single sentence in this book for looking at systems other than the ones I build.

\subsection{Arguing with cups}

People who study arguments built the two-cup rule for debates. Give each speaker a row of cups, one for every claim they've made. Everybody I read in this field is answering two questions about those cups: whose hand turns them, and can they come back down?

C. L. Hamblin, an Australian philosopher and logician who also came up with reverse Polish notation for early computers, wrote Fallacies in 1970, and it has a chapter where each speaker has a store of what they're on the hook for, and the rules of the debate update it, not the speaker. I haven't read that chapter itself. That's ``answers to the machine,'' for arguments: the speaker's hand is kept off the cup. Henry Prakken, who works on AI and law, built debate games in 2005 on stores like that and made the claims sticky: a claim can be taken back only after it's been challenged, and taking back has a cost. He also floats a rule that once a speaker takes a claim back, they can't take it on again, which is a revocation that stays revoked. Erik Krabbe, an argumentation theorist, names the awkward part in 2001: taking things back is frowned on, and yet settling a dispute takes somebody taking back a doubt. A store that never gives can't settle anything, and a store that gives whenever asked isn't a store. This book answers Krabbe with a price: a take-back is a second cup, ``withdrawn,'' and turning it costs at least one like any other turn. Where mine gives is Prakken's first condition: nothing in my cup asks why the second cup turned. Its event can look at the base and the first cup, and nothing requires a challenge.

The same field has something that looks a lot like earning. In Phan Minh Dung's 1995 setup, arguments attack each other, and the most careful way of accepting them starts from the arguments nobody attacks and keeps adding the ones they defend. Things go in and never come out: a row of cups turned one at a time. Pietro Baroni and Massimiliano Giacomin in 2007, and Martin Caminada in 2014, worked out a stronger version where every accepted argument is defended by others that don't lean back on it, so the support bottoms out. That's an earned claim, in argument form. Notice the bottom, though: an argument nobody attacks gets in on a check that can't fail, and chapter eight says a check that can't fail establishes nothing. Turn that on my own cups, because it lands. My first turn needs only that the event holds, and I charge one for it, and a charge isn't a check. So my earned records bottom out the same way Dung's do, in something let in without an argument. The one difference is that mine pays to get in.

\subsection{A box that can change its mind}

A box that's allowed to change its mind isn't lawless. It's somebody else's subject. Count how many times the cup may be turned. Once, from down to up, is the cup. More than once and you're somewhere else, and the people who went there sorted it by how many turns are allowed, and by how long the game runs.

The philosopher Hilary Putnam and the computer scientist E. Mark Gold, in back-to-back papers in 1965, looked at questions a machine answers by guessing and revising, where the answer is whatever the guesses finally settle on. Putnam's paper says outright that you're never sure you've got the right answer. What kind of answer can't you ever be sure you have? The way I read it: the cup's position at any moment is the current guess, the answer is where it ends up, and nobody watching can tell when it's done moving. Yuri Ershov, a logician in Novosibirsk, sorted those in 1968 by how many changes they're allowed. That's how Rod Downey, Carl Jockusch, Timothy McNicholl and Paul Schupp describe his ladder in 2014; I haven't read Ershov's paper. Allow one change, from no to yes, and you get exactly the sets a program can list, the bottom rung. That one you can check yourself. If a program lists the set, guess no for every number and switch to yes when the number shows up on the list. Going back, if every guess flips at most once, from no to yes, run all the guessing side by side and write a number down the moment its guess says yes.

Now put Putnam's worry on the cup, because it lands. On the bottom rung, a yes is final the moment it shows up, and a no never is, since down means ``not yet.'' A single cup can establish that something happened, and can never establish that it didn't. A final no would take a second cup, turned by finding a reason, and that's the two cups again. That's what the bottom rung costs. This book stays on the bottom rung.

The ladder doesn't stop at endless time either. A definition that only ever adds, stage after stage, and keeps everything at each limit, is a cup run through the infinite; Wayne Richter and Peter Aczel set those out in 1974. Let it take things back and keep only what stays put from some stage on, and you have what John Burgess, in 1986, called quasi-inductive definitions, which he met in theories of truth that grew up beside Saul Kripke's 1975 theory of truth built up in stages. And Joel Hamkins, the same set theorist as in chapter one, and Andy Lewis let a Turing machine keep running past endless time in 2000. At each limit, a square keeps its value if it settled, and reads 1 if it kept flipping forever. Their favourite trick is the toggle: flash a flag every time a guess changes, and at the limit the flag is on exactly when the guess changed endlessly often. Past infinity, the toggle is the clever one, because a cup can only tell you a thing happened at least once. I threw the toggle out anyway, and I'd do it again, because before the limit its reading can't be leaned on, and nobody using a real machine gets to wait for a limit.

One finite-time fact belongs beside that, so nobody reads too much into permanence. Hao Wang, in 1957, described a machine whose only write is to mark a square and which can never erase one, and it computes everything a Turing machine does. Permanence of a machine's own memory costs it no computing power, given an endless tape: each mark goes on a square nobody marked before, which is fresh paper again. What this book prices is a box raised by a step that has to work whether or not the box is already up. So the cost is in what a box can claim, not in what the machine can compute.

\subsection{One shape, two choices nobody forces}

``There's only one way to carry a record'' packs three different claims into one sentence, so take them apart.

Given the toll and a fixed price list, is there only one way to keep the books? Yes, on every state you can reach: that's chapter eleven's one honest count, and it assumes the price list.

Is every box over a base the same machine, charges and all? No, and the counterexample is embarrassingly simple. Take any box over any base, and give it a new tally that adds each step's old cost plus one more, whatever the step is. Walk the five rules. The box still answers only to the base and itself. The tally still only goes up. The turn still costs at least one; the toll is a floor and a surcharge only raises it. The box still stays up, and the same run still turns it. So the surcharged machine passes, and it isn't the one you started with. Do it with the cup and the die: charge one for every roll, six or not. The cup turns on the same roll as before. You've changed the cost and nothing about the record. A machine that charges for running time passes, and it isn't the one that doesn't.

And the second choice nobody forces, the one that still surprises me: which event is the record? Nothing in the five rules picks it, beyond the demand that some run reach it. You saw that above: for any event the base can reach, the cup on that event, priced at one, passes. Do it with the die. One cup turns on the first six. Another turns on the first one. Both pass. Roll a one first, and until a six shows up, the two cups disagree about whether anything has happened. So which permanent box counts as ``the record'' is a second thing nobody forces on you. The price list is one free choice and the event is the other, and that second one is what a later chapter is about: what, if anything, makes ``a check passed'' the event worth recording.

Now let the cup wobble. Say whatever turns it over sometimes does and sometimes doesn't, with chances you could write on a card. Does fixing the price list fix the chances? No: two cups with the same possible moves and the same charges, one turning with a fair coin and one with a two-to-one coin, pass the same rules and aren't the same. And a cup that, from down, can either stay down or go up, depending on chance, isn't a cup on any one event at all.

And is there one set of states, up to renaming, that every box machine has to have? No, because the bigger machine can carry any hidden stuff it likes.

\subsection{What leaving the base open buys}

So here's the answer to the suspicion I started with. The box isn't special pleading for one board. Over any machine that steps, a box that answers to the machine and never comes down is a cup on some event, and the argument doesn't care what the machine is. What a particular machine adds on top of the cup is a price list and an event, and both are choices. The surcharged cup shows the first: the price can change and the record doesn't notice. The two cups on one die show the second: the rules are happy with either event. That's also why the chairs in chapter two never needed the word ``check'': they force two arrows to meet in whatever stays.

And chapter five already showed what happens when a box has more than two positions: it's a row of cups, one for each line, each one a cup like this one, where each cup's event may look at where the row already is.

%===========================================================================
\section{The machine with the record dropped}
\label{sec:dropped}
%===========================================================================

Chapter seven put the machine behind a window and asked what gets through. Two states can give the same view and differ in what matters, and a price worked out from the window can't be exact if there's a pair like that. That was for one box. Now, with a whole row of cups from chapter five and the cup over any machine from chapter fourteen, I can ask the sharper version, which is also the one I care about. Take the ordinary machine, the one with no cups and no tally, and look at what's missing from it. What's the shape of everything it can't see, and what does the toll pay for, in terms of that shape?

\subsection{What's left when you cover the cups}

Back in chapter four you covered a coin with your hand and watched the rest of the machine run, move for move, as if the coin weren't there. Do that with everything: cover the whole row of cups and the tally. What's left is a machine that steps the same way and knows nothing about either.

That only works if covering them doesn't change how the rest moves. If some step of the machine looks at a cup and goes one way or the other because of it, then covering the cups leaves you with a machine that can't decide what to do next. So the honest condition is: nothing in how the rest of the machine moves depends on the cups or the tally. Where that holds, call what's left the uncovered machine. It's the ordinary computer.

Over any one state of the uncovered machine there's a pile: all the full states that look like that one once you cover the cups, differing only in their cups and their tally. That's chapter seven's pile behind one picture, with the window being ``everything but the cups.''

Every move has piles of its own too: the states that move sends to the same landing place. Call those its landing piles. A pile behind a picture is everything that looks the same before anybody moves. A landing pile is everything that ends up in the same place after one move. Keep the two kinds apart, because this chapter turns on how they meet.

\subsection{Pricing through a window, with a whole row}

First the window question again, with a row of cups. Say a clerk prices each step from what comes through a window before and after it. The price never misses if it charges at least one for every step that turns up a cup. It's exact if it also charges nothing for every step that turns up no cup. A clash is two steps that look the same through the window, where one turned up a cup and the other didn't.

With one box, the fix in chapter seven was to put the box in the window. So you might guess, for a row, that the window has to show some of the cups. Try the smallest case. Let the record be a count that climbs: 0, 1, 2, and so on, with a cup for ``at least 1,'' a cup for ``at least 2,'' and so on. Let the window show only the first cup, ``at least 1.'' Let the count go up on some steps and sit still on others. Once it's past one, that first cup is up before and after both kinds of step. So a step that turned up a later cup looks exactly like a step that turned up nothing. That's a clash.

So, you might guess next, the window has to show every cup. That's wrong too, from the other side. Same counting record, with two moves: one adds one, the other leaves it alone. Now let the window show only whether the count is even. You can't read the count off that window: 0 and 2 look the same through it. But look at when evenness changes. It changes on exactly the steps that add one, and on no other step. So ``charge one exactly when evenness changed'' is exact, every time.

That changed how I think about windows. A window doesn't have to show the cups to price them. It has to show them move.

The rest of the clerk's story carries over from chapter seven unchanged. If the window has a clash, no price from it is exact, and any price that never misses has to overcharge at least once for every step that turns up nothing but looks like one that does. That's the least it can overcharge, and there's a price that overcharges exactly that much. If the window has no clash, an exact price exists, and on a machine with finitely many states you can work it out.

\subsection{What a paid move destroys}

Now the piles. Chapter two showed that on a finite machine, a move that settles something for good makes two arrows meet somewhere. Chapter five showed the same for a step that turns up any cup, once cups only go up. Which two arrows? It matters a great deal.

If the two states that meet sit in different piles, the uncovered machine lost a distinction too: two of its own states became one. If they sit in the same pile, the uncovered machine lost nothing. What got crushed was only ever under your hand: two states that looked the same once the cups were covered, told apart only by their cups or their tally, now made one.

You might guess it's always the second kind, because the cups are where everything happens. It isn't always. It depends on whether the uncovered machine forgets anything on its own.

Here's the case where it's the second kind. Say the uncovered machine, for this move, never lands two of its own states on one. Two full states meet. Cover the cups, and their uncovered parts land in the same place too. But the uncovered machine keeps its states apart, so those two uncovered parts must have been the same to begin with. So the two states that met sat in the same pile. In that case every landing pile of the move lies inside one pile behind one picture.

And here's the case where it isn't. Take the stamp from chapter four, and let the window show the box itself, so each state is its own picture. The stamp sends NO and YES to YES. Two states meet, and they sit in different piles, because the uncovered machine is the stamp itself and the stamp merges. Nobody ever had to cover anything.

One more fact about landing piles, and it ties back to chapter three. The halving rule said each unit of cost pays for at most one halving. That holds exactly when no landing pile of a move has more than two to the power of its price in it: a cost of one allows piles of two, a cost of two allows piles of four. And that holds on a machine of any size.

So on a machine with finitely many states, a record that only grows, and an uncovered machine that forgets nothing, the toll is a price on crushing a pile: on taking two states that look the same once the cups are covered, told apart only by what's under your hand, and making them one.

\subsection{What's left over is the ordinary computer}

Put that together and you get the shape I've been circling since the night with the Flatland video that the afterword tells about.

An ordinary machine, read through anything its moves never raise, is what's left when you cover the cups of a machine that has them. Chapter four fitted the first cup. Over every state of the ordinary machine there's a whole pile, states that differ only in their cups and tally, and the ordinary machine can't tell them apart, as long as none of its steps looks at the cups. On a machine that obeys the toll, a step that turns up a cup costs at least one, whichever cup and however many. On a finite machine whose record only grows, when the ordinary machine forgets nothing, what that cost pays for is exactly crushing two states of one pile into one. A window can charge the right price without showing the cups, if it shows them move. And with the halving rule, the cost in halvings is never less than what got crushed.

What I haven't shown is that any of this is out there in the world, that nature keeps a direction like this over its own uncovered machines. That's a belief of mine, and nothing in this book proves it. What I have shown, and you can check, comes with its conditions showing. A machine whose box answers to the computation and climbs an order is a row of cups, one cup for each line. If it also has finitely many states, never lets its record come down, and prices two arrows meeting, it has to pay this way. And when its uncovered machine forgets nothing, what it pays for is exactly this. It's less than the Flatland night promised me. It's also the part of it that held when I pushed.

\subsection{Shortcuts}

A box is worth turning up because something later can glance at it and not have to work the thing out again, the way chapter ten's glance reads a block's bucket off a table without applying the rule from scratch. Call any move like that a shortcut, and call it sound when it gives the same answer the long way would have.

But everything in this chapter covered the box, and it could only do that because nothing in the machine read it. A machine that reads its box to take a shortcut isn't the machine this chapter studied: cover the box and the steps change. So what happens when the machine actually reads its box? That's the next chapter.

%===========================================================================
\section{Reading the box}
\label{sec:reading-box}
%===========================================================================

The last chapter ended on a sentence I didn't follow up: a box is worth turning up because something later can glance at it and not have to work the thing out again. Every chapter so far kept the box as a passenger. Chapter twelve said nothing in the bare machine makes a later step read it. Chapter fifteen asked, as its condition, that nothing in how the rest of the machine moves depends on the cups. Under that condition, cover the box and you get the ordinary computer back, step for step.

That's a fair way to study what a box costs. It's a strange way to leave it, because it means the machine pays to settle something and then never uses it. So in this chapter the machine reads its box. Here's what that buys, what it costs, and how chapter two walks back into the room the moment you try it.

\subsection{The shortcut}

Picture one piece of data, a number that can be changed, and a question to answer about it. There's a long way to answer: a thousand steps, right every time. There's a shortcut: one step, but right only when the number has some property, say that it's even. And there's a check for that property, and a box. The box goes up only right after the check passes, which is chapter eight's established record. Now give the machine one rule: the shortcut may run only when the box is up.

What happens when the number changes? The box was about the old number. So give the number an edition: every write moves it to the next edition, and the box is about one edition, the one that was checked. The shortcut may run only when the box for the current edition is up.

From a clean start, does the shortcut ever give a wrong answer? Walk it. The shortcut runs, so the box for the current edition is up. That box went up only right after a check passed on that edition. Nothing has written to the number since, because any write would have moved it to a new edition. So the number is the one that passed, the property still holds, and the shortcut is right. That's chapter eight's four conditions, doing a job: same property, same thing, same edition, honest check.

And look at what it does to covering the box. Cover it with your hand and you no longer get the same run step for step, because the machine with the box takes the one-step road where the machine without it takes the thousand-step one. You get the same answers, by a different number of steps. So this isn't chapter fifteen's machine. The box has stopped being a passenger.

It computes nothing new. Every answer it gives, the long way gives too. What it saves is steps, the way a cache saves them, and caches are old. What I want to show you is what keeping the shortcut honest costs, because it's chapter two's bill again, in four different currencies.

\subsection{Four doors}

The weak spot is the edition. It's a counter, and on a finite machine a counter can't climb forever. So what do you do? There are four doors, and I'll walk through each one with the machine that walks through it, the way chapter three did.

The first door: let the editions wrap around. Say there are two editions, 0 and 1, and after 1 comes 0 again. Four moves are enough to break it. Check the number 0: it's even, so the box for edition 0 goes up. Write 1: edition 1. Write 3: edition 0 again. Now ask. The box for edition 0 is still up, so the shortcut runs on 3, which is odd, and gives the wrong answer. The box was about the first edition 0, and the shortcut is looking at the second. That's chapter twelve's coin that moved, with a bad coin. And it's chapter eleven's lesson too: a counter that goes back down gets leaned on at exactly the place where it goes down.

Programmers know this one. IBM's manual for its System/370 computers, in the edition of 1974 as revised in 1975, warns about it in a note on an instruction called Compare Double and Swap. One program reads the head of a list; meanwhile another program ``removes the first two messages and then adds the first message back into the chain,'' and the first program, on continuing, ``is not aware that the list is changed.'' The fix in the note is a change number in the word next door, ``incremented for each modification of the list,'' which brings the risk down ``to a negligible level'': it can go wrong only if the first program is held up while a whole multiple of 2 to the 32nd changes go by. That's the first door with a very big wheel. R. Kent Treiber, at IBM, drew the same accident in a 1986 report and fixed it with what he called an allocation counter. The name came later. The earliest I can find is Maged Michael and Michael Scott's, in reports dated December 1995 and their 1996 paper: ``the ABA problem,'' a value that goes from A to B and back to A, so a check that only looks at the value is fooled.

The second door: never reuse an edition. Each edition gets its own box, and a write moves to an edition that's never been used. Every box stays up for good, so this is still a Thiele machine, and the shortcut is never wrong. But it pays in chapter two's second column: fresh paper, a new edition on every write. On a finite machine that's a budget. Once the editions run out, the honest move is a mark that goes up for good and says ``spent,'' and from then on the machine takes the long way. That's chapter eighteen's bank of fuses, one blown per update, with a design rule to make sure there are enough.

The third door: keep one box and knock it down on every write. That's a cache with an invalidate, and the shortcut is never wrong. But the box comes down, so it isn't a Thiele record any more; it's chapter fourteen's toggle. And it doesn't get out of chapter two either. Make the write as gentle as you like, even one that just flips the number between 0 and 1, which on the number alone is one-to-one. Knocking the box down still sends the number with its box up and the same number with its box down to the same place: two arrows meet. That's chapter six's WIPE. And the passing check that put the box up merged two states too: the number with its box down and the same number with its box already up both land on box up. The toggle pays twice.

The fourth door: don't tie the box to the edition at all. Tie it to the value that was checked. ``6 is even'' never goes stale. Write a different number, and the box for 6 is still true, because it's about 6, not about whatever's in the slot. Come back to 6 later and the box is waiting. No editions, nothing wraps, nothing gets knocked down, and every box stays up for good, so it's a Thiele machine and the shortcut is never wrong. It pays in room: one box for every different value that ever passed. That's chapter twenty-one's count. When the values are few, it's few boxes.

This door has an inventor. Donald Michie, at the University of Edinburgh, wrote in Nature in April 1968 about what he called memo functions. Give a function two parts, he said, a ``rule part,'' the procedure that works the answer out, and a ``rote part,'' a look-up table, and let the machine use whichever suits the moment, so that ``each evaluation by rule adds a fresh entry to the rote.'' The word programmers use now, memoization, grew out of his; it isn't in the paper. His rote is a box tied to the value: once an input has been worked out, it never needs working out again.

\subsection{Running all four}

I ran all four on the same twenty thousand programs, forty moves each, on a number from 0 to 5, with the long way at a thousand steps and the shortcut at one. The doors that never reuse an edition get four editions to spend, the same room the first door wraps around in.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.38\linewidth}rr>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Door & Wrong answers & Average steps & A Thiele machine? \\
\midrule
No shortcut at all, the long way every time & 0 & 25{,}992 & \\
1. Box stays up, editions wrap around & 37{,}564 & 17{,}027 & yes, and wrong \\
2. Box stays up, editions never reused (four to spend) & 0 & 22{,}529 & yes \\
3. One box, knocked down by every write & 0 & 20{,}082 & no: a toggle \\
4. Box tied to the value checked & 0 & 17{,}997 & yes \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\footnotesize Twenty thousand programs of forty moves each, on a number from 0 to 5; the long
way costs a thousand steps and the shortcut one. Wrong answers are counted over all of them.
\end{center}

The first door is the fastest, and it's fast because it's wrong. The other three are never wrong. On these small numbers the fourth door saves the most, and never needed more than three boxes, because only 0, 2 and 4 are even.

\subsection{Why the other doors exist}

If the fourth door is that good, why doesn't everyone always tie the box to the value? Ask chapter eight's warehouse question. In the run above, the data is one small number, and finding its box takes one look. Make the data chapter eight's list of a thousand numbers. A box tied to the value of the whole list can only be found by reading the whole list, to know which list you're holding. Reading a thousand numbers takes about as long as checking whether they're sorted, which was the check you wanted to skip.

So on big data the fourth door has three ways forward, and each one is a bill you've already seen. Tie the box to a short fingerprint of the list instead, and two different lists can share a fingerprint and a box; that's chapter eighteen's wall, scrambling functions that make that hard but not impossible, and when it happens it's the first door's wrong answer again. Or never change a list at all: keep exactly one copy of each different list and hand around a pointer to it, so the box can sit on the pointer and be found in one glance; that's fresh memory for every new list. Or go back to the second or third door, put the box on the container instead of the value, and let a write move the edition or knock the box down, so a later step can find it in one glance.

Keeping one copy of each value is old too. Andrei Ershov, in a paper printed in Russian in 1958 and translated into English the same year in the first volume of the Communications of the ACM, gave a program that writes programs a way never to work out the same expression twice. It scrambled each instruction into a number that said where to look, checked whether that place already held the same instruction, and reused it if so, in time that grows with the number of instructions instead of its square. Donald Knuth credits him, along with Gene Amdahl, as an independent discoverer of that kind of table. In 1974 Eiichi Goto, at the University of Tokyo, built the idea into a version of Lisp, from a proposal he'd made with three colleagues the year before, and called it monocopy: no two copies of the same structure, so that checking whether two are equal becomes checking whether they're the same one. (This is Andrei Ershov, the programmer. The Ershov whose ladder came up in chapter fourteen is Yuri, a logician.)

\subsection{What the doors say}

Every time a box goes up, it pays the toll, because on a finite machine that's two arrows meeting, and that's chapter two and chapter three. That part was already in the book. What this chapter adds is the second bill. Keeping a shortcut honest after the data changes costs one more thing, and there are exactly four ways to pay it: fresh editions, which is fresh paper or a fuse budget; a box that comes down, which leaves the definition and merges at the wipe; room for every value checked; or nothing, and wrong answers.

None of the four is new. Programmers have used all of them for decades. What's here is that they're four ways of paying chapter two's bill. I haven't found a fifth, and here's why I don't expect one. When the shortcut runs, the machine has to know that the thing in front of it passed the check. Either the box names the thing outright, which is the fourth door, or the box sits on a place and has to know that nothing changed in that place since the check. Knowing that takes a mark that changes when the place does. A mark that only ever moves to new values is the second door, and on a finite machine it runs out or wraps around into the first. A mark that moves back is a box that comes down, the third. That's an argument, not a proof that nothing else could work, and if you find a fifth door I'd like to see it.

And it leaves one question hanging, the one every machine with a record runs into once its programs can be handed their own code. These shortcuts were handed to the machine: I wrote the property, the check and the shortcut. Can a machine find its own shortcuts, and decide from the inside whether one is sound? That question runs into a wall, the same wall Kurt Gödel and Alan Turing hit, and I have to show you the wall is there before anything pretends to be on the other side of it. That's the next chapter.

%===========================================================================
\section{What a machine can't decide about itself}
\label{sec:diagonal}
%===========================================================================

In chapter ten I said you'd watch the same move get made again and again: somebody says ``everything is X,'' it's true enough to do real work with, and then somebody draws the diagonal. I owe you the diagonal itself now. The last few chapters handed a box to any machine that steps, and the next thing anyone wants from a machine with a box is for it to tell sound shortcuts from unsound ones by itself. Before I let anything claim that, I have to show you the wall every such claim runs into, and then exactly where a machine with a box stands next to it.

It doesn't stand on the far side, because nobody's on the far side. A box keeps a distinction and pays for it. What it can't do is the judging.

\subsection{Five names and a table}

Do this first, with a pencil, because the whole family of arguments is this one trick.

Write five friends' names down the left of a page. Next to each name, write a row of five digits, each 0 or 1, anything you like. Now build a new row. Its first digit is the opposite of the first digit of the first row. Its second digit is the opposite of the second digit of the second row. And so on, down the diagonal. Look at what you've made. It differs from row one in place one, from row two in place two, from every row at that row's own place. So it's nobody's row. Your table of five missed something.

With five rows that's a cute fact. Georg Cantor made the point in 1891 that it works for any list, however long, even an endless one: hand me a complete list of all endless rows of two symbols, and I'll walk the diagonal flipping as I go and hand you back a row that's on no line of your list. So there's no complete list. There are more rows like that than there are places on any list, and that's where ``some infinities are bigger than others'' comes from. (He wrote his two symbols as m and w, and that paper wasn't even his first proof of the fact. The first was in 1874, by a harder road, and in 1891 he called the new one much simpler. The diagonal was him finding the easy way.)

Now look at what the argument did, because it's going to happen again, in new clothes. There was a table: rows are the things being listed, columns are questions, and each square says ``does row i do column j.'' There was the diagonal: the squares where a thing gets asked about itself. There was a flip, something with no fixed point, like turning yes into no, applied along the diagonal. And what got knocked down was the thing assumed at the start: the complete list. Nothing outside the assumption was touched. A table, a diagonal, a flip, and the assumption eating itself.

\subsection{The same move, again and again}

Russell, in 1902, from chapter ten: the collection of all collections that don't contain themselves. That's Cantor's flipped diagonal with ``contains'' as the table, and Russell said as much in 1903: he got there by running Cantor's argument on a supposed collection of everything. What got knocked down was the assumed collection of everything, and Zermelo's fix was to stop assuming it.

Jules Richard, a French mathematician who taught at schools in Tours, Dijon and Châteauroux, noticed in a short note in 1905 that some phrases define numbers (``the ratio of a circle's circumference to its diameter''). There are only as many phrases as there are whole numbers, so list the numbers they define, run Cantor's diagonal down their decimal digits, and you get a number no phrase defines, except that you just defined it, in the phrase that describes the diagonal. The lesson I take from it is the one this whole chapter turns on: keep apart the language a system talks in and the language you use to talk about it. Mix them and the diagonal bites you.

Richard's paradox has a cousin small enough to do at the table, and it comes with a footnote I can't resist. Russell, the first to put it in print, in 1906, credited it to G. G. Berry, a junior librarian at the Bodleian Library in Oxford. In the version in Principia Mathematica it's about syllables. Take the phrase ``the least integer not nameable in fewer than nineteen syllables.'' There are only so many phrases shorter than nineteen syllables, so they name only so many numbers, so some least number is left over, and the phrase picks it out. Now count the phrase: the, least, in-te-ger, not, name-a-ble, in, few-er, than, nine-teen, syl-la-bles. Eighteen. In eighteen syllables it has named the number that can't be named in fewer than nineteen. ``Nameable'' is a word about the language, used inside the language, and that's where the bite is.

Kurt Gödel, 1931, with an improvement in 1936 by Barkley Rosser, a student of Alonzo Church, that lets it assume only that the system doesn't contradict itself. Take any such formal system, with rules you can check mechanically, strong enough to do some arithmetic. Then there are sentences it can neither prove nor disprove. The engine lets you build, for any property you can write down, a sentence the system proves equivalent to ``my own code number has that property.'' Apply it to ``not provable,'' and you get a sentence equivalent, inside the system, to its own unprovability. The table is the sentences listed by code number, the diagonal is a sentence about its own code, and the flip turns provable into not provable. Two cautions, which the Stanford Encyclopedia stresses too: the sentence doesn't literally ``say'' anything about itself; it's provably equivalent to a claim about its code, which is less mystical and more exact. And the theorem doesn't say the sentence can't be known. It says this one system can't settle it.

How did the world take it? Mostly, at first, it didn't. Gödel, twenty-four years old, announced the first theorem in a round-table discussion on the last day of a conference in Königsberg in September 1930, and by the account of Solomon Feferman, one of the editors of Gödel's collected works, the room met it with general incomprehension, except for one man. John von Neumann pulled Gödel aside, kept thinking about it, and a couple of months later saw that the argument gave something more: a system like that can't prove it doesn't contradict itself. He wrote to Gödel with it in a letter dated 20 November 1930. Gödel had already got there; his paper had reached the journal three days earlier with that second theorem announced in it, and von Neumann left the credit with Gödel. It's ``somebody got there first'' again, except this time the person who got there second was von Neumann.

Alan Turing, 1936. Chapter one told how what he actually proved differs from the halting problem as it's taught. The taught version fits on two index cards. Card A is the Checker: hand it any program card and it announces ``stops'' or ``runs forever.'' Card B is the Contrarian: ``Hand me to the Checker. If it says I stop, run forever. If it says I run forever, stop.'' Hand B to A, and either answer is wrong. So there's no Checker. The table is programs against programs, the diagonal is a program run on itself, and the flip turns stop into loop.

Stephen Kleene, 1938, and this is the one that turns the paradox into a tool, so slow down. Everything above needs the thing that refers to itself to exist: Gödel's sentence has to be buildable; the Contrarian has to be able to ``hand me to the Checker.'' Kleene's recursion theorem says: for any way of transforming programs that you can compute, there's a program that behaves exactly as if it had been handed its own source code. Any program may be written as though it already had a copy of itself in hand. He stated it almost in passing, in a paper about something else, and proved it in the last two lines of one section. I have that count from Yiannis Moschovakis, a logician who wrote a 2010 paper about it called ``Kleene's Amazing Second Recursion Theorem,'' and he calls it a little gem. It's why programs that print their own source code exist in every ordinary programming language. Try one in English: ``Print the following twice, the second time in quotes: 'Print the following twice, the second time in quotes:''' Obey it.

Those programs are called quines, and the name comes from Douglas Hofstadter in 1979, in Gödel, Escher, Bach, in honour of the philosopher Willard Van Orman Quine, who had made a long study of self-reference that works indirectly. Quine left a sentence you can take apart at the table, from a 1962 essay on paradox: ``yields falsehood when preceded by its quotation'' yields falsehood when preceded by its quotation. Do what it says. Take the part in quotation marks, put a quoted copy of it in front of itself, and look: it's the whole sentence, word for word. So the sentence says that it itself is false.
