It's the liar paradox, built without any ``this sentence,'' just an instruction to quote and copy. Quoting and copying is Kleene's trick in plain English. The paradox is what you get when the copy says something about its own truth. The tool is what you get when the copy just does something.

Henry Rice, 1953, whom you met in chapter seven: the general form. Any question about what a program does, not how it's written, that some programs pass and some fail, has no program that answers it for all programs. ``Does this program stop'' is one. ``Does this program ever print 7'' is another. ``Does this program contain the word 'if''' isn't, because that's about the text.

And the one that's an attack. Ken Thompson, who built the original Unix operating system, gave a lecture published in 1984. Build a compiler that, when it compiles the login program, plants a back door, and when it compiles a compiler, plants both habits in that one too. Then delete the trick from the source code. The compiled compiler still carries it, and recompiling a clean source with the poisoned compiler plants it again, forever. Reading the source never shows it. Picture a locksmith who teaches an apprentice to cut a spare for every town-hall key, and to teach that to every apprentice they train, and never to write it in the manual. Years later the manual is spotless and every locksmith in town cuts the spare. Thompson's compiler is Kleene's trick with a knife in it.

Thompson wasn't first either, and he said so: he wrote that he first read of the possibility in an Air Force review of the security of an early operating system called Multics, and asked anyone who could find it to send him the reference. It's Paul Karger and Roger Schell's 1974 security evaluation of Multics, which already described a trap door planted in a compiler that keeps itself alive. Is there any way out? A partial one. David A. Wheeler, in 2005 and in his 2009 dissertation, worked out a check: compile the suspect compiler's source with a second, independently made compiler, use the result to compile that source again, and compare it bit for bit with the suspect. If they match, under his stated conditions, the source and the binary correspond. Two compilers having different authors is useful evidence, not a substitute for those conditions. The question has moved to a place where we can name what we trust.

And Bill Lawvere, in 1969, proved a theorem that, as I read it, makes all of these one theorem in disguise: if there's a function with no fixed point, a flip, then no thing can list every question about itself. Cantor, Russell, Gödel and Turing all come out as cases. Noson Yanofsky retold it in 2003 without the heavy machinery. I mention it because ``it's the same wall'' is a claim I make below, and it's fair at the level of the argument's shape. Every case has its own fine print about who counts as a judge, and that fine print is exactly where most overclaims live.

\subsection{Where the diagonal gets misused}

Before pointing it at a machine with a box, look at how it gets misused. Each time, the trouble is forgetting who the judge is and what it has to judge.

``Gödel shows minds aren't machines.'' John Lucas, a philosopher at Oxford, argued it in 1961. Roger Penrose, the Oxford mathematical physicist, revived it in two books, The Emperor's New Mind in 1989 and Shadows of the Mind in 1994. The standard replies, as far as I can tell, have held up: the Gödel sentence is true only if the system doesn't contradict itself, so a person who ``sees'' it's true has to know that, which is the hard part; and nobody can build the Gödel sentence of a system they can't write down, so people might be machines too complicated to diagonalise. The theorem has an ``if'' in it, and the argument drops the ``if.''

The part that's easy to miss is that Gödel himself didn't go as far as Lucas. In a lecture on 26 December 1951, at a meeting of the American Mathematical Society at Brown University, he drew only an either-or from his own theorems: either the human mind infinitely surpasses any finite machine, even in pure mathematics, or there are questions about whole numbers, of a certain simple kind, that no proof a human can conceive will ever settle. Feferman, writing about that lecture, says Gödel privately believed the first half and still stated only the either-or, because he didn't have a knock-down argument against the machine side. Gödel meant to publish the lecture and never did. So the man whose theorem gets quoted for ``minds aren't machines'' stated an either-or and left the deciding to whoever came next. The ``if'' is there again, kept by the person with the most right to drop it.

``Undecidable means unknowable.'' No. Two senses share the word: no single program that's right on every case, and a sentence one particular system can't settle. Neither means nobody can know a given answer. Any single case is easy in the first sense: for any one program, one of ``say yes'' and ``say no'' is right. What's ruled out is one inside procedure that's right on every case.

``Everything interesting is undecidable.'' No. Rice is about what programs do, over all programs. It says nothing against checking the text, nothing against answering for particular programs, nothing against checks allowed to say ``I don't know,'' nothing against checking finite machines. Real compilers and analysers live with Rice by being careful and incomplete, and what limits them in practice is mostly cost.

``Self-reference is a paradox.'' It's a theorem with a construction, as Kleene, the quines and Thompson show.

\subsection{Now point it at the box}

Here's the question with nothing in it but what this book has built. A machine steps. A box rides along and turns up on an event, chapter fourteen. When the event is ``a check passed,'' chapter eight says the box lets you lean on a claim, and for a claim that a shortcut is sound, that's what makes the shortcut usable. So: can a machine like that have, as one of its own programs, a procedure that correctly decides, for every program, ``does this program have a sound shortcut''? Call that an autopilot: hand it any program and it says yes or no, and it's always right.

The exact claim is narrower than that question in plain words, so here it is first. ``Has a shortcut'' can be any question about programs with two features: two programs that behave the same always get the same answer, and there's one program that passes and one that fails. That's all the argument gets to look at. It doesn't see soundness. Whether a particular question about shortcuts has those two features is a separate fact, checked one machine at a time. A procedure that only answers for some programs, or needs outside evidence, isn't what the wall rules out.

And because the argument only looks at what programs do, not how, ``shortcut'' here is a label. A question like ``is there a cheaper program that gives the same answers?'' isn't the argument's question. The nearest thing I know of is Manuel Blum's speedup theorem from 1967, and it goes somewhere stranger: there are functions with no fastest program at all, where every program for them has another that's faster on all but finitely many inputs. That's about cost, not deciding, and nothing in this book proves anything there.

The answer is no, under conditions, and the proof is the two index cards. Suppose there were an autopilot that says yes exactly when a program has a shortcut. Build the flip: ``If the autopilot says yes about me, behave like the program that fails; if it says no, behave like the one that passes.'' If the machine has Kleene's trick, so a program can be handed its own code, and if that flip is something the machine can run, and if the question only depends on what programs do, and if there's one program each way, then there's a program that behaves exactly like its own flip. Ask the autopilot about it. If it says yes, the program behaves like the one that fails, and behaving alike means getting the same answer, so it fails: the autopilot was wrong. If it says no, the program behaves like the one that passes, so it passes: wrong again. Those are the only two answers.

Look at what that used: Kleene's trick, the flip being runnable, the two examples, and ``behaves alike, answers alike.'' It never touched the tally, the toll, or the box. It's Rice's trick one floor over, with its own conditions, so it isn't Rice's theorem word for word, and it says nothing about whether this question is harder or easier than the halting problem.

So the wall is there. The honest part is who counts as the judge. The argument rules out a judge whose flip the machine itself can run. It says nothing about a judge standing outside. An outside judge can exist for the very same question, right on every program. The line between inside and outside is Richard's lesson: the language you talk about a system in isn't the language it talks in.

\subsection{What this does to the book}

It does two things. It keeps me honest: nothing in this book claims a machine with a box can check its own shortcuts on autopilot, because that's a wall, the same wall, and I've shown you the bricks. And it tells you what a box can do instead: it can carry a shortcut along with its receipt, so that ``was this earned?'' is answered by a step you can point at. No judge has to exist for that, which is lucky, because on a machine with Kleene's trick no inside one can. The diagonal says the judging can't be automated from inside. The cup says the commitment can be recorded. Different jobs, and a box does the second.

The diagonal has a famous cousin that looks like it ought to bite a box too. Gödel's second theorem says a system strong enough for arithmetic that doesn't contradict itself can't prove that it doesn't, and Martin Löb sharpened that in 1955: if such a system proves ``if this is provable, it's true,'' it already proves the thing itself. So can a box vouch for itself, and does Löb stop it?

You might guess yes, since a box looks like a system talking about itself. It lands somewhere else, and mostly it's a relief. Hold two things side by side: ``there's a receipt for this somewhere,'' and the receipt in your hand. Löb's theorem is about provability, the bare claim that some proof exists somewhere. A raised box isn't the receipt in your hand either. From a clean start, chapter eight says a check passed before the raise. The box doesn't say which claim, and it doesn't hold a proof of it. Sergei Artemov's line from chapter eight, between ``there's a proof'' and ``this is the proof,'' is useful here, but a box doesn't turn one into the other. The trust in this book comes from a proof about the checker, the clean start and every move, made outside the machine. It isn't a proof inside the machine that the machine is sound, so it doesn't get around Gödel or Löb.

Push on that and it gives, because a receipt is only as good as whoever wrote it. Its trust rests on the checker meaning what it says, and that gets settled once, from outside. That's where I chose to stop this machine's chain of reasons. Eliezer Yudkowsky and Marcello Herreshoff's 2013 draft on programs that trust their own rewrites describes a related problem they call the Löbian obstacle: how a program can justify trusting its successor's proofs. It's a cousin of this question, it doesn't settle it, and it doesn't make my design the only possible one. So I didn't get rid of the receipt somewhere. I moved it out of the machine and handed it to whoever reads the record.

One more thing follows from the same picture, and it's short enough to check by hand. Can a box rest on itself, the way the sentence ``this sentence is certified'' would like to? Not if it's established and permanent. The check behind the raise happened while the box was still down, because the box goes up only after that check passes. And once up it never comes down, so it goes up only once. So no claim whose truth needs the box already up can be the claim the box went up on. Saul Kripke's 1975 theory of truth calls a sentence that only ever leans on itself ungrounded, and established boxes are grounded in his sense: every one bottoms out in a check that didn't lean on it. Caspar Oesterheld's 2019 work on programs that cooperate by simulating each other needed a grounding step of the same kind to stop an endless regress.

\subsection{Two walls}

One last distinction, because the two are easy to run together, and running them together is the easiest way to misread this book.

There's a second wall, nothing to do with the diagonal. It's chapter seven's: two states that differ in their box can give the same view through a window that doesn't let the box through, and then no reader of the window can get the box back. That wall comes from missing information. Nothing about it is undecidable. It holds for small finite things with nothing to compute. Rice isn't underneath it.

So there are two walls, on two different axes. The diagonal is about what no inside procedure can decide. The window is about what no outside watcher can see. The book needs both, and I'll try not to call one by the other's name.

%===========================================================================
\section{Five real systems}
\label{sec:five-systems}
%===========================================================================

So far there's been a toll, a counter, two kinds of wall and a cup, every one of them shown on paper, with cups and cards and dice I made up. It's time to go look at what people actually do. I picked five systems that keep records other people rely on, with real money or real security behind them, and asked each one the question from the paper: is there a price on the moment the record is written? Not on the work around it. On the write itself, the moment the mark goes on and stays.

I'll give you the answer first, because it's one sentence and it surprised me. Of the five, read through the pieces of them I copied out, one takes a fee at the moment of the write, and that one leaks at exactly the place chapter eleven said a counter leaks: where it goes back down.

``Price'' needs one meaning for all five, or the answer is whatever I want it to be. So here it is, fixed before looking. A system prices an event if it attaches one of three costs to it. A fee: something taken at the event itself, whatever happens afterwards. A bond: something put at risk by the event and lost only if some later event happens, like a contradiction. A budget: a limited allowance the event draws down, so only so many such events can ever happen. A wall isn't a price under this. Nobody pays a scrambling function; undoing one is just too hard. Neither is work a system needs and never charges for. The table at the end has a column for each of the three, and because the definition came first, what the table shows is a finding about the five, not something I arranged.

Three things are easy to blur here, so I'll keep them apart all the way through. There's the real system: the document that real money and real security run on. There's my copy: one piece of that document, copied out small enough to check by hand. And for some of the five there's a wrapper: a tiny model that takes one condition as given and says what follows. A claim checked at one level isn't a claim about the level above it. Near the end of the gas section I'll walk gas through all three, so you can see where each claim stops.

\subsection{Proof-of-stake finality: the price is on going back}

Ethereum, and chains like it, decide which blocks are final by having validators, people who've locked up money, their stake, vote on checkpoints: blocks picked out at regular numbered rounds, which they call epochs. A vote isn't for one checkpoint. It's a link from a checkpoint the validator already trusts to a later one, and every chain starts from one first block, the genesis block, which counts as trusted from the start. In the design Vitalik Buterin and Virgil Griffith published in 2017, called Casper, a checkpoint is justified when validators holding two thirds of the stake vote a link to it from a justified one, and final when it's justified and a two-thirds link runs from it to its direct child. The record is ``this checkpoint is final.''

Two rules carry the price. Don't vote for two different targets in the same round. And don't cast a vote whose span, from where it starts to where it lands, surrounds the span of another of your votes. Break either, and your deposit can be destroyed, which is called slashing. The theorem they prove, which they call accountable safety, says: if two checkpoints that contradict each other ever both become final, at least a third of the stake broke a rule and can be slashed. So the price is on going back, on making the record contradict itself. Writing the record, voting honestly to finalize, costs no stake at all. That isn't a claim that running a validator is free; it's a claim about what the deposit is taken for. In the terms above, Casper's price is a bond. The deposit stands behind every vote, the honest ones included, and it's only taken from a validator whose own votes contradict each other.

Do it with three validators of equal stake. Draw the genesis block at round 0, two competing blocks at round 1, and one child of each at round 2. Let A and B vote both links on the left branch, genesis to its first block and that block to the next. Let B and C vote both links on the right.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,every node/.style={align=center,font=\small}]
\node (g) at (0,0) {genesis\\round 0};
\node (l) at (-2,-1.8) {left\\round 1};
\node (r) at (2,-1.8) {right\\round 1};
\node (ll) at (-2,-3.5) {left child\\round 2};
\node (rr) at (2,-3.5) {right child\\round 2};
\draw[->] (g)--node[left] {A, B}(l);
\draw[->] (g)--node[right] {B, C}(r);
\draw[->] (l)--node[left] {A, B}(ll);
\draw[->] (r)--node[right] {B, C}(rr);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Each link has two of the three votes, two thirds of the stake. Both round-1 blocks are justified and each has a justified link to its child, so both are final, and they're siblings, so neither is an ancestor of the other. B voted for two different targets in round 1, so B's third of the stake can be slashed. A and C each voted one link landing in round 1 and one landing in round 2, which neither doubles nor surrounds, so they broke no rule. The picture records votes; nothing in it takes anybody's money. What it shows is that the theorem's condition can actually happen: forks like that can be built, and the theorem fires on them.

Why does the theorem hold? Walk it. Say two contradicting checkpoints are both final, one in round v and the other in round w.

If v and w are the same round, then two groups, each with two thirds of the stake, voted links landing in that round on two different blocks. Two thirds plus two thirds is four thirds, more than everything, so the two groups share at least a third. Every validator in that shared part voted two different targets in one round.

If they're different rounds, say v is earlier, look at how the later one got justified: a chain of two-thirds links climbing from genesis, every block on it an ancestor of the later checkpoint. None of those blocks is the earlier final checkpoint or comes after it, because then the later checkpoint would too, and they contradict. So that chain has to get past round v without touching the earlier checkpoint, and some link in it starts at or before v and lands after v. Three ways that can go. It lands at exactly v plus one: then it and the earlier checkpoint's finalizing link land in the same round on different blocks, and the shared third voted both, a double vote. It starts at exactly v: then its starting block is a justified block at round v that isn't the earlier checkpoint, and the two links that justified those two blocks land in round v on different blocks, a double vote again. Otherwise it starts before v and lands after v plus one, so it surrounds the earlier checkpoint's finalizing link, which runs from v to v plus one, and the shared third made a surround vote. In every case a third of the stake broke a rule.

Everything there stands on the overlap. Take away ``two groups of two thirds share a third,'' and the argument promises nothing.

The piece I copied out comes from a published proof by Runtime Verification and the University of Texas at Austin, based on Yoichi Hirai's, with that overlap taken as a condition: the copy takes it as given and doesn't do the arithmetic. It has no deposits, no rewards, and no rule for picking which branch to build on. On a chain with one validator, one vote finalizes the genesis block and nobody is slashed: that's ``writing costs no stake,'' shown in the simplest case there is.

One thing that means for the record itself: finality can be undone at a price, the price is named, and a final block can still hold a false statement.

Is the 2017 design what Ethereum actually runs? Partly, and the paper's title is the clue: Casper the Friendly Finality Gadget. Its first sentence says it's a finality system laid over a chain still choosing its blocks by proof of work, so it's a gadget, bolted on top. In 2020 a larger group, Buterin among them, wrote up Gasper, joining Casper's finality to a rule for picking branches called LMD GHOST, and on 15 September 2022, in the switch people called the Merge, Ethereum stopped choosing blocks by proof of work at all and went over to proof of stake. So the checkpoints and the two slashing rules are one part of a system that holds real money today, and my copy is of that part's safety argument, the way the 2017 design states it. Everything between the two, the branch rule and the rest of the running protocol, is the gap between my copy and the running system. None of it moves the price: finalizing still costs an honest validator no stake, and what costs is contradicting yourself.

\subsection{Gas: the one that charges a fee at the write}

Every operation on Ethereum costs gas, paid in ether by whoever sent the transaction. The yellow paper, Ethereum's formal specification, gives the reason in one line: every computation costs a fee, so the network can't be abused and questions about whether a program stops are sidestepped. The record I care about is a storage slot holding something other than zero, which every full copy of the chain in the world has to keep until it's cleared. Clearing a slot is allowed, and the refunds below exist to reward it.

Writing a fresh slot from zero to something costs twenty thousand gas, by the table in the yellow paper's appendix, plus twenty-one hundred for touching a cold slot, one the transaction hasn't touched yet. After the first touch it's warm. The point, as I read it, is to discourage storage growth, since storage grows every copy's database. That charge is a fee on the write, at the moment of the write, and it's the only fee in the five. Gas has a budget too: every block has a gas limit, and the gas its transactions use can't go over it, so only so many writes fit in a block.

Where does a number like twenty-one hundred come from? Somebody picked it, so the next question is who, and why, and the answer has a date on it. In September 2016, just as Ethereum's developers were gathering in Shanghai for Devcon 2, their developer conference (the third one, since they started counting at zero), attackers started filling blocks with an operation that was cheap in gas and expensive in fact: it sent every copy of the chain off to read data from its disk, and copies fell behind. The first repair, a change that October called Tangerine Whistle, raised the prices of the operations the attack leaned on. The cold-slot charge came later, in a proposal by Vitalik Buterin and Martin Swende called EIP-2929, switched on in the Berlin upgrade of April 2021, and the reason it gives is still those 2016 attacks. So twenty-one hundred isn't a law of nature or a measurement of anything deep. It's a scar: a price set after somebody found where the old price was wrong. That doesn't change the argument; the write is still priced at the moment of the write. It does change how I read the rest of that table. Some of its numbers, at least, are somebody's answer to ``what did this cost us last time,'' which is a record of a different kind.

Now the leak, which is chapter eleven's leak. Set a slot and clear it again in the same transaction, and you get a refund. Count it through. The first write costs twenty-two thousand one hundred. Clearing it costs a hundred and credits a refund of nineteen thousand nine hundred. Net: twenty-three hundred. Leave it set, and you pay at least twenty thousand. So the write is priced, and the write you take back nearly isn't.

Twenty-three hundred on its own misleads, though, because of the 2021 change from chapter eleven, the one that ended the refund-hoarding token's advantage: the refund a transaction actually gets is capped at a fifth of all the gas it used, and that includes the twenty-one thousand every transaction pays just for existing. Take a transaction that does nothing but set and clear the slot. Count only that base and the two stores (the steps that load their arguments add about a dozen more): it uses 43,200 gas, so the cap is 8,640, so it pays 34,560. That's 13,560 on top of the base. Look at the slot alone and forget the base, which the rule doesn't do, and you'd get 17,760. Either way it's far above twenty-three hundred.

You can check the twenty-thousand floor with a pencil. My copy follows the specification's rule for one slot in one transaction, starting from zero: a store of something that isn't zero into a slot that's still zero costs twenty thousand; any other store costs a hundred; and putting the slot back to zero gives back nineteen thousand nine hundred. Keep two lines on a sheet. ``Slot at zero: net so far at least zero.'' ``Slot not at zero: net so far at least twenty thousand.'' The fresh slot meets the first line. Now check that every store keeps whichever line fits where it lands. Zero to zero adds a hundred: still at least zero. Zero to something adds twenty thousand: from at least zero to at least twenty thousand. Something to something else adds a hundred: still at least twenty thousand. Something back to zero adds a hundred and takes off nineteen thousand nine hundred, nineteen thousand eight hundred off in all: from at least twenty thousand down to at least two hundred, still at least zero. So whatever the list of stores, if the slot ends up holding something, the net is at least twenty thousand. The cold charge comes once, on the first store, and nothing after it depends on it, so from a cold slot it's at least twenty-two thousand one hundred.

That copy covers one slot in one transaction, with the gas and refund arithmetic copied out of Ethereum's execution specification. It has no other slots, no calls between programs, and no refund cap.

The wrapper is a different kind of thing. It's a gas schedule in the abstract: any states, any steps, a cost on each step, a box that's up or down, and a rule saying which steps get charged. There's no Ethereum in it at all. It's chapter seven's clerk under a new name. Ask a schedule for two things: every run pays at least as much as the number of times it turned the box up, and no run pays more than that. Both together hold exactly when the schedule charges one on each step that turns the box up and nothing on any other. Both halves are easy to see on a one-step run. Leave one step that turns the box up uncharged, and that one-step run turns it up for free: the first fails. Charge one step that doesn't turn it up, and that one-step run pays more than the nothing it turned up: the second fails.

So walk gas through all three. Think of Ethereum as it runs as a kitchen, and my copy as a photocopy of one recipe from it. The photocopy says a set-then-clear nets twenty-three hundred, and inside the photocopy that's right. In the kitchen, the bill for that transaction is 34,560, because the kitchen has a base charge and a cap the photocopy doesn't. The wrapper isn't from that kitchen at all: it says what an exact price would have to look like, one per raise, and Ethereum's twenty thousand isn't that and isn't trying to be. A claim made with the photocopy in hand is a claim about the photocopy.

Gas prices storage and computation. It says nothing about whether what's stored is true.

\subsection{Attestation: a quote about the past}

A lot of computers carry a small security chip, a trusted platform module. It keeps registers that can only be extended: each new measurement gets scrambled together with the old value. A quote is the chip signing some of those registers along with a challenge from whoever's asking, so they can tell it's fresh, and the asker still has to check the signature, the challenge and the values they expected. That can give you a fresh report of what was measured. It doesn't tell you every important fact about the machine got measured.

Some of those registers can be reset, depending on the register and the rules of the platform. Some reset at startup; some allow resets partway through. So ``you can only extend'' describes a stretch of time with those resets left out, and a claim about an unbroken history has to say which resets it allows. Intel's SGX enclaves use their own measurements and reports for a similar purpose, but not these registers.

What does the specification price on the registers? Nothing: no fee, no bond, no budget. Forging a different measurement history that passes depends on breaking the scrambling or the trusted parts, which is a wall, not a price. An allowed reset is a separate thing, not an attack. That's the difference between expensive and impossible in practice, and it's easy to blur, which is why the definition at the start of this chapter leaves walls out.

So what stands behind a cost can come in three currencies. It can be money, a fee or a deposit the system takes: Casper and gas are there. It can be a wall, going back made too hard by something nobody knows how to undo, with no exact price on it: attestation and transparency are there. Or it can be physical, energy spent or hardware worn, which none of these five charges as a fee or a bond; the nearest is the chip's write budget, below.

Why the registers, then, when something that lasts would make a better record? Because the quote is what a faraway asker reads, and the registers are what it signs. The same chip has longer-lasting records, closer to the cup than anything else in this chapter. Version 2.0 of its specification has slots of a kind it calls a bit field, changed only by a command that ORs new bits into the old value: while that slot exists, it can set a bit and can't clear one. Deleting a slot and making it again is governed by separate permissions, and any claim about how long the record lasts has to include them. The chip also has counters that only go up, and a new counter starts above every count the chip has held before, so deleting one and making a new one doesn't start over at zero. That's a promise about this chip, not about somebody swapping in another chip.

Those slots are rationed. Bryan Parno and his coauthors, building a system called Memoir in 2011, noted that the specification of the day only required a counter to be able to tick once every five seconds, and that the chip's storage was expected to survive about a hundred thousand writes in its whole life, so writing once a second would wear it out in under twenty-eight hours. Memoir writes to it twice per startup. That's an expected lifetime, not a budget the protocol enforces, and the quoted registers have no number like it at all. But on the bit field it's chapter five's row of cups, which can only turn up as many times as there are cups. Phones and small controllers go one floor further down, to fuses like Chow's in chapter fourteen: a version number kept as a row of fuses, one more blown at each security update, and startup code that refuses anything older. A 2021 guide by Rob Smart for PSA Certified, a security certification scheme for chips and devices, tells designers to make sure there are enough fuses to last the number of updates expected over the device's life. That's chapter five's count of cups, written as a design rule.

What it's all for is rollback. Memoir's example is a password checker put back to an old saved state to undo a user's password change. In 2017 a system called ROTE did the same job for Intel's enclaves by having several machines vouch for each other's counters, and its authors say the only way left to break it is to reset every one of them to its start. In 2016 Raoul Strackx and Frank Piessens's Ariadne got the job down to flipping one bit of lasting memory per update, which they call the lowest possible. Notice who the attacker is in every one of these: whoever gets to put an old state back. Not a bad program. A record kept by the step can't see that attacker. Everything an established record guarantees about its history is stated from a clean start. Put back a state the machine really reached, and every guarantee still holds of it, through the run that reached it first. None of them can say nothing came after it. The toll on each step still holds after a restore; it just can't tell you what happened before.

Systems run as several copies put the same kind of record somewhere else: inside each copy, where a copy that's been taken over still can't make it say two different things. Byung-Gon Chun and his coauthors built a log a copy can only add to, called A2M, in 2007. Dave Levin and his coauthors shrank that to a trusted counter, TrInc, in 2009. MinBFT, from Giuliana Veronese and her coauthors in 2013, uses a trusted counter to agree with 2f + 1 copies when f of them can lie, where the usual count is 3f + 1. Damysus, from Jérémie Decouchant, David Kozhaya, Vincent Rahli and Jiangshan Yu in 2022, has a small trusted service it calls the Checker, which keeps a count of where the protocol is that only goes up. And Allen Clement, Flavio Junqueira, Aniket Kate and Rodrigo Rodrigues worked out in 2012, with proofs, what such a record buys, and as I read their result it's a good warning for this book. On its own, stopping a copy from saying two different things doesn't lower the 3f + 1 at all when messages can be slow. Add signatures a third party can check, and it turns a system that survives crashes into one that survives liars, with no extra copies. The way I read it, a record helps the others only as far as they can check what it says. That's chapter seven from the other side.

Under those three currencies there's a fourth, and every permanent record on a finite chip pays in it: the chairs from chapter two. Here's how. Forgetting by itself doesn't pick out a record write; any step that overwrites a field that could hold two things forgets something. What picks it out is where the forgetting lands. Count the states where the record already reads yes, and call that number K. Take a step that never turns the record off anywhere, and turns it on at one state that read no. That step sends each of the K yes-states to a yes-state, and it sends the one no-state to a yes-state too. That's K plus one starting places and only K yes-states to land on, so two of them land together. Both came from the yes side or from the one it just raised. So what a permanent write destroys is a distinction on the record's own yes side: two states it already vouched for, or the one it just raised and one that was up already. Any overwrite merges something somewhere. A permanent write has to merge it there. That needs no assumption about money, keys or heat. The other three each need an assumption of their own: a fee rule, a wall nobody can climb, a law of physics.

What the quote can do is say what was measured. It can't say the measured code is correct, and it can't say anything about what was never folded in. A correctly measured buggy program gets a beautiful quote. Try it with the smallest pair there is: two machines with nothing measured at all, one with some running flag up and one with it down. Their registers are the same, so their quotes are the same, and anything that reads only the quote has to give them the same answer. No reader of the quote can recover the flag. That's chapter seven's window again. Anything that depends only on what was measured, the quote can answer; that's what it's for. And a ``sign and check'' interface by itself promises nothing: a check that always says yes fits the interface perfectly and accepts a signature no key ever made. The protection has to come from the scrambling and the keys, which is a further assumption.

That limit, turned outward, is the honest edge of chapter eight's four conditions. Every one of them is about a thing the machine holds: same property, same thing, same edition, honest check. For a claim about the world, the oven is hot, the parcel arrived, there's no edition the machine controls. Whatever reads the world is a window onto it, and ``nothing changed between the check and the commit'' stops being something a step can enforce and becomes an assumption about the sensor. So a record can establish claims about the machine's own state, and claims about the world only through stated assumptions about whatever measured them. A correctly recorded sensor reading establishes what the sensor reported. Whether the oven was hot still depends on the sensor and on how its report connects to the world.

\subsection{Certificate transparency: visible, unpriced}

The certificates that put the padlock in your browser are issued by hundreds of authorities, and a bad or broken one can issue a certificate for a site it shouldn't. Certificate Transparency puts certificates in public logs you can only add to, built as trees of scrambled values. It was written up as RFC 6962 in 2013 and revised as RFC 9162 in 2021. I went looking for which one the running logs follow, and it's still 6962: Mozilla's reference for web developers says browsers are built to it, and as late as November 2025 Chrome's team was only proposing to drop its rule that every certificate carry a timestamp from at least one 6962 log. My copy is of 9162.

The tree is chapter four's chained running total, folded into a tree: Ralph Merkle's tree from the same chapter. Scramble each certificate; those are the leaves. Scramble the leaves together in pairs, then those in pairs, and so on, up to one value at the top, the root. To prove one leaf is in, the log hands you only the few values sitting beside the climb from that leaf to the root, and you redo the climb yourself. A second check shows that a bigger tree is a smaller one with leaves added on the end and nothing rewritten. The record is a leaf in the tree.

That kind of tree has a history of its own. The computer scientists Scott Crosby and Dan Wallach, at Rice University, built logs you can't quietly change on the same kind of tree in 2009, with a proof that an entry is in the log and a proof that a later version extends an earlier one, each only a handful of values long even for a huge log. A rewrite that doesn't extend a root somebody already saw gets caught by the second check.

Do the climb by hand. Lay out seven cards, d0 through d6. Join neighbours with paper brackets: a joins d0 and d1, b joins d2 and d3, c joins d4 and d5. d6 stands alone. Then L joins a and b, and R joins c and d6. The root joins L and R.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}cll@{}}
Step & Card handed over & What you rebuild \\ \hline
Start & $d_3$ itself & $d_3$ \\
1 & $d_2$, on the left & $b$, joining $d_2$ and $d_3$ \\
2 & $a$, on the left & $L$, joining $a$ and $b$ \\
3 & $R$, on the right & the root, joining $L$ and $R$ \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

To check d3, you only need three cards: d2, a and R. Join d2 on the left of d3 and you've rebuilt b. Join a on the left of that and you've rebuilt L. Join R on the right and you've rebuilt the root. You never opened a or R. Swap left and right anywhere, though, and the bracket changes: the proof has to carry the order as well as the cards. The same seven cards check every other leaf too. d0 needs d1, b and R, all on the right. d4 needs d5 and d6 on the right and then L on the left. d6, standing alone, needs only c and then L, both on the left.

Now the second check. Say you saw the tree when it had four cards, and its root was L. Now it has seven. The log hands you one card, R. Put L on the left of R and you get the new root. So the old tree is the left half of the new one, untouched, and only cards were added on the end.

My brackets are brackets, not numbers, so what you just checked is how the tree is put together. A real log uses short scrambled values, and ``two different trees can't give the same root'' is then a separate promise, from the wall.

What does the RFC price? Nothing. It says in its first section that logs let people detect a bad certificate afterwards. They don't prevent one. And a log that shows different trees to different people can beat the checking, so logs are still trusted parties. What it buys is visibility.

So why does any authority bother logging? Nothing in the RFC makes it. A browser does. Chrome's team set the rule, enforced from Chrome 68 in July 2018, that every certificate issued after 30 April 2018 had to meet its transparency policy, and a publicly trusted certificate that doesn't simply isn't trusted by Chrome: the visitor gets a full-page warning in front of the site. So there is a price after all, though it isn't in the specification. It lives in one company's browser, and it's the price of being shown to that browser's users at all. Which means the strongest lever on this record is a policy one company can change, and that's a fact about who holds the stamp, not about the tree. It changes nothing in the table, which asks what each specification prices. But keep two sentences apart: ``the specification prices nothing'' and ``nothing is priced.''

\subsection{Proof-carrying code: no toll required}

George Necula's idea, from chapter twelve, is to send untrusted code with a proof that it follows the receiving computer's rules; the receiving computer works out what would have to be true and checks the proof with a small checker before running anything. The record is the checked proof, or the result of checking it, and a computer may keep either. Nothing in the idea requires a permanent box, or charges anything for accepting. Producing and checking the proof both take work, and nothing here makes that work the toll.

\subsection{The same question, five ways}

Lay them out by the one definition of price from the start. Casper puts a bond behind the write and takes it on going back. Gas takes a fee at the write, mostly refunds it if you take the write back, up to a cap, and rations writes with a gas limit per block. The chip's quoted registers have no fee and no budget; its separate bit fields have only so many bits to set, which isn't a quota on everything the chip does. Transparency charges nothing at the write; what it gives is that a bad certificate can be seen afterwards. Proof-carrying code requires neither a toll nor keeping anything.

\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.15\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.21\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{System} & \textbf{Fee at the write} & \textbf{Bond} & \textbf{Budget} & \textbf{What my copy shows} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
Casper finality & none & the deposit, taken on a contradicting vote & none & two contradicting final checkpoints mean a third of the stake can be slashed \\
Gas & 20000 for a fresh slot, mostly refunded if taken back, up to a cap & none & the gas limit of each block & a write left standing in an empty slot nets at least 20000 \\
Attestation & none on the quoted registers & none & none on those registers & the quote can't tell you what it never measured \\
Transparency & none; a bad certificate can be seen & none & none & the RFC's own examples pass \\
Proof-carrying code & none required & none & none & a comparison of ideas; no checker is built here \\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

The first four rows come with a piece copied out and checked; the fifth is a comparison of ideas. Each copy shows only what it shows. To read a row as a claim about the running system you'd need evidence from that system.

So the thing the toll charges for, the moment the record is written, gets a fee in one of the five, and in that one the designers had to patch the place where the charge could be clawed back. I don't find that a win or a loss. It's a fact about what people built, and what they built was mostly walls and penalties for contradicting yourself. A toll on the write was rare. Whether a toll is a better design than a wall is something I can't prove, and I won't pretend the five say so. Because the definition came first, that last part is a count: of the fifteen fee, bond and budget squares in the table, three are filled, one of each kind, and only gas takes a payment at the moment of the write.

What I read in them, and it's a reading of five cases I chose, is that each one has something it treats as the record, they last for different lengths of time, and they all picked something different: final, committed, measured, logged, checked. Is there anything that picks out one event as the one worth recording, or is it a free choice every time? That's the next chapter.

%===========================================================================
\section{Which box is worth keeping}
\label{sec:worth-keeping}
%===========================================================================

Everything so far leaves one choice open. Chapter fourteen showed that a box that answers to the machine and never comes down is a cup on some event, and that the event is a choice: the two cups on one die were both happy under the same rules. Chapter eighteen found five real systems that each picked a different event. And in this book I picked one too: the established record from chapter eight, a box that goes up only right after a check of the very claim it records has passed. So I owe you an answer to the obvious question. Is that just my choice, or is there something about how people and machines lean on records that picks that event out?

What I have is a thesis, in the same sense the Church-Turing thesis from chapter one is one: a claim tying things I can define to the world, which no proof can settle. Nothing before this chapter depends on it. The cups, the windows and the counting hold whichever event a system records. What follows is where the idea came from, what exactly it claims, and how you'd catch it being wrong.

\subsection{A dial and the light off it}

The shape comes from physics, and I want to tell you exactly where, because I borrowed a word and didn't get to keep all of it.

Quantum mechanics has a famous problem. A system can be in a blend of states, and when you measure it you find one definite state, and nobody agrees on what measuring did. In 1981 Wojciech Zurek asked a sharper question: of all the ways a measuring device could end up, why does it end up recording one particular kind of quantity, the position of a pointer on a dial, say, and never some blend? His answer, built up over twenty years and summed up in a 2009 paper called ``Quantum Darwinism,'' is that the environment decides. The air, the light, the table, everything the device touches, keeps interacting with it, and only certain states of the device survive that; those are what he calls the pointer states. More than that, the environment picks them out and then copies them. The light bouncing off a dial carries the dial's reading to every corner of the room, over and over, so a thousand people can each catch a different scrap of that light and all agree on where the pointer is, without any of them touching the dial. On his view, being objective is being copied like that: a property is objective when many separate pieces of the world each carry it.

Zurek isn't the only one behind it. Fernando Brandão, Marco Piani and Paweł Horodecki in 2015 proved an approximate version: for most small pieces of a large environment, what each piece carries can be matched, up to a small error, by measuring the system in one common way and handing each piece the result. The error and the share of exceptions are part of their theorem, and agreeing on outcomes also needs the pieces to carry enough about them. Xiao-Liang Qi and Daniel Ranard in 2021 tightened the bound on the exceptions. Neither says every observer gets an exact record, or any useful record at all.

What I borrowed is the shape. Take a machine together with everything around it that reads, stores and relies on its records. The established record is to that crowd what the pointer reading is to the room: the thing that gets copied everywhere. It's a good shape, and I think it's the right hunch. But I'll be exact about what survived the trip, because my word ``copied'' does less work than his.

\subsection{The claim}

Here's the claim, said plainly. Of all the events a machine could put on its bill, the ones its price list charges for and its counter adds up, the established record is the one whose marks get copied and relied on by parties who didn't make them: checked, stored and acted on in separate places that share nothing else.

Copying alone can't be the test. A system run as many copies, like chapter eighteen's chains, copies everything it holds to every copy: the gas a block used, as faithfully as the word final. So rival events get copied too. Reliance doesn't split them apart either. On Ethereum, since a 2021 change called EIP-1559, the gas the last block used sets the next block's base fee: aim for a block half full, and if the last one came out completely full, the base fee goes up by an eighth, so 800 becomes 900; if it came out empty, it drops by an eighth, to 700. Every copy reads that number and acts on it. That's an event nobody would call an established record, copied and relied on by parties who didn't make it.

So examples can't show that only the established record gets copied and relied on. What the thesis has to ask is narrower: whether some kind of reliance, picked out for its own reasons, selects that event, and whether that selection gives a reason to charge for it. Any exact test fixes its readers and their actions in advance. It can test those choices, but it can't choose the real readers for me.

There's a second handle on the same question, from chapter three. On a finite machine, every move that settles something for good is a move Bennett's rule charges, and that argument never used the word ``check.'' So it points at a narrower candidate: the events worth pricing are the ones that write a record no later step can take back. The established record is one of those. As an exact answer, though, that candidate is wrong, because the three cards in chapter three showed a charged move that takes a star back. Whether the records that get copied and relied on are the permanent ones, and whether being copied and relied on picks out anything the merging doesn't, is what the two handles have to settle between them.

I can see two holes in this from here, so you don't have to find them for me.

The first is a missing step. Even if the world copies established records more than any other kind, ``copied'' doesn't give you ``priced'' by itself. Something has to connect them, and the best candidate I have is about incentives: a record that many separate parties copy and lean on is exactly the record worth forging, so it's the one where a price buys the most protection. That's reasonable, but it's an assumption. The thesis states the connection as something to test. It doesn't explain it, and nothing in this book does.

The second is a circle. If ``established record'' just meant ``the record other people rely on,'' then of course it would be the one that gets relied on and copied, and the claim would say nothing. So the event has to be picked out without mentioning reliance, and it is: a box going up for good right after a check of the same claim passed. You can find that in a run without asking who reads it. Whether the world copies that event more than its rivals is then a real question, and it could come out no.

Now the word. Fix, for some crowd of readers, a test for whether an event is copied and relied on by parties who didn't make it. That test has to come from somewhere other than the established record, or the whole thing repeats a definition. Call an event a pointer, after Zurek's dial, if it passes that test and, on every state the machine can actually reach, every other event that passes holds wherever it does. There may not be one. But there can't be two that disagree: if two events both pass and each holds wherever anything that passes holds, then each holds wherever the other does, so on the reachable states they're the same event.

So here's the thesis, which I call the pointer thesis. Take record-keeping the way people actually run it, modelled faithfully. Then two things. First, the established record is the pointer: it gets copied and relied on by parties who didn't make it, and every event that gets copied and relied on that way holds wherever it does. Second, every record the system puts a price on gets copied and relied on by parties who didn't make it.

It's a thesis and not a theorem because of two words: ``faithfully'' and ``actually run.'' Those are what tie the definitions to the world, the way ``everything a person could do by following rules'' does for Church and Turing, and no definition can do that job. The other thing it needs does have an exact meaning. Readers have separate carriers when each reader's view depends only on its own part of the world, and any two readers' parts could be set independently of each other. The states the machine reaches can still tie those parts together; the thesis asks for agreement there. Everything else, which systems, which states, which event, which prices, goes in as input. The thesis proves nothing about which real systems fit it.

\subsection{How much of the borrowed shape survived}

Zurek's copying comes in degrees: each piece of the environment carries almost all of it, with a little lost. And it needs the pieces to be separate: different people reading different parts of the light.

My first try at writing this down had neither. It was the no-loss case with no separateness at all: a crowd of readers, one yes-or-no each, and an event counted as copied when every reader's yes matched it exactly. Nothing in that says two readers draw on different parts of anything. Picture three readers all looking at the same single switch. They agree perfectly, and they're one reader three times. So I'd kept the word and dropped the separateness that makes his version mean ``objective.'' That's why the thesis above asks for separate carriers, and it's also why separate carriers aren't enough on their own: three separate switches can be set any way at all, so something, a copying process, has to put them in step before they agree.

The separateness I dropped is exactly what the thesis's closest published neighbour keeps. Jess Riedel, Zurek and Michael Zwolak in 2016 took histories of a closed quantum system, the story a whole run of events tells, and asked which of them the world keeps records of in many separate pieces of the environment. As I read their paper, records kept in many separate pieces make a history behave, so ordinary yes-or-no reasoning about it works, and they pick out a small set of histories from the huge number that merely behave: in their words, an objective past. That's ``what gets copied is what counts,'' in quantum form, with the separateness in it, and it came before this book. Jonathan Halliwell, in a 1999 paper called ``Somewhere in the universe,'' had already carried the link between records and well-behaved histories from the simplest starting states to messier ones, where the records still exist but can't be perfect.

There's a deeper mismatch in the borrowing, and it runs against this book, so it goes here. In Zurek's picture the whole thing, system and device and environment together, follows quantum mechanics' rule for a closed system, and that rule never lands two states on one. Every step can be run backwards. So seen from the whole, the environment's copying is just copying: the dial's reading gets written onto fresh light, and no two states anywhere meet. That's chapter two's way out, fresh paper, and chapter six's blank page. It isn't a claim that a closed system goes on to new states forever; a finite closed system can come back around.

Look at the dial alone and it's different, because you've thrown away how it's tied up with the light. Quantum states carry a phase, a part that only turns up when things interfere. Take two states of the dial that are as different as two states can be, differing only in that phase. Let the environment wash the phase out completely, and the dial on its own ends up exactly the same from either start. Two arrows met, in the dial's own description. If the washing out is gradual, they only get harder to tell apart, and never exactly equal at any finite time. So an exact merge in a part of the picture, a slow loss of what you could tell apart, and a flow of heat are three different claims. Getting a heat cost out of any of them needs more: how the thing was prepared, what the change in disorder was, and the conditions that make heat the right word. Copying by itself supplies none of that.

So the thesis points at copies being kept and used, and the quantum account explains particular ways information spreads and becomes readable. It doesn't attach this book's toll to the copying.

A published no-go cuts closer still. Philippe Allard Guérin, Veronika Baumann, Flavio Del Santo and Časlav Brukner gave an argument in 2021 that, under assumptions that look natural in quantum mechanics, an observer's own record of an outcome at two different times can't share one reality: no joint chance for the two, depending in the simple straight-line way on the starting state, matches what quantum theory predicts for each. A record that lasts, for the one holding it, is exactly what gets into trouble once the whole is treated as never merging anything. It isn't a theorem about my machine, which isn't quantum. It's still the strongest published reason I know to hold the word ``permanent'' loosely when a physicist is in the room.

Which leaves the question to ask of any borrowed shape: has anybody checked the original? For a long time quantum Darwinism was a theory without a lab. Then, in 2018 and 2019, three groups working separately, in Italy, China and Germany, went looking for its fingerprint, as Philip Ball's 2019 report in Quanta Magazine tells it. The German one, at Ulm, with Zurek among the authors, watched the spin of a single defect inside a diamond and the carbon atoms around it, and saw the copying the theory predicts: the defect's state turned up copied in its surroundings, and it didn't take much of the surroundings before more of them told you nothing new. The other two did it with light. Is that the bottom? Not by a long way, and the people involved said so. Zurek himself pointed out that the experiments with light make their copies artificially and that the diamond's environment is small, and others noted that all three are sketches of a real environment and don't rule out rival accounts. So the shape I borrowed has passed some first tests in a lab, at the scale of a handful of atoms or bits of light, with those cautions written on it. None of that touches the thesis here, which is about machines and never rested on quantum physics. It changes how I hold the picture: what I borrowed is a lead somebody is checking. It isn't settled, and I don't get to lean on it as if it were.

\subsection{What the thesis asks of the examples}

Three comparisons would test it, and each one could come out against it.

First, look at a record a real system puts a price on. Do parties who didn't make it copy it and rely on it? A priced record that nobody else relies on breaks the second half.

Second, put the established record next to its rivals. If some event the established record doesn't cover gets copied and relied on to the same standard, the established record isn't the one pointer. The base fee from a moment ago is a candidate: on Ethereum it's read and acted on everywhere. Whether it passes a test of reliance that was picked for its own reasons, and not to let it in or keep it out, is exactly the kind of thing the thesis has to face.

Third, give the readers separate carriers in a faithful model, and ask whether the established record itself passes. Each reader has to be able to recover the event from its own view. A reader whose view stays the same whether the event happened or not can't; that's chapter seven's wall, where nothing about the box comes through. Naming a crowd of readers isn't enough. The model has to say what each one can read and what each one relies on.

Those comparisons are the evidence the claim about the world asks for. The exact test only turns into a definite question once the model and its readers are chosen.

\subsection{Where the argument has got to}

This is the last choice the book has to own, so here's the whole road behind it, with what was shown kept apart from what was chosen.

Chapters one and two showed that on a finite machine, settling something for good makes two arrows meet, unless the machine uses fresh paper on every tick. Chapter three chose a price on two arrows meeting, Bennett's rule, and showed that with it, every move that settles something for good costs at least one. Chapter four built the smallest machine where that happens. Chapter five showed that a record with more than two positions is a row of cups, that a straight line of stages needs exactly one cup fewer than it has stages, and that the toll on the row charges at least one on any step that turns up a cup. Chapter six showed that looking can be free: a look uses up a blank page, which can be handed back, and owes no floor. Chapter seven showed that a window with a clash can't price exactly. Chapter eight chose what ``established'' means: up only right after a passing check of the claim. Chapter nine drew what settling looks like, a sorting with a door that only swings one way. Chapter ten said what structure is and which ruler I chose to measure it. Chapter eleven showed that once the price list is fixed, the counter is arithmetic, and that a counter that goes down gets leaned on where it goes down. Chapter twelve showed what a ticked box lets you lean on, and no more. Chapter thirteen chose the meaning of insight: a distinction made to stay, with price and depth kept apart. Chapter fourteen showed that over any machine, a box that answers to it and never comes down is a cup, with two choices left open, the price list and the event. Chapter fifteen showed what the toll pays for when the machine is finite, the record only grows and the uncovered machine forgets nothing: crushing two states of one pile. Chapter sixteen let the machine read its box to take a shortcut, and found four ways to keep the shortcut honest after the data changes, each one chapter two's bill in a different currency. Chapter seventeen showed that a machine's own shortcuts can't be judged from inside it, and that the commitment can still be recorded. Chapter eighteen found that of five real systems, read through my copies, one charges a fee at the write. And this chapter proposes the event, and says how the proposal could fail.

\subsection{Does the list of requirements pick out the toll?}

One question about the price itself is still open. Do the requirements I've put on a price pick out the toll and nothing else? If a short list of them left only one price standing, that would settle it. The lists I tried don't, and the no is small enough to check on three states.

Call them blank, once and twice, with once and twice reading yes. Two moves. The flip takes blank to once and leaves the other two where they are. The smooth takes once to twice, and leaves twice and blank alone. Draw the arrows. The smooth lands once and twice both on twice: two arrows meet, so Bennett's rule charges it. The flip lands blank and once both on once, so the rule charges it too. But only the flip raises anything, and only from blank. The smooth goes from yes to yes.

Now try requirements that never mention the reading. No charge on a move that changes nothing. No charge on a move that forgets nothing (both moves here forget, so this one asks nothing). The same cost if you rename the states (the only renaming that keeps both moves the same is leaving every name alone, so this asks nothing either). A total that adds up along a run. A cost that depends only on the state and the move. And a cost of one on the flip from blank. Let the price depend on the state as well as the move, the way chapter seven's clerk was allowed to. Go through the six steps one at a time. The flip from once and the flip from twice change nothing, so they're free. The smooth from blank and the smooth from twice change nothing, so they're free. The flip from blank costs one. That leaves the smooth from once, and nothing on the list says a word about it. Charge it nothing, and you have the toll: one on the raise and nothing else. Charge it one, and you have a price on merging. Charge it two, and that passes too. The list can't choose.

What chooses is a requirement that names the record: over any run, the total is never more than the number of times the reading went up. The smooth from once raises nothing, so it has to cost nothing. With the floor from the toll, that leaves exactly one price, one per raise and nothing else, which is chapter seven's step price again. So the uniqueness is real, and it's small. It holds once you've said which reading you're charging for. And which reading deserves the charge is the pointer thesis, which is still a thesis.

The uniqueness results that do exist are about pricing information lost, and they're worth a look because they say what kind of charge a price on merging is. John Baez, Tobias Fritz and Tom Leinster in 2011, mathematicians who work in category theory, the mathematics Lawvere's diagonal from chapter seventeen came from, asked what a measure of information loss has to look like. As I read their paper, they proved that for maps that keep track of the probabilities, Shannon's entropy, up to a constant factor, is the only measure of information loss with three natural properties: it adds up along a chain of maps, it mixes the way probabilities mix, and it changes smoothly. János Aczél and Bruno Forte, mathematicians at the University of Waterloo who worked on functional equations, and Che Tat Ng, who had taken his doctorate there under Aczél in 1971, asked in a 1974 paper which entropies deserve to be called natural, and proved, by their account, that the ones with a short list of natural properties are exactly the mixtures of Shannon's and Hartley's, where Hartley's is just the log of the count. So even for merges, the choice between the average and the count is a real choice. Both price merges. Neither prices raises, so neither picks out the toll, and that's the three states again, at a larger size. On the other side of the fence, Luciano Pomatto, Philipp Strack and Omer Tamuz in 2023, economists at Caltech and Yale who asked what learning something should cost, proved, by their account, a uniqueness result for what it costs to get information, when running the same experiment twice costs exactly twice as much as running it once. That prices producing a signal, which is a different matter from chapter six's copy onto a blank page; the copy owes no floor and borrows fresh paper.

Most of that isn't a machine. The arguments in this book are about any states and any moves, and chapter eighteen's five are small copies of somebody else's systems. The smallest machine is the box from chapter four, with every part named and every price written down. That's where everything above has something to bump into. If a statement up there doesn't mean what I think it means, the box and the five copies are where it shows.

Every price in this book so far is one I wrote down. Whether the world charges anything like it, in heat, is a question for physics, and it's the next chapter.

%===========================================================================
\section{Heat}
\label{sec:heat}
%===========================================================================

Every price in this book so far is one I wrote down. Chapter one said it had nothing to say about heat, and chapter three promised heat its own chapter, with the people who doubt it named, and promised that Landauer's heat would turn out to be owed only for what you didn't know. This is that chapter. Everything in it is either counting, which I'll do with you, or physics, which I'll name and source and leave as physics. I'll keep saying which is which.

\subsection{The stamp at zero}

Start where chapter three left one door open. Take a sheet of paper whose reading is just BLANK or STAMPED, and one move: stamp it if it's blank, leave it alone if it's already stamped. It's finite, two readings. It's permanent: STAMPED stays STAMPED. And it settles something, because BLANK goes to STAMPED. Both readings land on STAMPED, so two arrows meet. The paper's fibres, the ink and your memory aren't in this two-state picture; the merge is a loss from the reading, not the disappearance of every trace.

Now set the price of that move to zero. The toll fails, and you can watch it fail: the box went up for nothing. Nothing in counting stops you from doing that. A price of one satisfies everything chapter three asked for, so the asks can hold together; whether they must is a different question, and only a claim about the world can answer it. The nearest such claim is Rolf Landauer's.

I have to be exact about the gap between his claim and mine. Bennett's rule, as I wrote it, charges every move where two arrows meet, whether or not you already knew which state you were in. Landauer's heat, as this chapter will show, is owed only for what you didn't know. My rule is the worst case on purpose: a move that has to work whatever it's handed pays for the worst it could do. And it keeps only a yes-or-no question, whether any pair can meet. The one-bit reset is where it comes from, and it doesn't measure the heat of any particular preparation.

\subsection{What Landauer said}

Here's what Landauer said, in 1961, as exactly as I can put it. I went past the summaries and read the paper itself. A logical operation with no single-valued inverse, a step that forgets, in this book's words, must give off heat, ``typically of the order of kT'' per operation, where k is Boltzmann's constant from chapter three and T is the temperature. For the specific case of resetting one bit whose value you don't know, the minimum is kT times the natural log of 2, about 0.69 kT.

I want you to hear two things in that. First, ``of the order of'': he wasn't handing out a number to three decimals. Second, and this surprised me, he didn't measure anything. The paper is argument, with models. He writes that he can ``show, or at least very strongly suggest'' the conclusion, and that his reasoning gives ``no guarantee that this minimum is in fact achievable.'' That's the founding paper of the whole subject, and it's a careful man saying ``I think this is right and here's why.'' I like him more for it.

Was it measured, eventually? Yes, and only recently. In 2012 a group at the École Normale Supérieure in Lyon, Antoine Bérut and Sergio Ciliberto's lab working with Eric Lutz, trapped a single glass bead, two millionths of a metre across, in a double well made of laser light, and used the two wells as the two values of a bit. Then they erased the bit, by lowering the barrier, tilting the trap and raising it again, many times over, slower and slower, and measured the heat each time. The average heat came down toward Landauer's kT ln 2 as the erasure got slower, closer the slower it went, and never under it, on average. Single runs went under it; the bound is about the average, and any one erasure can beat it. And if you only succeed in erasing half the time, the floor drops to zero, which the paper's formula shows and which I think is the most honest line in it.

Others have done it since with different devices. Yonggun Jun, Momčilo Gavrilov and John Bechhoefer did it in 2014 with a bead in a feedback trap, and ran the same moves with no tilt as a control. Jeongmin Hong, Brian Lambson, Scott Dhuey and Jeffrey Bokor did it in 2016 with tiny magnetic memory bits. Rocco Gaudenzi and his coauthors did it in 2018 with the spins of molecules at one kelvin. The table near the end of this chapter has their numbers. So it was predicted in 1961 by argument, and seen in a laboratory fifty-one years later.

\subsection{Who doubts it}

Is it settled? No, and I'd be lying by leaving that out, because the people who doubt it aren't cranks. John Norton, a philosopher of physics at Pittsburgh, has argued since 2005 that the usual proofs of the principle quietly assume what they're trying to prove, and that the kinds of process they assume, put together, could be made to break the very law the principle is supposed to guard. An earlier objection, made in 1998 and 1999 by Norton and his Pittsburgh colleague John Earman, also a philosopher of physics, was that the principle can't be independent of the second law. Charles Bennett, who did more than anyone to build the subject, rated that one ``in my opinion the one of greatest merit,'' and then disagreed with it. Others replied to Norton; Norton replied back. Owen Maroney, a philosopher of physics who works on exactly this, wrote the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy's entry on it in 2009, and it ends without a verdict. I've read what I could of both sides, I'm not qualified to referee them, and I'm not going to pretend the laboratory settles a question about what the principle means in general, because measuring one bead doesn't do that.

So here's what I did with it, and I think it's the only honest thing to do. The charge on forgetting is written into this book as an assumption with a name. Nowhere is it proved as a general fact, because it can't be proved from counting. For a particular machine with a particular price list you can see it hold, the stamp at cost one, and that's a different thing. If Norton turns out to be right, every argument in this book still holds; they just have one assumption fewer that the world agrees to. If Landauer's side turns out to be right, it backs a heat price under its conditions, and the whole-number charge on every merging move is still my bookkeeping rule. Either way nothing here says a device does anything until somebody measures the device.

Naming an assumption isn't the same as leaving it unplaced, though, and a hedge isn't a position, so I'll give mine. Earman and Norton's objection is a fork, set out in the first part of their paper, in 1998. Either an information-based answer to Maxwell's demon assumes the second law, which they call the sound horn, and then it adds nothing the second law didn't already give; or it rests on a new principle tying information to entropy, the profound horn, and nobody has given that principle grounds of its own. My named assumption is the sound horn, on purpose. That's where Bennett ended up in 2003, and Norton's ``Too Good to Be True,'' written in 2025 and published in 2026, says the careful literature almost always takes that horn, and argues that whatever works there is ordinary thermodynamics relabelled. I'll take that. Ordinary thermodynamics, relabelled onto a ledger, is all this book asks of the physics.

And on the counting, I'm on Norton's side outright. His 2018 paper ``Maxwell's Demon Does Not Compute'' disposes of the demon with Liouville's theorem and nothing else, no information and no computation: motion that keeps every patch of states the same size can't crowd a big set of states into a small one. The counting at the bottom of this book, that a step losing nothing can't carry a no-state into a set that stays up, is that point in finite, countable form: chapter two's chairs.

If you want the best case for the assumption itself, the one I found is James Ladyman, Stuart Presnell, Anthony Short and Berry Groisman in 2007. From one of the standard statements of the second law, the one named for Kelvin, they derive that a logically irreversible operation can't be carried out in a thermodynamically reversible way by any of their model machines, which they call L-machines. That's a physical argument under their stated model of a machine. It deserves to be argued with on those terms. It doesn't by itself give my whole-number charge.

\subsection{Where the difference goes}

A physicist asks one question before any of that, and it's a good one. The laws underneath never merge anything. Classical mechanics carries each exact state forward to exactly one next state and could carry it back, and Liouville's theorem adds that a patch of states keeps its size as it moves. Quantum mechanics is one-to-one too. So when this book says a step merged two states, where did the difference go?

It went out of the machine. The merge is in the machine's own description, the states its reading is about. The machine together with everything around it still runs one-to-one, and the distinction the machine forgot gets pushed out into the surroundings. The surroundings are chapter two's endless strip: fresh paper, which the universe as a whole may have to spend and the machine doesn't, because the machine isn't the universe and its memory fills.

Does that make the whole argument depend on where you draw the machine? Draw it coarser or finer and you get different states: the dial on a bench is a state, and so is each atom in it, and so is each atom together with the light it gave off. It doesn't, and the reason fits in one picture.

Draw the fine states, and draw a fence around each group of them that counts as one coarse state. Say the coarse description follows the fine one exactly: wherever a fine state steps, its fence steps to the fence of where it landed, and every fence has at least one fine state in it. Now say the coarse picture merges: two fences step to one fence. Then something has to give down below. Suppose it doesn't: the fine step never merges, never steps outside the fenced states, and no run goes on landing on fine states it hasn't been to before, forever. Follow any fine run. It must come back to some fine state it's been in, and since nothing merges, walk the two visits back together one step at a time, the way chapter two did, until the earlier one reaches the start. So every fine run is a loop through its own start. Look at that loop through the fences: every fence is on a loop too. Now take the two fences that stepped to one. Wait as many steps as the product of their two loop lengths: each is back where it began. But they were together after one step, and the same rule can't pull them apart again, so they must have begun in the same place. They didn't.
