So down below, one of three things happens: the fine step merges, or it steps out of the states the coarse picture describes, or it runs off taking a new one every tick. The three ways out from chapter two come back one level down.

Chapter nineteen's dial and light are the example. In the dial's own picture, phases wash out and two starts end up the same: a merge. In the picture of dial and light together, the step is one-to-one, and it's the second way: the light starts blank and leaves carrying the copy, so the fine step leaves the states where the light is still blank. Nothing merges at the fine scale.

And that's where the heat floor sits: where the merge really is. Landauer's assumption charges the scale whose step merges states that were really possible. A description that merges only because it stopped looking at the light owes that floor nowhere just because of that. Pushing a distinction out isn't heat yet, either: another memory could keep it as a readable record. It becomes heat when a reset removes real uncertainty into something warm, a reservoir, and then Landauer's assumption bounds the average heat handed over.

None of that heat has to be wasted. Takahiro Sagawa's 2014 analysis says erasure can in principle approach a process that produces no entropy overall: the reservoir's entropy goes up by what the memory's goes down. A positive heat can be unavoidable without being wasteful.

\subsection{Two meanings of reversible}

Ladyman and his coauthors and Sagawa look like they disagree, and they don't. They use ``reversible'' for two different things.

Ladyman's L-machines are devices, and a device counts as reversible only if it produces no entropy on every input it's fed, each one known. Their section 4 says it: the L-machine is thermodynamically reversible if and only if every one of its processes, one per input, produces no entropy. Sagawa's reversible erasure is a process on one given spread of inputs, with the device tuned to that spread. The standard eraser is reversible in Sagawa's sense and not in Ladyman's: on an even mix of 0 and 1 it produces no entropy, and on each known input it produces k ln 2.

One identity holds the two together. Take a fixed device that sends every input to one output, and any spread of its inputs. The average, over the inputs, of the entropy produced on each input known, equals the entropy produced on the spread, plus k times the Shannon entropy of the spread. It holds because the heat is the same average either way, the device being fixed, and the output is the same either way. The only difference is at the start: the spread's own entropy includes a term for not knowing which input it is, and each known input's doesn't. Sagawa makes the first term on the right zero. Ladyman reads the left side, which is then k times the spread's entropy, more than zero. For the even mix of 0 and 1 that's k ln 2 on the left, nothing plus k ln 2 on the right.

That identity hands you a floor that doesn't need anybody's knowledge. Take K inputs a fixed device sends to the same output, and spread your chances evenly over them. The average over the K of the entropy produced on each one known equals what's produced on the even spread, plus k ln K. The second law makes what's produced on the spread zero or more. So the average is at least k ln K, and the worst of the K is at least the average. On some one of them, the device produces at least k ln K of entropy, and, when dissipated work is temperature times entropy produced, at least kT ln K of dissipated work. Going slowly doesn't remove that. Using different controls for different known inputs changes the device being compared; the bound is about one fixed device across them. So the floor sits on the merging move, on its worst input, whatever anyone knows. That's the shape of the toll.

\subsection{Counting in bits}

There's one more way to say all this, and I include it because it's how physicists say it, and because it says exactly when the heat floor is zero: when no two states you might be in land on one.

Claude Shannon, in 1948, gave a number to how much you don't know. If something could be in any of n states and you've no idea which, your uncertainty is log base two of n bits, the number of halvings from chapter three, and that's the most it can be for n states. Push your uncertainty through a step that forgets nothing and it stays the same; through a step that merges two states you might have been in, it drops.

Why does Shannon's number share a name with the physicists' entropy at all? There's a story. Shannon wanted to call it uncertainty, and the mathematician John von Neumann told him to call it entropy instead, for two reasons: the same formula already lived in physics under that name, and nobody really knows what entropy is, so in an argument he'd always have the upper hand. It's a great story. It comes from Myron Tribus, who spent years tying information theory to thermodynamics, who said Shannon told it to him in 1961, and who printed it with Edward McIrvine in Scientific American in 1971. Then in 1982 an interviewer, Robert Price, asked Shannon about it, and Shannon said he didn't think von Neumann had told him any such thing. So it's a legend with one witness, and the person the witness quoted doesn't remember saying it. What doesn't hang on the story: the two formulas really do have the same shape, and it still takes Landauer's assumption to turn a count of bits into heat.

Here's how the count works when the chances aren't even. Give each state a chance, all adding to one. Each state contributes its chance times the number of halvings it would take to single it out, and you add those up. Try four equally likely cards, A, B, C and D. Each has chance a quarter, and singling one out takes two halvings, so each contributes a half: two bits in all. Now send A and B to a box marked X, C to a box marked Y and D to a box marked Z. The boxes' chances are a half, a quarter, a quarter. X takes one halving to single out, Y and Z two each, so the three contribute a half each: one and a half bits. Half a bit was lost on average. You can see where: half the time the box is X and leaves one question unanswered, A or B? The other half of the time, Y or Z tells you the card exactly. An actual draw never loses half a card; the half belongs to the average over many draws.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[thiele]
\node[cnode] (a) at (0,1.5) {A: one quarter};
\node[cnode] (b) at (0,.5) {B: one quarter};
\node[cnode] (c) at (0,-.5) {C: one quarter};
\node[cnode] (d) at (0,-1.5) {D: one quarter};
\node[pipe,text width=4cm,minimum width=0pt] (x) at (6,1.5) {X: one half\\could have been A or B};
\node[pipe,text width=4cm,minimum width=0pt] (y) at (6,-.5) {Y: one quarter\\was C};
\node[pipe,text width=4cm,minimum width=0pt] (z) at (6,-2.5) {Z: one quarter\\was D};
\draw[pipearrow] (a) -- (x);
\draw[pipearrow] (b) -- (x);
\draw[pipearrow] (c) -- (y);
\draw[pipearrow] (d) -- (z);
\end{tikzpicture}

\small Half the time one question is left open; half the time the card is known.
On average, half a bit is lost.
\end{center}

A move that forgets nothing sends each state somewhere different, so the chances get carried along one for one: nothing piles up, and the uncertainty is the same number after as before. That's why a price on bits lost can leave every reversible move costing nothing, which is the shape the toll needs.

Now push uncertainty through a permanent raise. Say m states already read yes, and the move turns k states that read no into yes. Before the move you might be in any of the m + k states in play. After it, you're in one of m. How much uncertainty can m places hold? At most log base two of m, and only when the chance is spread evenly. Here's why that's the most, in one line. The curve of the natural log never rises above the straight line y minus one; they touch only at one. For each place with chance q, put y equal to the even share, 1 over m, divided by q. The line says q times the log of that y is at most the even share minus q. Add over the places: the right side totals at most one minus one, which is zero. The left side totals the spread's uncertainty minus log m. So the uncertainty is at most log m, whatever the spread.

So if you started with every one of the m + k states equally likely, you had log2 of (m + k) bits, you end with at most log2 of m, and the move removed at least log2 of (m + k)/m. That's chapter three's halving count again, in Shannon's units. There's a second floor too, and sometimes it's bigger. Count one landing pile at a time. Say a pile holds s of the starts. You land in it with chance s over (m + k), and when you do, log2 of s bits are gone: the s starts in it can't be told apart any more. So that pile's share of the total loss is s log2 s over (m + k). A pile of one adds nothing. A pile of two adds 2 over (m + k). And s log2 s is never less than 2(s minus 1): the two are equal at s of two, and from there s log2 s climbs faster. So every pile adds at least 2 over (m + k) for each start after its first. There are m + k starts and at most m piles, so at least k starts come after the first in their pile. That gives at least 2k over (m + k) bits. The first floor is reached when k is a multiple of m: keep each old yes-state where it is and send the same number of newcomers to each. The second is reached when k is at most m: send each newcomer to its own old yes-state.

And in the worst case it's a full bit every time. A permanent raise makes two arrows meet, by chapter two. Put half the chance on each of those two states, and the move removes exactly one bit, however many states already read yes.

The assumption, stated as I use it: for a chosen physical memory and its spread of inputs, a step that lowers the memory's logical uncertainty by some number of bits hands at least kT ln 2 of heat per bit to its surroundings at temperature T, on average over that preparation, not on every single run. It needs a physical model in which the logical uncertainty has that meaning; a table of arrows doesn't supply one. And it's for a symmetric memory, whose two states are alike apart from their labels, as Landauer assumed. For a memory whose states differ, the floor changes, which Owen Maroney worked out in 2009, and Gavrilov and Bechhoefer in 2016 measured erasure work in a lopsided double well that came out below kT ln 2.

\subsection{How big is that}

Take the case where one state flips in beside one that was already yes. The floor is kT ln 2. At room temperature, about 300 kelvin, that's roughly three zeptojoules: three thousandths of a billionth of a billionth of a joule. That's the biggest floor a one-state raise gets from the even spread. With one newcomer the floor shrinks as more states already read yes, because the squeeze gets thinner: with a thousand of them it's 2 ln 2 over 1001 of kT, about a seven-hundred-and-twentieth, some five hundred times less than the case of two.

So for the even spread, one unit of cost is not one halving. It's one halving exactly when the squeeze is two, and for any thinner squeeze the physics asks for less, and the toll still charges a whole unit, because the cost comes in whole numbers. In the worst case over what the machine might be holding, the way chapter three's halving rule reads a move, it's a full bit every time, so the gap between the unit and the bits is a fact about the even spread. Whatever the size, it's heat only if you grant the assumption; nothing on this page measured anything.

For comparison, Anson Ho, Ege Erdil and Tamay Besiroglu's 2023 tally of the limits of ordinary chips puts switching a single gate at a hundred attojoules at the low end, over thirty thousand times this floor. The point of the number isn't that a permanent raise is expensive. It's that, on the assumption, it isn't zero.

The same logarithm turns up in a different physical question, and I want to keep the two apart. Yûto Murashita, Ken Funo and Masahito Ueda in 2014 studied processes whose reverse histories include some with no forward partner, which they call absolute irreversibility. Their bound on average entropy produced picks up the logarithm of one over the share that's left. Their example is gas that starts in one part of a box split into two, the parts in the ratio l to 1 minus l, and spreads out through the whole box; the logarithm is of one over l. (Take l to be a tenth and you get the logarithm of ten; that number is my illustration, not theirs.) Make l the old yes-states' share of the pile, and out comes the same logarithm as the squeeze. That's a match of numbers, not of processes. My arrows don't say what the reverse experiment is or how likely its histories are, so they don't determine that share. Gonzalo Manzano, Gülce Kardeş, Édgar Roldán and David Wolpert in 2024 studied questions like that for computations with one-way steps and random stopping times. Their physical assumptions are on top of my counting.

The even spread is the easy case, and it's fair to worry that the heat depends on picking it. It doesn't. Take any spread that gives a real chance to the state that flips and to every yes-state. After the move, they all land among the yes-states, and there's one more of them than there are yes-states, so two of them with real chances land together. Count bits state by state: each state carries its chance to where it lands and finds whatever else landed there, and its share of the loss is how much more crowded its landing spot is than what it brought. Nobody's spot holds less than they brought, so every share is zero or more, and the two that landed together each find a spot holding more than they brought, so their shares are more than zero. A positive number of bits, at a positive temperature, is positive heat.

\subsection{Three roads, two strengths}

There are three ways to charge for forgetting, and they come in two strengths. Charge the bits a move removes, on every spread: that's entropy pricing. Charge, as chapter three did, at least as many units as the halvings in a move's biggest landing pile: that's the halving rule. These two are the same rule. The most bits a move can remove, on any spread, is log2 of its biggest pile, because once you know which pile you landed in, the starts left to tell apart are at most that many; and the even spread on that biggest pile removes exactly that much. So a price that covers every spread covers the biggest pile, and a price that covers the biggest pile covers every spread.

The third road is Bennett's rule from chapter three: at least one whenever any two arrows meet. That's the halving rule looked at through two starts only: if two starts land apart nothing's lost, and if they land together, a pile of two asks for one halving. So it's weaker. Try eight equally likely cards all sent to one box: three bits lost. A charge of one passes Bennett's rule and fails the other two, which ask for three. All three give the toll on a permanent raise; only the stronger two give the size of the squeeze.

\subsection{The coin under the cup}

Now the case where the floor is zero. Toss a fair coin under a cup without looking at how it landed, and let the rule be: set it heads up. You might have had tails; now you have heads; one bit of what you didn't know is gone, and that's the bit Landauer charges for. Now lift the cup, see heads, put the cup back, and apply the rule again. Nothing you didn't know changed, and no bit was lost. And the assumption, applied to a state you already knew, asks for nothing: a known state is a spread with all its chance on one state, which holds no bits before or after, so the assumption reads ``heat at least zero.'' It sets no floor.

That's easy to overread, so be careful with it. It doesn't mean a stamping machine run on a known-blank sheet gives off nothing. A real stamping device may cost something because it runs in finite time; how much depends on how it's built, how it's driven and how fast it goes. All it means is that the assumption sets no floor there. The floor comes from what you didn't know. And it doesn't mean telling somebody which way the coin landed changes an unchanged device's heat. If you lifted the cup after an uncertain toss, you made a record that's tied up with the coin, and resetting that record has to be counted with that tie. If the coin was handed to you heads up for certain, there was no uncertain toss to record. Those are different preparations.

This is the general form of Landauer's principle, worked out by Owen Maroney in 2009, in a paper he wrote at the Perimeter Institute in Canada, and by Sagawa in 2014. And Landauer himself had the seed of it in 1961: for a row of bits all already ONE, he wrote, there's no need to carry out any operation, so no entropy changes and no heat is given off; and resetting a row all ZERO to ONE involves no entropy change either, because neither row carries any information.

Notice that the toll still charges the known-state stamp one unit, because the toll is a rule about the move, and the move doesn't know what the watcher knows. That's the worst-case gap from the start of the chapter, and it's the price of having a rule a machine can follow without asking who's watching.

The physics of all this has a standard review, Juan Parrondo, Jordan Horowitz and Takahiro Sagawa's ``Thermodynamics of information'' from 2015, which puts Landauer's bound, its general form and the costs of measuring and acting on what you measured side by side. And the gap between a device and the spread it runs on has a physicist's number on it. Artemy Kolchinsky and David Wolpert showed in 2017 that a fixed process built to waste as little as possible on one spread over its states pays extra when it runs on another, and the extra is how much closer the process brings the two spreads together, measured in relative entropy. They called it the dissipation due to an incorrect prior, and in 2018 Wolpert and Kolchinsky gave it the name it goes by now, mismatch cost. Under their conditions, their formula says which input pays: the one the device's own best spread gave the least chance. A device can make one input cheap only by making another dear. That's the worst-case gap again, priced for an actual spread, where the toll prices the move.

There's a second piece of fine print, about who works the controls. Lower this barrier, tilt that well, raise the barrier again: those operations have a timing and a physical build, and counting bits prices neither. Paul Riechers, Alexander Boyd, Gregory Wimsatt and James Crutchfield in 2020 analysed memories held behind barriers under controls that are the same played forwards or backwards. A symmetric clock pulse can be like that; an arbitrary one needn't be. For that setting, as the allowed error shrinks, the least work picks up a term that grows like kT times the log of one over the error, times the chance that the memory sits in a state the operation, done twice, doesn't bring back to where it started. They call those transitions nonreciprocal. A permanent raise is nonreciprocal at the no-state: doing it twice can't bring it back to no. So a known input there can face extra work in their setting, even though it holds no bits. Their class also includes a three-state cycle, which merges nothing. It's about the allowed controls as well as the arrows, and it doesn't change the coin under the cup.

One more limit, about which states collide. Chapter two guarantees two arrows meet among the yes-states together with the one coming in. It doesn't guarantee that the one coming in is one of the two. Take four states with only the first reading no, and a move that sends the first to the second, the second to the third, and leaves the third and fourth where they are. The second and third land together on the third, and the first shares with nobody. So for a spread with all its chance on no-states, this counting alone forces no loss. The toll on the move still follows from Bennett's rule.

So here's what the counting and the assumption give together. A permanent raise has two starting states that land on one. Prepare those two with equal chances, and the raise removes one bit, and on the assumption its average heat is at least kT ln 2. That's a worst-case preparation, not a charge on every run. A known start gets no floor from the assumption. A fixed device that merges K inputs produces at least k ln K of entropy on one of them. And the ledger charges the merging move whatever the preparation, because my rule says so. The counting proves a collision; the preparation decides how much uncertainty is removed; and the physics connects that to heat.

\subsection{Going faster gives off more heat}

Everything so far is about the floor, the least heat as you go slower and slower. Going at a speed costs on top of that, and here there's a small model I can work all the way through.

Charles Bennett's Brownian computer, from his 1982 review, is a walker on a line that's pushed forward a little. At each tick it steps forward with some chance p and back with the rest. Its speed, on average, is the difference between the two, 2p minus 1. Grant the standard assumption of this corner of physics, called local detailed balance, which Udo Seifert's 2012 review sets out: a forward step hands the surroundings kT times the log of p over 1 minus p in heat, and a backward step takes the same amount back. Then the average heat per tick, in units of kT, is the speed times that log, which follows from Jürgen Schnakenberg's 1976 formula for the entropy produced by a system that jumps between states. When p is more than a half, both are positive; when it's less, both are negative; either way the product is positive. Only at exactly a half, no drift at all, is it zero. And no drift doesn't mean a walker standing still: it still jumps about, and it can reach a nearby spot on the first tick. It just doesn't get anywhere on average.

How much heat to get somewhere? Per step forward on average, the heat is the log of 1 plus v over 1 minus v, where v is the speed, and that log is never less than 2v: the difference is zero at no speed and only grows from there, because its slope, 2 over (1 minus v squared), minus 2, is never negative. So walking a distance N costs at least 2N times v in heat and takes N over v in time, and heat times time is at least twice N squared. Halve the speed and the heat about halves while the time doubles; the product never drops below twice the distance squared. Going slowly makes the heat for a given distance smaller and the time longer. These are averages.

The walker is the case I can work by hand. Others have worked other models. Tim Schmiedl and Udo Seifert in 2007 found the best way to drag a bead in a spring-like trap through water from one place to another in a set time, and the least work you can get away with is the drag times the distance squared, divided by the time plus twice the drag over the stiffness: at long times, drag times distance squared over time. Erik Aurell, Carlos Mejía-Monasterio and Paolo Muratore-Ginanneschi in 2011, for a bead that drifts, and Naoto Shiraishi, Ken Funo and Keiji Saito in 2018, for states that jump, showed that changing one spread of chances into another in a set time produces at least a fixed fraction of the square of how far apart the two spreads are, divided by the time and by how lively the motion is. Those are their results under their stated motion, and I cite them as physics. They're about entropy produced, not positive heat on every step: a step that leaves the spread exactly as it was owes nothing on them, and a single step's heat can be negative when the device's own entropy goes up.

And there's a machine that, in principle, owes nothing at all. Edward Fredkin and Tommaso Toffoli, whose gates that lose nothing were in chapter two, also described in 1982 a computer made of perfect billiard balls bouncing their way through a computation, and in principle it could run without wasting anything. Bennett wrote in 2003 that such ballistic machines compute at constant velocity without dissipating any energy, and that they must be kept isolated from heat baths and noise. Keeping one on course against noise means correcting its errors, and whether that brings the waste back I don't settle here; in 2003 Bennett called the thermodynamics of error correction a subject that appears to be in its infancy.

The bead of Jun, Gavrilov and Bechhoefer shows the other half. Put through the same moves as an erasure but with no tilt, so nothing is squashed, its work, carried out to the slow limit, came out at 0.05 kT, give or take 0.03: within two error bars of zero. No floor under it, which is what the assumption says. With the tilt, so two wells really do become one, it came out at 0.71 kT, give or take 0.03, against ln 2, which is 0.693.

So keeping something costs, on top of going at a speed, either a merge, which on Landauer's assumption owes kT ln 2 per bit that no slowness removes, or a state the device has never used before. Taking a fresh state escapes the floor, and doesn't settle how much a real device wastes in finite time. And a finite set of states, where every state has a next one, forces the merge.

\subsection{A simulation in code}

I wanted to see those steps, so I ran a simulation in code. It models a bead in a double well shaped like a quartic curve, and follows the experiment's sequence: lower the barrier for one second, ramp a tilt, take the tilt away, raise the barrier for one second. The well is a shape I chose and the motion is a numerical approximation; it isn't the measured trap, and nothing in it was measured. The modelled bead starts from the thermal spread in that well, equally likely on either side.

Keep three accounts as it moves. Changing the trap while the bead holds still does work on it. The bead moving in a fixed trap hands heat to the water around it. Whatever's left changes the bead's stored energy.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[thiele]
\node[pipe,text width=5.8cm,minimum width=0pt] (a)
  {Old trap\\bead where it was};
\node[pipe,text width=5.8cm,minimum width=0pt,below=1.1cm of a] (b)
  {Changed trap\\bead in the same place};
\node[pipe,text width=5.8cm,minimum width=0pt,below=1.1cm of b] (c)
  {Same changed trap\\bead moved};
\draw[pipearrow] (a.south) -- node[right=8pt]{work in} (b.north);
\draw[pipearrow] (b.south) -- node[right=8pt]{heat out} (c.north);
\end{tikzpicture}

\small Work in minus heat out is the change in stored energy.
Either one can be negative on a single step.
\end{center}

Work in minus heat out is the change in stored energy, on every simulated path, to rounding. That checks the bookkeeping. It doesn't show the time steps are small enough to copy continuous motion exactly.

Here's what came out, with 3,000 paths at each setting and a time step of half a millisecond. Each setting uses a tilt picked to make roughly nine erasures in ten work, and success means ending in the right well. The last two columns average only the successes, in units of kT. The time column is the tilt ramp; the two one-second barrier ramps come on top.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
Tilt time (s) & Success rate & Heat out on success & Work in on success\\
\midrule
2  & 0.909 & 2.517 & 2.501\\
5  & 0.916 & 1.466 & 1.490\\
10 & 0.913 & 1.073 & 1.095\\
20 & 0.899 & 0.830 & 0.907\\
40 & 0.893 & 0.773 & 0.782\\
80 & 0.869 & 0.693 & 0.719\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\footnotesize At the slowest setting the sampling errors are about $0.023\,kT$ for heat and
$0.011\,kT$ for work. They leave out error from the time step and from the model.
\end{center}

Heat and work are close in some rows and less close in others; the difference is measured by the simulation, not put in by hand. Now try to make a floor out of those six points. Fit the successful heat to a constant plus a number divided by the tilt time, and the constant comes out at 0.674 kT, give or take 0.015, near ln 2. Halve the time step and run the six settings again, and it comes out at 0.732 kT, give or take 0.016: it moved by nearly four of its own error bars. The second run also samples different paths, so that comparison mixes sampling error with numerical error. Still, it's enough to stop me treating a fitted constant near ln 2 as a confirmation. The barrier ramps are still one second long, the tilt changes between settings, and the fit doesn't take the whole procedure to a slow limit.

There's a theoretical comparison, and it's a comparison with work. For the continuous thermal model, starting in equilibrium and ending in the same symmetric trap, there are two bounds, both written in terms of the success rate p. Averaging over successes and failures, the work is at least kT times ln 2, plus p ln p, plus (1 minus p) times ln (1 minus p); that's the formula from Bérut and his coauthors' 2012 paper that drops to zero at half. Looking only at the successful paths, the work is at least kT times the log of 2p. Bérut, Artyom Petrosyan and Ciliberto worked that one out in 2013, from a version of Christopher Jarzynski's 1997 equality that sorts paths by how they end, due to Suriyanarayanan Vaikuntanathan and Jarzynski in 2009 and to Ryoichi Kawai, Juan Parrondo and Christian Van den Broeck in 2007. At nine successes in ten the two come to about 0.368 kT and 0.588 kT. Neither is a heat bound for the simulation without also counting its change in stored energy.

The script is in the repository as scripts/bead\_erasure\_sim.py. It reports heat, work, stored-energy change, sampling errors and the fits, and takes a smaller time step for checking them. What it shows is that the slower settings use less work and give off less heat on average. It doesn't find a universal floor, and it doesn't reproduce the laboratory's fitted constant.

\subsection{What's been measured}

Here's the laboratory side in one place, so you can see how near it has come to each piece.
