\endgroup

These experiments test particular memories and procedures, including the symmetric-memory floor and heat against time. They aren't a survey of all physical computation, and they aren't an experimental proof of the counting.

\subsection{Why the rule sits on the raise}

So here's how the heat and the toll fit. The toll charges for what's kept, whichever way it's paid, merge or fresh paper. A price on merges alone charges only the merge, so it misses something kept by taking fresh paper: the machine that keeps every history, chapter two's endless strip, raises its box at zero cost under it. And it charges merges that keep nothing, like a reset that takes a box down. The toll doesn't ask that reset for a unit, and it still merges two states, so in a real device it owes Landauer's floor whenever the bit it lowered was really unknown. The ledger leaves that to physics, along with the waste of going at a speed.

That's the heat. What none of this touched is what keeping takes when the steps themselves are noisy, or quantum, and how much room a memory needs to keep its history without any merge at all. That's the next chapter.

%===========================================================================
\section{Keeping without the heat}
\label{sec:keeping}
%===========================================================================

The last chapter was heat, and every line of it leaned on an assumption with a name. This one doesn't need it. It's the counting that holds whether or not anybody ever measures a joule: what keeping takes when the steps are noisy, or quantum; how much room a memory needs to keep a history without any merge at all; what a machine's counter has to do with heat; and, at the end, a plain list of what you've earned so far and what you haven't. Then one last section that isn't counting at all, kept apart so you can check none of it leaked in.

\subsection{A cell that's written once}

Start with the smallest memory that keeps something. A cell has three states: blank, zero and one. The question is ``is the cell written?'', no on blank and yes on the other two, and a written cell stays written. The move is ``write a 0'': blank goes to zero, and a written cell is left alone.

Draw the arrows. Blank lands on zero. Zero lands on zero. Two arrows meet, so writing a 0 on nothing is, by itself, a merge. The history where the cell was blank and the history where it already held a 0 now leave the same cell behind.

The obvious way round it is a fourth state that remembers: ``0, and it was blank before.'' Now blank lands on a state nothing else lands on. But the remembering state has to go somewhere on the next write too, and it's written, so it has to stay written. Count. There are three written states now, zero, one and the remembering one, and each of them lands on a written state. Blank lands on a written state too. That's four starting places and three places to land, so two share. It's chapter two's chairs, and adding a fifth state, or a fiftieth, only moves the problem along: as long as finitely many states are written, the written ones plus blank are one more than the room they land in.

The only way to write with no merge, once every state has a next one, is a supply of fresh states that never runs out: a new cell of an endless tape for every write, forever. Or a tape that stretches both ways, with the machine stepping one cell along it each tick. Every cell has one before it and one after, nothing ever shares a landing, ``past the start'' goes up on the first step and never comes down, and the machine never returns. That pays at every step by taking a cell it has never used.

The argument leans on one thing, and I want it in the open: the write is a move defined on every state of the cell, written ones included. A write defined only on blank, with the machine simply stopping when it meets a written cell, merges nothing. That's the machine that stops, from chapter one: it buys the 0 by having no next step at all.

And notice what it takes to start. A blank handed to you is a starting condition, not proof that somebody paid heat to make it blank. If a device has to reset an unknown cell so it can be used again, the last chapter's floor can apply to that reset. Precise versions of that floor, like David Reeb and Michael Wolf's from 2014, assume a reservoir that starts thermal and untangled from the memory and a combined motion that merges nothing; their heat bound holds under exactly those conditions. Reading a past or future payment off a known blank would need more assumptions than a known blank supplies.

\subsection{Noisy steps}

Real states are noisy: the bead hops on its own. So take the step to be a table of chances. From each state, there's a chance of landing on each state next, and every row adds to one. A permanent reading is a set of states you never leave once you're in. And ``nothing merges'' has an exact noisy meaning: start with the chance spread evenly over the states, take one step, and it's still spread evenly. In the table, that's every column adding to one, as well as every row.

Can a step like that raise a reading that stays? No, and the count is the chairs again, with chances in place of people. Add up all the chance flowing into the yes-states from everywhere, one column at a time. Each column adds to one, so the total is the number of yes-states. But the yes-states alone already send exactly that much, because they never leave: each of their rows puts all of its one into the yes side. There's nothing left over for any outside state to send in, and chances can't be negative, so every outside state sends in nothing at all. Not a small chance. None.

And it runs the other way. A step that can carry an outside state in can't keep the even spread, so one step from the even spread leaves the chance more bunched than it was. Here's a way to measure bunching. Draw a state twice, independently, using the same chances, and ask whether the two draws match. For two equally likely states the matching pairs have chances a quarter and a quarter, so a match comes up half the time. Put three quarters on one and a quarter on the other, and the matching chances are nine sixteenths and one sixteenth: ten sixteenths. Bunching made a match likelier. Each state contributes its chance squared; add those up and call it the collision weight. The even spread has the least of it, one over the number of states.

Why does a step that lets anything in raise it? Say there are N states and column j adds to c\_j. Starting even, the chance at j after the step is c\_j over N. The columns add up to N, because the N rows each add to one. Now add up (c\_j minus one) squared over all the columns. Multiply it out and use that total, and it comes to the sum of the c\_j squared, minus N. At least one column isn't one, so that's more than zero, so the sum of the c\_j squared is more than N, and dividing by N squared says the new collision weight is more than one over N. The spread got lumpier. Shannon's uncertainty drops too, by the line from the last chapter: the even spread is the only one that holds the full log of N.

And a step that keeps every column at one never makes any spread lumpier. Each new chance is an average of old chances, weighted by a column that adds to one, and an average of squares is never less than the square of the average. Add over the columns, and each old chance squared gets weighted by its whole row, which adds to one. So the new collision weight is at most the old.

The first of those needs only the yes side to be finite; the rest of the machine can be as big as you like. The last needs no finiteness at all. The middle one needs the even spread, so it needs finitely many states. A lopsided memory that keeps its own lopsided spread can't raise a reading that stays either: weight each state by its chance and the same sum comes out.

\subsection{Quantum steps}

The quantum version needs a different picture. Draw a sheet through the middle of a room. Call every direction along the sheet YES, and the direction straight through it NO. Now turn the whole arrangement without stretching it. Can the sheet stay in its own plane while the direction through it turns into that plane? No. A turn keeps right angles. If the sheet still fills its plane after the turn, the direction that was at right angles to all of it is still at right angles to all of it.

Here's what carries over. A quantum state is described by a direction, an arrow with a length. A reversible quantum step keeps lengths and keeps the overlaps between arrows; that's called unitary. The YES part is a flat slice of directions that includes every mix of its own directions. The directions with no overlap with any YES direction are the NO states. And permanence means a YES arrow is still YES after the step.

One detail has to be settled before the right-angle argument works: the old YES directions have to fill the whole YES slice after the step, with nothing missing. Pick a finite list of YES directions that build every YES arrow, none of them built from the others. A unitary step keeps that list independent, because any way of building one image from the others could be run backwards to build the original from the others. The images are still inside YES, there are just as many, so they still fill it.

Now take a NO arrow. Before the step it has no overlap with any YES arrow. After the step those overlaps are still zero, because a unitary step keeps overlaps, and the stepped YES arrows still fill the whole YES slice. So the stepped NO arrow is still NO. Step again and the same holds. A unitary step can't raise a reading that stays.

Finite size was used exactly once: a full finite list of YES directions still fills the slice after the step. Drop it and the endless ladder from the cell above, one rung along each tick, is a unitary step that raises ``past the start'' on the first tick and keeps it forever, taking a rung it has never used every time. So in finite size, keeping a quantum reading takes a step that isn't unitary. Not every step that fails to be unitary squeezes, though. One that keeps the even spread, as pure dephasing and an unread measurement do, can't raise a reading that stays either, by the same column count as the noisy steps. Which way a quantum measurement pays when its outcome is kept isn't settled here. The collapse models people test keep the even spread too, so the heat the experiments look for is the heat of the noise those models add, not a Landauer price on a kept outcome.

\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{p{0.28\textwidth} p{0.64\textwidth}}
The loop & Rauer, Erne, Schweigler, Cataldini, Tajik and Schmiedmayer (2018): in an isolated
gas of thousands of atoms, the phase correlations, one collective measure of its coherence, came
partly back around, twice, each time a little lower. A collective quantity of a closed system
returned; the full state of the gas wasn't shown to return, and in a system that size couldn't be. \\[3pt]
Heat of collapse & Vinante and others (2017): a cantilever at millikelvin shows a force noise
of unknown origin, compatible with the heating one collapse model predicts. Donadi,
Piscicchia, Curceanu, Di\'osi, Laubenstein and Bassi (2021): the radiation a gravity-driven
collapse would give off wasn't seen at Gran Sasso, ruling out the version of that model with
no adjustable settings. These test the noise a collapse model adds, not a Landauer price on a
kept outcome. \\[3pt]
The ceiling on fresh states & Bekenstein's proposed bound uses energy and size; it isn't a
measured count of states. \\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

Bekenstein's bound ties entropy to energy and size under its own physical assumptions. It doesn't hand you a finite list of a device's states. Every count in this book takes finitely many states as an assumption, and nobody has measured where that ceiling is.

\subsection{How much room keeping takes}

So far, keeping meant a merge or fresh states forever. In between there's a question with an exact answer: how long can a finite memory hold off the merge?

Take an operation that sends every value to one fixed value and leaves that value alone, the way a reset does. Hand it N different starting states, one of which already holds the fixed value. Ask it to be repeated T times without any two of the states it passes through landing on the same next state. How many states does the memory need?

Walk it. Each of the N minus one starts that isn't already fixed, followed through T repetitions, passes through T plus one states, and none of them twice: a repeat would walk back, the way chapter two walks back two meeting arrows, to the start being equal to a later state of its own path, and every later state holds the fixed value while the start doesn't. Two of those paths can't share a state either: walk back to the earlier time, and either two different starts are one state, or a start is a state that already holds the fixed value. That's (T + 1) times (N minus 1) states. The start that's already fixed needs one more, and only one: every state on its own path holds the fixed value, so walking back along it finds no contradiction, and its path can fold onto a single state. So the memory needs at least 1 plus (T + 1) times (N minus 1) states.

And that's enough, so the number is exact. One state for the start that's already fixed; the operation leaves it there. For every other start, a row of T plus one states: the first reads the way that start reads, the rest read the fixed way, and the operation walks the row, sending its last state to the common fixed state. For as many repetitions as you asked for, nothing lands together. On the next one, the last state of a row and the fixed state both go to the fixed state: the merge, delayed exactly as long as you paid room for.

With L of the starts already resting, the same count gives N plus T times (N minus L). In bits, a memory of B bits has at most 2 to the B states, so holding off T repetitions with one resting start takes B at least log2 of 1 plus (T + 1) times (N minus 1). The memory grows with the logarithm of how long the history is kept. A fresh state every step isn't a fresh cell every step.

Charles Bennett showed in 1989 that a reversible machine can save room by undoing and redoing its own intermediate work, keeping only enough to get it back. That trades time for space. It doesn't let a machine running on its own, under this count, reuse a state it has been in without a merge.

\subsection{Two levers}

Now a harder version of the same question. Put two reset levers on a box, and use three values on the tabletop: 0, 1 and 2. The first lever wipes only 1 to 0 and leaves 2 alone. The second wipes only 2 to 0 and leaves 1 alone. On the tabletop it doesn't matter which you pull first. Start one full state above each value, and allow every sequence of at most T pulls. Each lever has to keep different states apart, through the promised horizon. How much room does the box need?

Count the zero states first. At each depth, the first lever takes every state already reading zero, and also the start that reads 1, into zero, and keeps them apart. So the zero states grow by at least one per pull, and with the two other starts there are at least T plus 3 states. A single line of zero states reaches that: walk the line with either lever and stop at its end.

But try the levers in opposite orders on that box and the hidden states disagree. Ask for more: the order of the levers must leave the whole machine the same, not just the tabletop. Then the count goes up, to 2T plus 2. Take the simple case first, where the second lever leaves the start above 1 standing still, and the first lever leaves the start above 2 standing still.

Start above 1 and pull the first lever T times. That walks a chain of T zero states, all different: if two were the same, walking back would make the start above 1 equal to a later state that reads 0. Now ask what the second lever does to a state on that chain. Commuting says pulling it after the first lever lands where pulling it before would, and before, you were at the start above 1, where the second lever stands still. So the second lever holds every state on the first chain still. The same reasoning with the levers swapped walks a second chain of T zero states from the start above 2, and the first lever holds every one of those still.

Can the chains share a state? Everywhere on the first chain but its last state, the first lever moves you on; everywhere on the second chain, the first lever holds you still. No state does both, so nothing on the first chain except its end can be on the second, and the same with the levers swapped. The most they can share is their two ends, as one state. If they don't share it, that's 2T zero states, and with the starts above 1 and 2, 2T plus 2. If they do, that's 2T minus 1 zero states, and now the zero start can't be on either chain: if it were, pulling from it would reach the shared end within the horizon, and the lever that walks that chain would send both the end and the state just before it to the end, two states landing together before the promise ran out. So the zero start is one more, and with the starts above 1 and 2 it's 2T plus 2 again.

When the idle lever moves the hidden state instead of holding it still, the path it traces above 1 or 2 costs states of its own. It runs for a while and then, if it repeats, falls into a loop. Switch levers there, and every pull spent getting into the loop is one pull fewer for that chain, so the trade is one state for one. Commuting still pushes any sharing between the two chains out to their far ends, because a shared state would have to come back to itself after a full turn of the other loop. The count comes out at 2T plus the longer loop, and when both loops are a single state, the zero start adds the last one, as before. Either way, 2T plus 2, and the exhaustive search on the small horizons agrees.

There's a box that reaches it. At horizon one it has four places, and you can write every arrow down:

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccccc@{}}
Place & Tabletop & Pull the first lever & Pull the second lever & A start? \\ \hline
$a_0$ & 1 & end & $a_0$ & yes \\
$b_0$ & 2 & $b_0$ & $b_1$ & yes \\
$b_1$ & 0 & $b_1$ & end & yes \\
end & 0 & end & end & no \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

On the three starts each column lands on three different places. On the whole four-place box each column has a repeat. And both orders of the levers agree on every row. So the promise that holds through a horizon and the promise that holds everywhere really are different promises: ask each lever to keep states apart everywhere, forever, and the count goes up once more, to 2T plus 3, which two separate arms running on forever reach at every horizon.

Change the tabletop and the room can fit differently. Give it two bits, with one lever clearing the first and the other clearing the second. Lay out a grid of points (i, j) with i and j whole numbers from zero up, plus one separate blank state. One lever adds one to i, the other to j, and both leave blank alone. Read a grid point as whether i is zero and whether j is zero. Start at blank and at the three grid points (0, 0), (1, 0) and (0, 1).

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, every node/.style={align=center,font=\small}]
\node (a) at (0,0) {grid $(0,0)$\\reads both bits set};
\node (b) at (4.4,0) {grid $(1,0)$\\reads first bit cleared};
\node (c) at (0,-2.7) {grid $(0,1)$\\reads second bit cleared};
\node (d) at (4.4,-2.7) {grid $(1,1)$\\reads both cleared};
\draw[->] (a)--node[above] {first lever}(b);
\draw[->] (a)--node[left] {second}(c);
\draw[->] (b)--node[right] {second}(d);
\draw[->] (c)--node[below] {first lever}(d);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Both orders reach the very same grid point, not just the same reading. Each pull adds one to i plus j, so after t pulls you can reach exactly the triangle of points with i plus j at most t plus 1. Its rows hold 1, 2, up to t plus 2 points. Add them and the blank: 1 plus (t + 2)(t + 3) over 2. And that's the least any box with this tabletop can do. Here's why. Each reading with exactly one bit cleared starts with one state, and every pull its lever sends that reading's own states, plus at least one state with both bits set, into it, kept apart: so it gains at least one state per pull, and after t pulls holds at least t plus 1. The reading with both bits cleared starts with one state too, and at the pull after depth j, one lever sends its own states and a whole one-cleared group of at least j plus 1 into it, kept apart: so it gains at least j plus 1. Add those gains from the first pull to the t-th and it holds at least 1 plus t(t + 1) over 2. Add the one state with both bits set and the two one-cleared groups, and the total is 1 plus (t + 2)(t + 3) over 2: the grid's own count. The three-value box needed extra room to commute. This one fits its best count with commuting built in.

All of those counts are checked by exhaustive search on the small horizons, where a computer can try every box.

\subsection{Keeping every history}

There's one way to make every step merge nothing, and it's the first way out of chapter two, put inside a machine. Keep the previous state on a list: each step, push where you were onto the front of the list. The step you can see is the same as before, but the whole state now carries its past. Two different whole states can't land on the same one, because popping the list takes you back. And the list gets one longer every step, so the run never comes back to a whole state it's been in. That's the fresh-state way of keeping, and on any memory you can build, the list fills it, and then the merge is back.

So ``this step merged, so it gave off heat'' can't be said of the step you can see by itself. It's a sentence about the whole state, including whatever history is kept, and a physical model has to say what's kept.

\subsection{The counter and the heat}

Does a machine's counter track heat? Here's the one test I think is good discipline, and it's a test of whether two assumptions can even hold together. Say you want both of these: every step you've chosen to count gives off at least one unit, and nothing gives off more than its charge. Can both be true at once? Exactly when every step you've chosen to count is charged at least one. If one is, take the heat to be the charge itself: both hold. If a counted step is charged zero, it would have to give off at least one and at most zero.

I should say how little that shows. The model that passes the test just takes the heat to be the charge, so it only shows the two assumptions don't contradict each other. It doesn't show the counter tracks anything real. Still, it bites. Point it at a machine that computes at no cost underneath and counts every step that collapses some yes-or-no question, and it fails: such a machine has free moves that merge, and no heat can be at least one and at most zero. A machine that prices the moves that raise its record and leaves its other merges free has both assumptions for the toll's steps, and not for the rest. The hardware can give off heat on those free merges, off the ledger. The ledger counts the record and nothing else. A unit of cost is not a joule.

Three statements are in play here, and each points its own way. Energy at a scale, a unit of cost times so many joules, is a conversion; it points nowhere. ``Nothing gives off more than its charge'' puts the charge above the heat: an upper bound. Landauer puts the heat above a loss of uncertainty: a lower bound. The test above says the assumptions can live together. That's compatibility, not evidence.

Is there a heat prediction the permanent record makes that Landauer alone doesn't? No, and the reason is short. The smallest case, one state already yes and one flipping in, is ``set a bit to one whatever it held,'' which is the reset Landauer described and Bérut's group measured. The general bound, for squeezing m plus k equally likely states into m, is what Landauer's assumption gives for that squeeze. What the counting adds is the structural half: on a finite machine, a reading that stays forces the raising move to merge. It doesn't say the state coming in is one of the two that meet, and it doesn't supply the spread or the physical conditions a heat bound needs. Supply those and Landauer prices the uncertainty actually removed. The counting and the physics do different jobs.

There's one more way to put receipts next to merges, and it's exact. Draw the arrows of one run, and put a receipt beside each state the run leaves from: charge on entry into a parked state, and let the parked state idle for free. Now mark every landing place that two different states in the run lead to, and count each mark once. Ask for one thing: whenever two different states the run leaves from land together, at least one of them pays. Then the marks never outnumber the receipts. Every marked landing has a paid state behind it, and one paid state can't stand behind two marks, because it only goes one place.

Try it with no going to yes and yes going to itself. Charge one at no and zero at yes. Entry pays the one, and every idle step after pays nothing: the merge is covered and the parked machine stays parked for free. Now ask that parked yes-state how many merges you've watched. After one step you've seen only no leave: no merge yet. After two you've seen no and yes both lead to yes: one merge. The state in your hand is yes both times, and it can't tell you which. Add a clock, and the two endings become yes at 1 and yes at 2, so you can read off the count. But draw the new machine, with the clock in it, and the clock keeps those states apart. Keep both drawings on the table so you can see which arrows you're counting.

\subsection{What you're entitled to}

Let me total it up: what you've earned the right to conclude, and where there's still a way out.

You've earned this, with nothing but one next state for every state: on any machine run by one rule, finite or endless, a run that raises a reading and keeps it up either merges two of its own states or never repeats one. You've earned this: on a finite machine, a move that raises a reading either forgets something or takes a reading down somewhere else. That's chapter three's merge or take back. You've earned this: on a finite machine where a reading, once up, stays up, raising it makes two arrows meet, and if it raises several no-states at once, the yes-states together with the newcomers land on that many fewer places than there are of them. That doesn't say each newcomer shares. And you've earned this: if two arrows meeting is charged, the toll follows, and if it's charged per halving, the size of the toll follows too. That's chapter three's rule, derived, with every assumption named.

Now what you haven't earned, so you don't assume it. You haven't earned that forgetting costs anything in the world; that's Landauer, measured for beads and argued over in general, and here it's an assumption with a name. Even granting it, it asks no heat of a run whose state is known, so what the physics forces is a floor on a device that has to work whatever it's holding. You haven't earned that any machine you'd want to build is of this kind, either, and there are two easy ways to miss. A machine that keeps its running total inside its own state, as a number with no ceiling, has endlessly many states, so the finite argument fails for it as a whole. And you can't patch that with a ceiling: a total that only climbs, in finite memory, either stops at its top, and then two different histories leave the same top value, which is a merge, or rolls over to zero, and then it doesn't only climb. So any running total a finite machine keeps for itself is honest up to its width and no further. And a machine whose price list charges the raise but lets some other step merge two states for free raises its box honestly and still doesn't charge for forgetting in general. Either one puts a machine outside these arguments as they stand, though a finite piece of it can still sit inside.

The smallest finite piece I could build where every condition can be seen to hold is this. Four slots, each with a flag: eight states. Three kinds of move. Stamp sets the flag and goes to the next slot, cost one. Jump goes to a chosen slot, cost two. Next goes to the next slot, cost zero. Check it with me. It's finite. The flag never comes down on any move. Next forgets nothing: every state lands somewhere different. Stamp lands at most two states together, a flag up and a flag down from the same slot, so one halving, and it costs one. Jump lands all four slots with the same flag together, two halvings, and it costs two. So every move's price is exactly the number of halvings in its biggest pile.

And you haven't earned anything about what the flag means. Chapter two's chairs don't know what they're counting. The cost is for raising a flag that stays up. Whether raising it ever amounts to standing behind a real fact was the subject of chapters eight through twelve.

One more thing, because it should nag at you. ``Permanent'' was chosen, by me. Why should a flag stay up? Merge or take back is the start of an answer: on a finite machine that charges for every merge, a flag that goes up for nothing is a flag the same move takes down somewhere, and a record that can be silently taken back is exactly what the clay ball, the write-once disk and the hash chain were all built against. That's durability, one of chapter four's five jobs, and it's the one I chose.

And ``permanent'' is an idealization, which I should say before a physicist does. No physical record lasts forever. A bit behind an energy barrier leaks over it at some rate, however slow, and a closed finite system comes back around, which is Zermelo's point from chapter two. The arguments that start from a flag that stays up need it to stay up on every state the machine can reach, including states a given run never visits, forever. Weaken that to ``it stays up on the states that actually turn up, for as long as anyone cares,'' and merge or take back says what goes wrong: the move could take the flag down somewhere the machine could go and never does, and then it isn't forced to merge at all.

One piece of a time-span version I can hand you. Take one move and repeat it from one start, forever. On a finite machine the states that run passes through are finitely many, so it falls into a loop. If the move forgot nothing on those states, it would just shuffle them, and a shuffle brings every one of them back around, the start included, where the flag was down. So if the flag goes up along that run and stays up from some point on, the move merges two states the run itself visits. No permanence is used anywhere in that.

Runs that mix different moves split two ways. If the moves are picked from outside, in any order, the answer is no. Take two states, one reading no and one reading yes. One move swaps them; the other does nothing. Run the swap once and then the do-nothing move forever: the flag goes up at the first step and stays up for good, and neither move merges anything anywhere. The swap would take the flag down from yes if it ever ran there; on this run it never does. But if the machine is closed, picking its next move from its own state, the run is one map repeated, and the single-move argument applies as it stands. And if the moves come in a pattern that repeats, one phrase played over and over, treat the whole phrase as one move at the states where each playing of it begins, as long as the flag was down somewhere after the repeating started. It merges two of those, and the first move where their paths meet merges two states the run visits.

\subsection{The shape of the second law}

Here's a comparison that's easy to read as more than it is, so I'll say first what it doesn't do. It doesn't give entropy's numbers. It doesn't derive Landauer. It makes no physical claim. It puts two drawings side by side and points at the parts that line up, and every paragraph says which kind it is.

Shown, and only about the model. A window groups states: every state gives one picture, and the states that give the same picture form that picture's pile. A physicist calls the picture a macrostate and the states under it its microstates; the word is different and the drawing is the same.

Shown, and only about the model. A Thiele machine's record never falls; that's in the definition. And it never climbs for free: the toll charges every climb, and the counter that adds the charges never goes down either. So a Thiele machine carries two quantities that only climb along every run, the record and the counter, and the first can't move without the second moving too.

Shown, and only about the model. Keeping something costs room or a merge. On finitely many states a reading that rises and stays rises by a merge, and when each unit of price pays for one halving, the bits a run squeezes out are bounded by what it paid. A noisy step that keeps the even spread can't raise a reading that stays.

A thesis, not shown. Put those beside the second law of thermodynamics and the shapes line up: a coarse description, the many fine states under each coarse one, a quantity that only goes one way, and a rule that the one-way change has a price. My guess is that this isn't a coincidence, and that the second law, read as a statement about what a coarse description can keep, is the toll seen from the physics side. I can't prove that. The model has no temperature, no energy and no probability unless an argument puts one in, and the bridge from a unit of cost to a joule is an assumption with critics, named in the last chapter. If the match is real, somebody has to show it with physics, and that somebody isn't this book.

\subsection{At the bottom: what I suspect and can't prove}

This is underneath the arguments, so read it with the lights on. None of it is shown, and I'm not going to dress it up. It's why I care, and it's here, after the arguments and apart from them, so you can check that none of it got into them.

Physics has a word for the moment a question closes: measurement. And the people who thought hardest about it keep describing it in this book's shape. They ran together for me until I put them on one picture. Flip a coin in a crowded room. Before it lands, it could go either way. After, it's settled, somebody chalks it on a board, and the result gets copied out into the world, the light and the air, so many times that it can't be taken back and everybody who looks agrees. Five pieces: the question, the landing, the chalk mark, the copies, the agreeing. Each person leans on a different one. Zurek, in chapter nineteen, leans on the copies. Rovelli's coin, in chapter six, has to hit the ground, so his piece is the landing.

Niels Bohr, the Danish physicist whose institute in Copenhagen was where quantum mechanics got argued into shape, leans on the mark. In a talk at Columbia University's bicentennial in October 1954, printed in his 1958 book Atomic Physics and Human Knowledge, he said ``every atomic phenomenon is closed in the sense that its observation is based on registrations obtained by means of suitable amplification devices with irreversible functioning such as, for example, permanent marks on a photographic plate'': a door that swings one way. I first found that line quoted in a 2026 paper by Mauro Dorato and Jan Faye, and their quotation runs on into a clause about water drops forming in a cloud chamber that isn't on Bohr's page. The page is worth reading yourself. John Wheeler, the Princeton physicist who made the name ``black hole'' stick, went further in a 1989 talk in Tokyo and leaned on the question: ``every it,'' he said, ``every particle, every field of force, even the spacetime continuum itself,'' gets ``its function, its meaning, its very existence'' from ``the apparatus-elicited answers to yes or no questions, binary choices, bits.'' He boiled it down to ``it from bit.'' As I read him, the coin has no face until something asks and something writes the answer down. How literally he meant that, I can't tell. And Peirce, from chapter nine, long before any of them, leans on the agreeing: the settling worth trusting is the kind that has to answer to everybody. Laid out like that, they pair up. Bohr and Rovelli lean on a step that only goes one way, in the plate and in the coin. Zurek and Peirce lean on many onlookers who agree, one with light and one with people.

Lay that next to everything in this book and the parts line up: a check that could have come out either way, a result that's now settled, a price paid in forgetting or in fresh states, a record that stays. I suspect that isn't a coincidence. I suspect the event this book prices on a machine is the same kind of event physics calls a measurement, turning up in a different place. The forgetting is my step: Bohr only says irreversible, and getting from a record that stays to a merge or to fresh states is chapter two's counting. Push on it and it gives at Zurek, for the reason chapter nineteen gave: copying onto fresh light merges nothing and uses up fresh states, which is chapter six's look that owes no floor. So physics' own word lands on both sides of the line: the chalk on the paying side, the copies on the borrowing side. The landing I'd put with the chalk, and when chapter six pushed on Rovelli's paper it slid toward the borrowing side, the side of finding out. So two pieces already stick out, and I'll leave them sticking out. That's as far as I'll take it: a suspicion.

John Bell asks for precision in those words. He was the Northern Irish physicist at CERN, and in 1990 he wrote a short, furious essay called ``Against 'measurement','' with a list of words he said have no place in a physics that wants to be precise. On the list: system, apparatus, environment, irreversible, information, and measurement, which he called the worst of all. I used half of them in the last few paragraphs. To turn the suspicion into a physical claim, each of those words would need a stated meaning and a tie to the device. The arguments in this book work with the machines I've defined; anything physical says which further assumptions it uses.

That's the whole of it: what's counted, what's assumed, what's chosen, and one suspicion kept apart. The last chapter lays those out side by side.

%===========================================================================
\section{What's shown, what's chosen, what's argued}
\label{sec:shown-chosen}
%===========================================================================

This is the last chapter, and it's the one where everything gets laid out on the table at once, sorted into piles, so you can see which pile each piece came from. Page one promised that: found, assumed, chosen. Every chapter since has said which pile it was adding to. Here they all are.

Start with the smallest machine, because it carries the whole book in three layers. Two boxes, one move that sends both to YES. What I found is the merge inside it: two states landing on one, which counting forces on anything that keeps a distinction on finitely many states, and which nobody chose. What I chose is the price: one unit on the move that climbs, a choice that could have gone another way. What I assumed, and only where an argument says so, is the heat: that a merge of states the machine really could have been in costs something in the world. That's Landauer's, not mine, and not shown here. Everything below is one of those three, or it's a thesis, which is a fourth thing, and I'll say which every time.

\subsection{What it is, and what it's for}

You've had the model since page one: a thing, moves, a way to tell how settled the thing is where settled never goes back, some run from a start you pick that settles it more, a price on every move that's nothing or more and never a refund, and the toll. A counter and a window come in when an argument needs them. You can't buy it in a box. It isn't a device, a chip or an instruction set; those are things you'd build to carry it.

So if it computes nothing new, and it doesn't, what's it for? The same thing other ways of counting a computation are for. The random-access machine, in the form Stephen Cook and Robert Reckhow gave it as a way of counting cost in 1973, computes exactly what Turing's does, and it's worth having because it counts steps the way real memory is used. Andrew Yao's cell-probe model, from 1981, counts only how many times you look into memory and leaves all the computing in between out of the count. Alok Aggarwal and Jeffrey Vitter's model, from 1988, counts only the blocks moved between a slow memory and a fast one. None of them computes one function more than Turing's machine. Each is worth having for what it counts. The Thiele Machine's own count is what a run made the case, and what that cost. Look at a computer through a window that drops that, and you get the ordinary picture, chapter four's coin covered by your hand. Read a computer through whether it has stopped, and it's already a Thiele machine, with the price left out.

\subsection{The objection a theorist makes in four seconds}

The toll is a floor, and a floor is easy to meet. Charge one unit for every move, whatever it does, and the toll holds for any reading you like, because every move that settles something is a move, and every move costs one. So any computer with a step counter beside it obeys the toll, for every yes-or-no question you could ask about it. A clock passes.

That's exactly why the toll isn't the whole definition. Charging every move doesn't make a machine a Thiele machine; the reading still has to never go back, and some run still has to settle it. And it means the toll, by itself, only puts a floor under the settling move. It doesn't tie the rest of the bill to anything kept. One thing in this book does tie it down, and it's a ceiling: ask that a run's total never be more than the number of times the box went up. Chapter eleven showed that with a price that can depend on where the step starts, the floor and that ceiling leave exactly one way of charging: one unit per raise, nothing else. With a price per move name, the way page one defines it, no machine whose box ever goes up meets both, because the raising move, played again from YES, raises nothing and still costs what it costs. A clock fails the ceiling the first time it charges a tick that raised nothing.

Two more things fall out of the same picture. The toll doesn't care how strong the machine is. A machine is called Turing-complete when it can imitate a Turing machine; the toll never asks. Stronger, weaker, exactly as strong: bring the parts, charge the settling move, and you're in. It was never about what you can compute. It's about what a move is allowed to do for free.

And nothing is in scope just for being a computation. A Turing machine's run isn't an example of anything in this book because it's a run. It becomes one when somebody says what the states are, which ones read yes, what each move costs, and checks the toll. Until then the arguments say nothing about it, and I won't pretend they do. What chapter two found has no such condition, though: every run of anything with one next state for every state lands somewhere new every tick, makes two arrows meet once, or goes round a loop through its start and keeps nothing.

\subsection{Bolting two together}

Put two Thiele machines side by side: each move is a move of one or the other, and the costs add. Is the pair a Thiele machine? It depends on how you read the pair.

Read it as ``either box is up,'' or ``both are.'' Then the pair's reading can only go up when one of the boxes goes up, that move is a move of that machine, and that machine's toll already charged it. So the pair obeys the toll. Read through ``both are,'' a run that starts with both down and ends with both up raises each box once, each raise is charged by its own machine, so the run costs at least two. From a start with one box already up, one raise is enough, so two is a floor only from the clean start.

Now read it as ``the first is up and the second is down.'' That reading comes down the moment the second box goes up, and a Thiele machine's reading never comes down. The pair is out.

So some readings of a pair keep the toll and some don't, and you can tell which by asking whether the pair's reading can go up without one of the parts going up. Whether every way of building one machine out of others keeps the toll is a bigger question than this book answers.

\subsection{Steps that branch}

Everything so far gave every state one next state. Plenty of models don't: a machine that can guess, a lambda calculus term you can reduce in whichever place you like, a system of rewrite rules, a proof read as one rule applied at a time. Nothing earlier was stated for those, so here are the two arguments, both short.

The first is chapter two's chairs on one choice of next step. Say only finitely many states read yes, none of them can step to a state reading no, each has at least one next state, and some state reading no can step into the yes side. Pick one next state for each yes-state, and the step in from outside. That's one more step than there are yes-states, each leaving a different state and landing on a yes-state, so two land on the same one. It needs the yes side finite; Hilbert's hotel keeps a reading with nobody sharing.

The second follows one run, however its choices fall. Take the step where the reading goes up, and say it stays up for the rest of that run. If the run is in the same state at two times after the raise, step both times back together. They can't stay equal all the way back past the raise, because the state just before the raise reads no and every later one reads yes. So at the first pair that differs, two different states of that very run step onto one. After the raise, the run makes two arrows meet or never repeats a state, on any number of states. Before the raise it can come back to a state and then leave it for good with nothing meeting, which one rule can't do. A state with no next step at all is chapter one's machine that stops: a reading kept by stopping is kept with nothing meeting.

For branching steps the first argument says less than it looks. In the lambda calculus, any term N is one step away from two different terms: apply ``do nothing'' to it, or wrap it in a function that throws its argument away and hand that function anything. Two different states sharing a next state is no news there. The second argument is the one that carries weight.

Where it all stops. Steps in continuous time have no next state to count, and nothing here covers them. Noisy steps where endlessly many states read yes, and quantum steps whose kept part has endlessly many directions, get none of the finite arguments; the endless ladder keeps a reading with nothing meeting, a fresh rung every tick. What still reaches them is the single run: the second argument covers any one run of them. And a truth isn't a process at all. That a statement is provable is a fact with no step in it; the arguments are about a run that derives it.

\subsection{What doesn't follow}

Some things look like they follow and don't. Here they are, so you don't carry them out of the book by accident.

The counter isn't a heat meter. Once you pick the start and the price list, the number is forced by arithmetic, and it isn't energy. To read it as energy you'd need to say which states carry information, how the charge relates to what a device gives off, and that both of those can hold at once; chapter twenty-one did the last part and showed it only means the two don't contradict each other.

No question about what computers can do in a given time follows. Whether P equals NP is one of the seven problems the Clay Mathematics Institute put a million dollars on, at a meeting in Paris in May 2000. One of the seven has been declared solved since: the Poincaré conjecture, by Grigori Perelman, who posted his proof in 2002 and 2003. A second has a claim on it. On 8 September 2026 OpenAI announced that one of its models, run as about ten thousand agents for about eighty-eight hours, had produced a proof, written out for a proof checker, that a three-dimensional fluid flow with a smooth force pushing on it can break down in finite time. That's one of the four answers the official statement of the Navier-Stokes problem accepts. As of October 2026 other mathematicians haven't finished checking it, there's a dispute over credit with Tristan Buckmaster and Levent Alpöge, who were working on a closely related result, and the Clay Institute's own page lists the problem as active, not solved. P versus NP has no such claim. If a two-state clash on my machine settled it, I'd be owed a million dollars. Nobody owes me anything. A window that loses a box is a fact about that window. A question like P versus NP is about everything every possible program can do within a budget, and nothing in these clashes reaches that far.

The choice of event isn't forced. The pointer thesis is a thesis. The cup over any machine is a theorem, and it says the event is a choice.

The two walls stay two walls. Chapter seven's is about what a watcher can't get back through a window; chapter seventeen's is about what no inside procedure can decide. Linking them would take an argument of its own, and this book doesn't have one.

The entitlement count in chapter twelve is handed its groups and its covering. It doesn't dig them out of an arbitrary run.

Records with chances on them don't inherit the uniqueness of the counter. Two honest random records can have the same branches and the same charges and still give those branches different weights, one choosing evenly and one two to one. The price list doesn't fix the chances.

Every guarantee about an established record is about runs: any list of moves, from a clean start, on a step that does what it says, one writer at a time. Whoever sets the start, the wire that carries the report out and the tools that built the step are outside that fence.

The five real systems are copies of pieces, with their limits named in chapter eighteen. None of them is a check of the running system. And I looked for a result about real systems that needs the record and is ready to use. I don't have one.

\subsection{The ledger}

Here's every claim the book makes, one line each, with what kind of claim it is and where it lives. If a line here ever says more than its chapter does, the chapter is right and the line is wrong.

Ledger is a good word for it, and it's older than bookkeeping. Around 1400 a ledger was a big book that lay in one place, a large church breviary, from the old verb for to lay; the account-book sense turns up in the 1580s. Staying put is the job here too. This is the one place where every claim lies still, so you can come back and find it where you left it.

There's a way to use it that takes five minutes. Pick the sentence in this book you trust least. Find its line. Read the kind before anything else. Then go to the chapter and check that the line says no more than the chapter does. If it says more, you've found my mistake.

\begingroup\small

\endlastfoot

``Shown'' means shown on the page, under the conditions the chapter states, and checked by machine on small cases where a computer can try them all. ``Chosen'' means I picked it and said so. ``Assumed'' means it's somebody's claim about the world that an argument leans on, named. ``Cited'' means it's somebody else's result, sourced. ``Thesis'' means it ties a definition to the world and no argument can settle it. ``Not claimed'' means it looks like it follows, and it doesn't.

\subsection{Whose is what}

None of the pieces is mine alone, so here's the short form of who did what, with the long form in the appendix.

The toll isn't only my preference: on a finite machine a reading nothing turns off can only be turned on by a step that makes two arrows meet, so if that's priced, raising it costs; and a raise where nothing meets needs endlessly many yes-states. The counting under it is the finite cousin of the recurrence Zermelo threw at Boltzmann in 1896, and the price on a merge comes from Landauer's reset. Others had already priced merges. Thomas Ouldridge and David Wolpert put a physical price on them for finite automata in 2023. Ming Li and Paul Vitányi in 1996 put a unit price on each bit a computation erases, or takes in, irreversibly, and Erik Demaine and his coauthors in 2016 charged for instructions that destroy information. What's here is the toll that kind of price forces on a reading that stays.

That a price worked out from a window can't tell apart two steps the window shows the same is a fact about functions far older than this book; what's here is the pricing form of it. That a box driven by the computation that never comes down is a cup on one event, over any machine, uses old pieces too: latches and history-keeping variables are old, and the result covers only boxes that never come down. And the move of guaranteeing something about every run, which an established record leans on, isn't mine at all. It's the reference monitor's: James Anderson's 1972 study for the US Air Force, which asked for a mechanism that is ``tamper proof,'' ``always invoked,'' and ``small enough to be subject to analysis and tests.'' Fred Schneider made the idea precise for monitors that watch a run in 2000, and it was proved for monitors checked at every instruction by Arthur Azevedo de Amorim and his coauthors in 2015. What I put under it is a definition, and its idea isn't new either: a check tied to the claim it looked at, with the toll charged on top.

What's mine is the putting together, and the bet that it belongs in the step.

\subsection{The Thiele thesis}

One more claim ties the counting to the world, the way Church and Turing's ties computing to it, and I'll state it as what it is, a thesis. I call it the Thiele thesis: every physical process that computes with final answers has an exact description as a machine run by one rule, with a next state for every state, written down in a way a program can follow, on which the answer is a reading that no run from any input ever turns off, on inputs accepted and rejected alike.

No argument settles it, because ``a physical process that computes'' isn't something you can do mathematics on. A model of computation is, and for a model written down as states, a step and a reading that stays, what keeping takes needs no thesis: it's chapter two. The thesis is the tie between the two, and it's about what a physical computation pays for what it keeps.

There's an established road to that kind of tie, with its conditions in the open. Robin Gandy in 1980 wrote down principles for mechanisms, and Pablo Arrighi and Gilles Dowek restated his argument in a simpler form in 2012: sameness across space and time, finitely many local states, a bound on how fast anything travels, and a start that differs from a quiet background only in a finite region. From those, the evolution is computable. Those are real assumptions about the world, and they don't pick an event or a price in joules. I'm not asserting them of every physical system.

What can be shown sits on either side of the thesis. On one side, chapter two applies to every such description: a run that raises the answer merges two of its own states or never visits a state twice. On the other side, look only at which inputs it accepts, and the thesis says something you can check: every set a physical process accepts with final answers is one a program can list. Here's how. Run the description from every input, a step at a time across all of them, taking turns, and write an input down the first time its reading says yes. Nothing ever turns an answer off, so a yes seen once is the answer. And the other way: a set a program can list is accepted by a machine that searches the list for its input and then steps from its stopped state to itself forever, read through ``stopped,'' which nothing turns off. That builds a machine with the same accepted set. It doesn't build an exact description of the original process. And nothing about how the keeping is paid survives that view: the same search held at its stop by a step to itself, which merges, or by a counter that keeps ticking, which takes fresh states, accepts the same inputs.

``Final'' has to cover the rejected inputs too, and here's why. Build a machine that starts at no, reads yes while program n is still running, and falls to no for good if it stops. It reads yes for good on exactly the programs that never stop. No program lists those: one that did, run beside the plain search for a stop, would tell you for every program whether it stops, and chapter seventeen showed nothing can.

\subsection{What isn't settled}

Four things, each with what would settle it.

Whether Landauer's critics are right. That would leave every argument standing and one assumption unowned by the world. What settles it is measurements of real devices, and an argument about what the principle means in general that both sides accept.

Whether the established record really is the pointer event. What settles it is chapter nineteen's three comparisons, run on systems people actually use.

Whether the toll is the right thing to price at all. The five real systems mostly built a wall or a penalty for contradicting yourself. I can't prove a toll is better, and I won't pretend the five say so.

And whether anything sits below a split with a one-way door, by a ruler someone can state. The arrow road met mine at the bottom. If you find a smaller start, it would change this book.

\subsection{Back to the warehouse}

Walk the warehouse from chapter eight one more time. Two computers, the same sorted list on both. On the one with the established record, a different part of the state tells you a check happened and a matching commitment followed it, and the rules keep that tied to the edition that was checked. Put your hand over that part of the machine and the difference between the two vanishes from where you're standing. Change the list and you can watch the old check go stale. Those are different operations with different consequences, and you can follow each one with your finger.

Carry a report out of the warehouse and you've got another question: what connects the report to the machine? The box doesn't answer it. A window, a copy, a second machine and a reader each bring relations of their own, and the chapters asked, one at a time, which of those hold.

At the bottom of all of it is one distinction made to stay: a bead in a dip, a cell written, a question closed. What that settling keeps, what it takes, and what the price I chose charges for it. What I'm arguing is that keeping is part of computation, not something you bolt on afterwards, and that the price belongs in the step. The box and its toll are the part of that I've worked all the way through. The rest is yours to push on.

%===========================================================================
\phantomsection\section*{Afterword: how I got here}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Afterword: how I got here}
\label{sec:origin}
%===========================================================================

What I am is curious, past the point of good sense.

I pick a thing up and cannot set it down until I have turned it over enough times to see how it holds together, and this time it did not let go.

I am not a programmer and I was never formally taught any of this.

I was not trying to build a new model of computation.

I would not have known what one was if it bit me.

I was trying to build a 3D renderer.

There was no plan behind it.

Just a pull to make something real with my own hands, and the kind of stubbornness that will not let a thing go until it stands up on its own.

I have worked on it in off hours every day since.

Once I started digging in, two things came clear fast. I knew nothing about computers, and the field was already saturated, every obvious road paved and crowded. So I needed an angle nobody else was standing on, and the angle I had wasn't a strategy. It was just the only way my head works. I did not know what compositional meant. I had never heard of category theory. I called the thing I was building digital DNA, because that is what it felt like in my hands, and I thought about it in the only language I had: gravity pipes and directions and vectors, things that were linear and somehow not linear at the same time, structure that flowed and joined and came apart without my owning one proper word for any of it. I built a custom rendering pipeline out of those intuitions, by feel, throwing out pieces that did not sit right even when I could not have told you the rule they were breaking. And then, the way I always do, I went looking for what I was actually doing, and found out it already had a name. An old one. A deep one. What I had been reaching for in the dark was composition. It was category theory, a whole field I had never once heard of, sitting there describing the exact shapes I had been shoving around. The moment that landed, the rendering pipeline stopped being a rendering pipeline and started turning into a categorical engine, and I started swallowing the real theory as fast as I could find the words for things I had already built.

I took it one concept at a time, and I would not move to the next until I could watch the current one actually run. Category. Object. Morphism. Functor. Monoidal product. It came together in pieces and never in chapters, the way a picture resolves out of static. The Yoneda lemma (a result buried deep in category theory) was the piece that made the rest snap into place.

Then within weeks I found out the engine I had built to draw pictures was general enough to run a physics simulation on the exact same primitives, without my rewriting any of it. I wrote Newton's laws as a program in it, half expecting nothing, and it ran. I want to be careful about what that was and what it was not, because this is exactly the kind of thing that gets misread. It was not me deriving physics, and I am not claiming the machine explains gravity. It was the compositional structure being general enough to express a physics simulation the same way it had expressed a renderer, and that generality is what told me the structure was the point and the pixels never were. Somewhere in those weeks I crossed from debugging why one pixel would not show up to understanding that I had quietly built a categorical computing engine, and that the rendering had only ever been the first thing I aimed it at. The pixel did eventually show up, for what it is worth.

I kept going, because by then I could not not keep going. The night the engine first ran a program declaratively, meaning I told it what should happen and let it work out the how without my spelling out each step, I told my wife, Julie, I thought I was onto something. She said okay.

Then I hit a wall, and it is the wall this whole book is about. Every tool I had for settling questions about what I'd built could settle the small ones and fell over on the exact ones I needed answered, which left me staring down an infinite hallway of guess-and-test with no proof waiting at the end of it, ever. One night, not even working, I was watching a video about Flatland, the old story about a creature living in two dimensions who cannot so much as picture a third. And it landed on me sideways, all at once, the way the real ones always do. I said it out loud: I get it now. There is another axis. Classical computation only ever sees what it can read and write off its own surface, and there is a whole direction it is blind to. It isn't broken. It was just never built with an eye that points that way. That's how it landed that night, and it didn't survive in that form: the direction turned out to be one an ordinary computer can reach, and what the usual rules leave out is the price (page one says where the axis ended up, and chapter fifteen says how much of that night held when I pushed). The two computers on page one, and again in chapter eight, are my attempt to show you that blindness with something you can hold. That same night I gave the idea a name: the Thiele Machine. The name began as a working title and became the name of the model. It carries the larger claim plainly: the machine treats structural state as part of computation.

What survived the whole trip from there to here is not the renderer. Not one piece of it, and good riddance. What survived was a single commitment I made early and never once let anyone talk me out of: structure is the state itself, the actual thing the machine runs on. It is no metaphor laid over some realer state underneath. Category theory was the first language I found for that structure. It is not what the model is. The basic model asks for states, instructions, a step, prices and a reading. The ledger and the window are further structure used in the constructions. None of that needs category theory. The morphisms in that first engine, the night they first composed correctly at two in the morning, were objects that remembered where they came from; an established record remembers where it came from too, and you can walk up to one and challenge it. The receipt grew teeth. But the shape itself never moved. The shape was the substrate the entire time, and everything else, every renderer and every proof, was me slowly learning to see what I had already built. By the shape I mean structure held as state. Not category theory. Category theory was just the first language I had for it.

Every step pulled back to show another layer underneath that I had not known was there, and I mean that plainly: I did not know what a Turing machine was when I started, I did not know most of the words in this book existed. I learned each one the same way, by walking face-first into a wall that needed it and not being let past until I had it. The needing always came first. The learning came after, dragged along behind the needing because it had no say in the matter.

Large language models were my hands the whole way through, and I want the division of labor exact, because it is the thing people most want to get wrong about work like this. The hands had nothing like this to draw on: no reference implementation, no prior project to point at. The ideas, the architecture, the direction, the demand that the thing be true: those are mine, all of them, no asterisk. The symbol-pushing, the code and the formal derivations, the models produced. So I read every statement. A derivation can settle an empty question, and I had to refuse that too. However the symbols got onto the page, the argument on it is checkable by reading, and it would be exactly as right or as wrong if a million monkeys had typed it. The monkeys could not have refused.

A word on what I actually do, because it runs under everything after it. I do not write code or math by hand. I do not pick constants. I do not grind through derivations. I have never written a line of code on paper or in a notepad in my life. What I do is see, and direct, and refuse. When I take hold of an idea it does not stay a point. It opens up. It becomes a shape I can blow up and turn over and take apart, lay across another shape to find where the two of them touch, run forward and then back to where it started, zoom into until it is the only thing in the world and pull back until it is one dot in a field of other dots, and the whole time it stays wired to everything I have ever connected it to, the right connections and the wrong ones both, all of them live at once. I am not reading that field neutrally, the way a search would. The thing doing the looking is me, the whole of me, my gut and my mood and my stubbornness picking what lights up and which way I move through it, and that is the part you cannot lift out without turning me back into a search engine. So I see where a thing has to fit, the shape it is obligated to have, usually well before I could write it down in a single symbol. Then the model gives me the symbols, and I refuse anything whose argument does not hold or does not match the shape I am holding. That refusal is the one thing in this entire book that is mine and only mine: the demand that it be true, and the throwing-out of everything that wasn't.

I am telling you all of this because it's the engine. I did not come up through anywhere that hands you a foundation and says trust us, this part is settled. No professors, no colleagues, no field full of authority to lean back on when a claim got heavy. From the outside that is a deficit. From the inside it is the whole reason any of this is solid: when I meet a claim I either find the proof or I do not believe it, and that goes double for my own, because my own are the ones I most want to wave through. It is how I am built. It is the only way I know to hold a thing and actually trust that I am holding it.

So everything in here gets built from the ground up. It isn't a courtesy to you. I build it because I do not believe I understand a thing until I have built it with my own hands, watched it stand up on its own, and tried to knock it over. I built this book the exact way I built the machine, brick by brick, refusing each brick until it held weight. That includes the parts you already knew cold before you opened this. I did not skip them for you, and I could not skip them for me.

The vocabulary I had to go look up to do any of this sits at the back of the book, so the argument comes first. If a word stops you, it is there. If you already know this material, you never have to open it. But if you catch something in the basics that is wrong, I want to know, and I mean that as a real ask. It isn't politeness. Nothing in here is correct just because I wrote it. Especially the parts I wrote.

\subsection*{On the name}

I wanted to call it the meta machine. Then, in October 2021, Facebook renamed its company Meta: a company taking a term for self-reference as its name and draining the word of meaning in the same motion. So I went one level up, where this machine already lives. The most meta move available, for a machine that runs on meta-level reasoning, was to put my own name on the cover and take the word back. That's the whole of it, a meta move on a meta machine. It happens to be my dad's name too, and my wife's now. So I asked them before I did it. They said go ahead.

\end{document}
